FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 THURSDAY, APRIL 21, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met in executive session at 10:05 a.m., in room 2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

Chairman MORGAN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in executive session for a continuation of the hearings on the foreign aid bills, H.R. 12449 and 

H.R. 12450.

Our witness this morning is the Honorable Joseph J. Sisco, Assistant Secretary for International Organization Affairs, Department of State.

Mr. Sisco, you have a prepared statement and you may proceed, sir. 

STATEMENT OF HON. JOSEPH J. SISCO, ASSISTANT SECRETARY FOR INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION AFFAIRS, DEPARTMENT OF STATE 

Mr. SISCO. I thought what I would do was make a brief oral statement and with your permission submit a longer statement for the record. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The prepared statement of Mr. Sisco is as follows:) STATEMENT OF HON. JOSEPH J. SISCO, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION AFFAIRS, BEFORE THE HOUSE FOREIGN AFFAIRS COMMITTEE 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I appreciate the opportunity of appearing before you today in support of the President's recommendations for voluntary contributions to international organizations and programs. The President has proposed authorization of the appropriation of such amounts as may be necessary for such contributions over the next 5 years. For fiscal year 1967, he has recommended new obligational authority in the amount of $140.5 million which, together with a carryover of $4.1 million, will make $144.6 million available for commitment. 

As Assistant Secretary of State for International Organization Affairs, I am responsibile for U.S. contributions to virtually all the international bodies to which the United States belongs. Those contributions to international organizations which are in the nature of dues are presented annually as part of the State Department's regular budget. The voluntary contributions, which are authorized by the Foreign Assistance Act, are also administered by my bureau by delegation from the Administrator of the Agency for International Development. 

In a recent memorandum to the Secretary of State, President Johnson has directed that we apply to international agencies the same rigorous standards of program performance and budget review as we do to our Federal programs. 571

572 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

"Our purpose," he stated, "must be to see that- "Future expansion of the activities of the international organizations is governed by the tests of feasibility and reasonableness. "The programs of the organizations are vigorously scrutinized so that funds are allocated only to high priority projects which we are convinced are in the interests of the international community and of our own country; and "Each international agency operates with a maximum of effectiveness and economy."

The President also called upon the heads of the various Federal departments and agencies involved in participation in international programs to give their full cooperation to the Department of State in carrying out his instructions. 

In accordance with the President's directive, we are intensifying efforts to insure that the multilateral activities in which we join are carried out efficiently and economically.

Of the $144.6 million proposed for commitment in fiscal year 1967, about 85 percent will be used in multilateral programs which will help give the people of the less-developed world the knowledge and skills, the food and the strength, to move toward self-sufficiency. With a few exceptions, we are asking about the same amount of money as last year.

By action of the U.N. General Assembly last fall the expanded program of technical assistance, which was initiated in 1950, and the Special Fund, which got underway in 1959, were combined into the U.N. development program with a single top management and a single governing council. The United States was in the forefront in initiating this merger, which will produce greater efficiency in the planning and execution of this major U.N. development assistance activity. Under the technical assistance arm of the U.N. development program, know-how is transferred to the less-developed countries through the provision of experts and the granting of fellowships for training abroad. The Special Fund sector concentrates on surveys of natural resources, economic feasibility studies, establishment of training institutions, and the creation of research institutes. 

The Special Fund is serving an important function through its preinvestment activities. According to data released by Mr. Paul Hoffman, formerly executive head of the Special Fund and now Administrator of the combined program, 22 completed Special Fund projects in the preinvestment field developed the necessary plans and data to attract capital totaling more than $1 billion. The cost to the U.N. for these projects was about $28 million. 

In 1960, the U.N. General Assembly set an annual target of $150 million in contributions to the U.N. development program. This target was exceeded for the first time in 1965 and last fall the General Assembly increased the annual target to $200 million. For 1966, a total of $105.4 million has already been pledged by 

110 countries other than the United States while the United States pledged $65 million. In light of the demonstrated willingness of other countries to increase their support of the program and on the condition that our contribution will not exceed 40 percent of total governmental contributions, it is proposed that the U.S. contribution in 1967 be raised to $70 million. For every $4 that we contribute, the other governments will put up $6. 

Widely known throughout the less-developed world for its many activities benefiting children and mothers, the U.N. Children's Fund received additional recognition last year when it was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize. The United 

States should take pride in the fact that Mr. Henry Labouisse was chosen to succeed Mr. Maurice Pate, another American who had served as the Executive 

Director of UNICEF since its inception at the end of World War II. 

In cooperation with other agencies in the U.N. family, UNICEF continues to carry out useful work in the field of disease control, health services, and nutrition. In recent years it has given increased emphasis to education and vocational training. The United States continues to be prepared to match other governmental contributions at the 40-percent ratio. We propose a contribution of $12 million to UNICEF's program in 1967-the same amount as last year. 

Following an experimental 3-year period, the world food program, which is jointly supervised by the United Nations and the Food and Agriculture Organization, is now embarked on a broader continuing program for the utilization of foodstuffs on a multilateral basis to promote economic and social development. 

At a pledging session held in January this year, Secretary Freeman pledged up to $92 million in agricultural commodities, shipping services to move the com-

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

573 modities to their destinations, and up to $6 million in cash; the pledge was made subject to congressional action. The commodity pledge includes a 50-percent matching provision and the cash pledge a limitation of 40 percent. We are requesting $2 million in this bill as our fiscal year 1967 cash contribution, the same as last year. Cash contributions are used primarily for administrative costs. 

Through the international recruitment and placement of personnel with the necessary skills and language competence, the U.N. technical and operational assistance program for the Congo (Leopoldville) helps meet that country's continuing need for technical advisers and operational experts. In fiscal year 1967 it is proposed to contribute $5 million to the U.N., the same amount as last year, for financing of positions which we consider of key importance in the fields of public finance, economic analysis and coordination, civil aviation and meteorology, puble works, telecommunications, police training, and natural resources planning. We hope it will be possible to phase out of this modest program over the next several years as the Congolese develop their own capacities to take over these functions.

The major purpose of the operational program of the International Atomic Energy Agency is to help the less-developed countries share in the peaceful uses of the broad spectrum of benefits afforded by atomic science. 

The program operates through provision of fellowships for study abroad and of experts and equipment for training in atomic science in the developing countries themselves. 

Through this program the Agency also builds experience which will be useful when, as we hope, it will play a larger role in assuring proper safeguards of atomic energy installations throughout the world.

The executive branch proposes to pledge up to $600,000, with a limitation of 37.5 percent of total cash contributions, to the Agency's program for 1967-a reduction from previous years' shares. In addition, it is proposed to make available fellowships for training in the United States the services of U.S. experts, and small equipment grants with maximum value of $400,000. Thus, a total of $1 million, the same as previous years, is proposed for fiscal year 1967. 

Through the exchange of information and the provision of technical assistance, the International Secretariat for Volunteer Service encourages the establishment of volunteer service programs comparable to the U.S. Peace Corps for service abroad and to VISTA for domestic service. Since 1963 ISVS has helped 

24 countries to set up such programs. Last year about 7,000 volunteers were enrolled in these programs. A further contribution to the ISVS budget for 

1967 is proposed at a level of $65,000, subject to the conditions that the U.S. share does not exceed 28 percent and that our contribution will be used only to finance the costs of informational clearinghouse functions. 

The World Bank's present estimate of the fiscal year 1967 installment due on our commitment to the Indus Basin Development Fund is $33 million. In spite of temporary delays resulting from the outbreak of hostilities between India and Pakistan last September, construction is generally on schedule and was 65 percent complete as of the end of 1965. Mr. Richard Palmer, officer-incharge, Pakistan Affairs, AID, is here to provide additional information on the 

Indus Basin project.

In his message to the Congress on foreign aid, the President identified "the incessant cycle of hunger, ignorance, and disease" as "the common blight of the developing world" and said that this vicious pattern can be broken and must be broken if democracy is to survive. The voluntary contributions to international programs which are appropriated as part of our foreign aid serve in important ways to attack the central problems of the developing countries. 

During the present year we estimate that $23.6 million of our voluntary contributions to such programs as the U.N. Children's Fund, the U.N. development program, and UNRWA are being used for health improvement activities. 

Through the U.N. development program and the world food program about $24 million of our contributions are being utilized for improvement of the agricultural output of the less-developed countries, and approximately $21 million is being devoted to the improvement of educational systems through the U.N. development program. 

II 

The second major category of programs proposed for U.S. support in fiscal year 

1967 are those designed to maintain peace and promote stability in the eastern 

Mediterranean area.

574 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

The U.N. Relief and Works Agency provides basic health and education services for the more than a million Palestine refugees on its rolls. It also provides a minimum food ration for 873,000 of the refugees. While some of the refugees, utilizing skills obtained through the UNRWA educational program, are able to find employment and become at least partially self-supporting, the basic problem remains unsolved because the parties concerned have been unable to agree on a program to implement the U.N. resolution of 1948 which states the refugees have a right to repatriation or compensation for lost properties. 

With a view to making the most efficient use of available resources the U.N. 

General Assembly in the fall of 1965 directed UNRWA to "take such measures, including the rectification of the relief rolls *** to assure ** * the most equitable distribution of relief based on need * **" The Commissioner-General is now seeking ways to implement this directive through cooperation with the Arab host governments. Although much remains to be done in rectification, the 

Commissioner-General made limited progress on this problem last year. Moreover, he has been urgently requested by the United States to assure that members of the Palestine Liberation Army are removed from UNRWA's rolls. 

The U.S. contribution to UNRWA for fiscal year 1965 was $24.7 million. For fiscal year 1966 the executive branch proposed a reduction of $1 million in agricultural commodities. In addition, Congress reduced the cash portion by $800,000. Our total pledge for fiscal year 1966 therefore was $22.9 million. 

During the past winter, UNRWA indicated that it could utilize additional agricultural commodities, and the United States agreed to furnish them on the understanding that there would be a commensurate reduction in our cash contribution. Our contribution for fiscal year 1966 is therefore composed of $14 million in cash and $8.9 million in foodstuffs. 

In view of the continuing natural increase of the refugee population and of the rising prices of goods and services purchased by UNRWA, the executive branch recommends a contribution for fiscal year 1967 at the same level as in fiscal year 1966. The Commissioner General is actively seeking additional contributions from other governments, and several European countries have indicated a willingness to increase their contributions over previous years' levels. 

The United States has supported the Commissioner General's efforts, and it is expected that the U.S. share of total governmental contributions will gradually diminish.

The United States also helps to maintain order in the Middle East through contributions to the costs of the U.N. Emergency Force, which since 1956 has been stationed on the armistice line and international frontier between Israel and the United Arab Republic. As the result of a U.S. initiative the size and costs of the force were restudied in late 1965, and on the basis of that report, the U.N. 

General Assembly reduced the 1966 appropriation by almost $4 million to a level of $15 million.

The UNEF financing resolution adopted by the General Assembly for both 1965 and 1966 apportioned 5 percent of the total cost to the developing countries on the basis of the U.N. regular scale of assessments; the balance was apportioned to 26 developed countries on the basis of the regular U.N. scale of assessments adjusted to take into account reductions accorded to the developing countries. Thus, as in past years, proportionately higher amounts are allocated to the richer countries than the poorer countries. The resolution apportions an additional amount to the 26 developed member states equal to 25 percent of its base apportionment "in order to meet reserve requirements." It is understood that this 25 percent will be used to cover the shortfall expected to result from the failure of certain members to pay their UNEF apportionments. 

The U.S. share of the costs of UNEF for calendar year 1966, as apportioned by the General Assembly, is $6,838,000. This amount is requested as part of the fiscal year 1967 estimate for voluntary contributions to international organizations.

III 

Funds available for voluntary contributions are being used this year to meet the U.S. share of the costs of the U.N. force in Cyprus (UNFICYP). The financing of UNFICYP has been on a purely voluntary basis, with 41 countries pledging support. Since the force was inaugurated in March 1964, the United 

States has pledged approximately 40 percent of the total costs. When the Pres-

ident's fiscal year 1967 budget was prepared some months ago, our hope was that by the beginning of fiscal year 1967 progress would have been made on a political solution and the force might be phased out. Regrettably, we cannot report such progress. If the force is continued into fiscal year 1967, as now appears likely, funds available from the voluntary contributions appropriation or from the contingency fund will be used.

Finally, we are proposing further contributions to two relatively new members of the U.N. family; $150,000 is proposed as a second contribution to the 

International Agency for Research on Cancer, which is being established at Lyon, France, under the auspices of the World Health Organization. In addition to the 

United States, six other nations are supporting an international attack on cancer, at this point primarily through research on the epidemiology of the disease. 

Each of the members have agreed to contribute $150,000 annually for 5 years. 

A second contribution is also proposed to the U.N. Institute for Training and 

Research, a new organ of the U.N. designed to train international civil servants as well as diplomats of the developing countries, and to conduct research on important problems of peace and progress; $400,000 is requested for contribution to the regular budget of the Institute, with a limitation of 40 percent on our contribution. In addition, $100,000 is requested to finance Adlai E. Stevenson fellowships administered by the Institute.

IV 

U.S. participation in the international programs which I have described serves important national interests. Through our contributions, the United States- 

Supports operations designed to maintain peace and order in troubled areas.

Extends the benefits of knowledge, skills, and technology to the less developed countries.

Encourages other governments to share in the burdens of peacekeeping and economic development.

I urge you to approve the recommendations on voluntary contributions to international organizations and programs which are before you. 

Mr. Sisco. Mr. Chairman, I appreciate the opportunity to appear in support of the President's recommendations for voluntary contributions to international organizations. I would like to note at the outset that while I have appeared before this committee on previous accasions, this is the first time that I will have appeared before the full committee as Assistant Secretary of State.

I just want to make one comment regarding my basic approach to my job and to the question of U.S. policy in the U.N. This is an organization that I have dealt with personally in the last 15 years. It is one which in my judgment was oversold in 1945 at the time of the 

San Francisco Conference.

We as Americans have tended to view this organization pretty much as a panacea and as a cure-all. I think in the last 20 years we have learned that this is an organization with both capacities and limitations, and that the basic yardstick that has to be applied by any policymaker is, How does this organization serve the national interests of the United States? That is basically my approach to the whole question of the U.N. and U.S. policy toward it. 

We are recommending, as indicated on page 57 of the presentation book, new obligational authority for $140.5 million with a carryover of $4.1 million from last year, bringing the total to $144 million plus. 

I would like to make a few comments regarding the principal pro grams within the framework of chapter III. 

First, we have the United Nations Development Program. For 1967 

we are requesting authorization for $70 million, which represents an Lu

increase of $5 million from last year. As the committee is aware, last fall the General Assembly merged two very important programs, namely, the expanded Program of Technical Assistance and the Special Fund into one program, which is now called the U.N. Development Program. 

It is under single management, under a single governing council and it is headed by an American, Mr. Paul Hoffman. The reason we were in the forefront in merging these two organizations is that we felt it would result in an organization of greater efficiency, better both in planning and in execution.

I might also explain that another reason why we supported this merger is that more and more we want the various specialized agencies of the United Nations to go to the central fund; namely the U.N. 

Development Program, for the costs of a number of the programs of assistance to the developing countries. 

[Security deletion.] The principal development regarding the U.N. Development Pro gram over the past year is that the General Assembly this last fall established a new target of $200 million. The original target of $150 million was established by the General Assembly in 1960. 

The interesting thing is that this is a real success story in terms of the number of countries that have this year decided to make specific contributions in support of the U.N. Development Program. Already this year $105 million has been pledged by over 110 countries other than the United States. 

Our pledge, as you know, does not exceed 40 percent. Recently Mr.

Hoffman put out an overall review of the program. I think the most significant point in this overall review is that 22 completed projects in the preinvestment field have helped to develop plans that attracted something like $1 billion worth of capital investment. The cost of these projects to the U.N. itself was only $28 million. We e support this program because we feel that, like a number of the other U.N. programs in the economic and social field, it is really trying to attack the root causes of war, it is trying to help create conditions of stability and by providing skills and know-how, helping a number of these developing countries to stand on their own two feet. 

It serves our interest in that we want these countries to be able to stand on their own feet, free of interference by others. 

Another program that I would like to say a word about is UNICEF. 

We are requesting the same amount of money as in the previous year, $12 million. I would only say that this program has been so universally recognized as a successful one that it was awarded the Nobel 

Peace Prize last year. 

There are a number of other programs that are listed, about which I will make no comment, Mr. Chairman. I am submitting a fuller statement for the record.

I do want to say just a word regarding the second major category which is dealt with; namely, the question of peacekeeping and the maintenance of peace. We are asking this year for the same amount as last year for the United Nations Relief and Works Agency. As this committee knows, this is an agency that has been in existence for a good

many years. It is providing health and educational services for over a million refugees, as well as providing rations for something like 875,000.

We have sought during the past year, very much in the spirit of the views expressed in this committee in previous years, to see whether we could make some progress on the problem of rectification; namely, the elimination of ineligible persons on the relief rolls. Progress in the past has been very modest indeed. Since 1950, there were 320,818 net deletions from the ration rolls, offset by additions of 237,484 children for a net reduction in the total number of refugees on the ration rolls of 83,334. In the past two and a half years the net reduction of the ration rolls was 8,951.

As is indicated on page 57, Mr. Chairman, we are asking this year for authorization of $6.8 million to pay for the costs of the United 

Nations Emergency Force in the Middle East. This $6.8 million request is based on a resolution that was adopted by the General Assembly last fall which is designed to apportion the expenses of the United Nations Emergency Force among the maximum number of members of the United Nations.

The formula that was adopted was simply this: About 5 percent of the total cost of the budget is apportioned to the developing countries and the rest of it is apportioned among 26 developed countries, including the United States, on the basis of the regular United Nations scale adjusted to take into account the reductions accorded to developing countries.

This resolution also apportions an additional amount, 25 percent of the base apportionment, in order to meet reserve requirements and take care of any shortfall that might occur in payment for the force. This $6.8 million which is being requested for 1967 is the same amount which the United States will have paid for 1966. 

I would draw the attention of the committee to the fact that no money is requested to pay for the costs of the United Nations Force in Cyprus. Actually the reason for this is that when these figures were put together some months ago, it was our hope that the United Nations. 

Force could have been phased out beginning this coming fiscal year. 

Unfortunately there hasn't been any appreciable progress on a polit ical solution [security deletion]. 

I want to say a brief word regarding the state of affairs on peacekeeping at the United Nations at the present time. First of all as the committee is aware, there have been very fundamental differences regarding how future peacekeeping operations should be financed, and how they should be authorized. Basically the differences between ourselves and the Soviet Union are political and they reflect a difference of view as to what kind of a role the U.N. should play in the peacekeeping field.

The objective of the Soviet Union continues to be to reduce the U.N. to a debating society, whereas our whole approach has been, and continues to be, that we want a U.N. with an executive capacity to act, one that can help maintain the peace, because in the last 20 years this organization, particularly in the peacekeeping field, has served in the first instance the national interest of the United States.

There has been no fundamental agreement regarding either the financing or the authorizing of future peacekeeping operations. Even though two committees have been dealing with this matter in the U.N. over the past weeks, and will be doing so over the coming months, there remain the same differences which resulted in the article 19 issue in which neither the French nor the Soviets have been willing to pay for their part of the expenses for the United Nations Emergency Force in the Middle East and for the United Nations operation in the Congo. These basic differences continue and what it amounts to is that we are unwilling to agree with the Soviet contention that all future peacekeeping operations, both as to authorization as well as financing, should be subjected to the Soviet veto. 

We feel that the residual power of the General Assembly should be maintained and we cannot agree with the view that the Soviets continue to press, namely that no peacekeeping operation can be undertaken by the U.N. unless it has the full concurrence of the Soviet Union. 

We will continue, Mr. Chairman, to press for the broadest possible sharing of the burden as far as future peacekeeping operations are concerned, but it is clear as a result of the regrettable unwillingness of the General Assembly to apply the loss of vote sanction to either the French or Russians a year ago, that it is pretty generally recognized now at the U.N. that when a major power has a fundamental political objection to a peacekeeping operation, there is no way in which the 

General Assembly can enforce the loss of vote sanction.

This means that it is not enforceable against the Soviet Union, nor would it, of course, be enforceable against us. Insofar as the future is concerned, as one looks ahead regarding the U.N. peacekeeping role, the situation is serious, but not fatal. What it really means is that those like-minded states who support a peacekeeping operation politically will in the future have to band together informally and support. politically desirable peacekeeping operations with the kind of resources that are needed.

I don't think it would serve the national interest of the United States to permit a few of the recalcitrant states to prevent the United Nations from taking any peacekeeping action in the future. We cannot therefore agree to the Soviet contention that all peacekeeping operations come within the exclusive purview of the Security Council itself. 

That is about all I wanted to say, Mr. Chairman. I am ready to answer any questions that I can.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Sisco. 

Mr. Sisco, on page 10 in your prepared statement, you refer to a new organization under the U.N. umbrella, called the International 

Agency for Research on Cancer. You say six nations contribute $150,000. What other five nations are making contributions?

Mr. SISCO. France is the host country. In addition to the United 

States, the United Kingdom, Italy, Ireland, Republic of Germany,

Australia, and the Soviet Union have joined. 

Chairman MORGAN. Why did they pick the location at Lyons, France?

Mr. SISCO. The reason is that the creation of this Agency is in large measure an initiative on the part of the French Government itself.

The French came up with the idea of an international agency for research on cancer. They have a very strong interest in this area of research.

This idea was more or less put together by the French and they pressed for the location of this research center in Lyons, France, and there was general consensus it should be placed there. 

Chairman MORGAN. On page 59 of the presentation book it shows the United States is committed to make available $416,800,000 for the completion of the Indus Basin project. How much of this has already been completed?

Mr. SISCO. I think if you will take a look at a subsequent paragraph on the same page, Mr. Chairman, that the foreign exchange commitments to the Indus fund total $947.9 million, including $173.8 million from India and $774.1 million from members of the so-called Indus fund-I have here Mr. Palmer who deals with this on a day-to-day basis. He may want to add something, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. How much remains to be financed in the future?

STATEMENT OF RICHARD B. PALMER, OFFICER IN CHARGE, PAKISTAN AFFAIRS, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOP- MENT Mr. PALMER. Of the U.S. foreign exchange commitment of $416 

million, we have contributed so far just about half, $208 million. 

Chairman MORGAN. There will be about $208 million more to be given? 

Mr. PALMER. Yes.

Chairman MORGAN. Is this program moving ahead or did the disturbance last fall bring everything to a standstill?

Mr. Sisco. There was some delay as a result of the disturbance. It is moving along reasonably well. There was some delay as a result of the war in the subcontinent. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Sisco, on page 60 of the book it states the 

United States is planning to make a contribution of $5 million to the United Nations for the Congo. It also says that last year the United 

States contributed $1 million to the U.N. for the Congo on a matching basis.

From now on the presentation books indicates that other contributing governments prefer to handle their assistance directly and bilaterally. On page 192 of the book the United States will provide $20 million of supporting assistance to the Congo and $25 million for other Far East matters.

Since we are maintaining an AID mission in the Congo, why do we channel $5 million of U.S. funds to the U.N. rather than administer them directly?

Mr. Sisco. I think the explanation in part is this, Mr. Chairman: 

The U.N. program is there on the basis of a very substantial United 

Nations presence, beginning with the United Nations force in 1960. 

Then the U.N. had both a military as well as an economic program in the Congo.

One reason why the U.N. economic program continues is that there is a good deal of technical know-how that the U.N. can provide which is pretty difficult for others to provide. Another reason why we are anxious and quite glad that a number of these countries are doing these things bilaterally is illustrated by the fact that the Belgians today are doing a major job in the educational field. We have been very anxious for the Belgians to assist the Congo in as substantial a degree as possible. As far as we are concerned, there are areas in which the U.N. has a particular technical know-how that is very helpful to the Congo. I want to add, Mr. Chairman, that our plans are to phase out this now very modest program, hopefully over the next several years.

It is a diminishing program as far as we are concerned. What is happening is that the slack is being picked up bilaterally and it is being done largely in terms of Belgian manpower, Belgian teachers, and so on.

Mr. Cashin who deals with the program, may want to add something.

STATEMENT OF RICHARD M. CASHIN, DIRECTOR, OFFICE OF CENTRAL AFRICAN AFFAIRS, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. CASHIN. I think, Mr. Chairman, the main reason that we have. continued to provide assistance to the Congo through the United Nations is our own difficulty in finding substantial numbers of Americans who speak French and are willing to serve in the Congo. Therefore we feel that the United Nations can provide the technical and operating manpower that are urgently needed in the Congo in a way that we couldn't do ourselves. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Sisco, I remember very well 2 or 3 years ago we received testimony from-I don't remember whether it was from Mr. Bell or not-to the effect that the Congo was going to be a self-sufficient nation within a reasonable time. Since then there have been internal disturbances there but again you paint an optimistic picture indicating that we will be able to get rid of this program. 

As I remember it, it was pointed out here several years ago that in about 5 years' time the Congo would be able to get along on its own. 

What is the picture there today as far as you are concerned? 

Mr. SISCO. To say that the Congo is going to be on its own in the near future is quite optimistic. I think it would be optimistic as it relates to a good many developing countries. I think that it can be said modestly without overstating the case, Mr. Chairman, that the 

Congo was in a very serious situation in 1960. The Russians were betting on Lumumba, betting on a Russian takeover. Today, the situation in the Congo is reasonably stable, and the Congolese are getting on with the job of beginning to learn to do these things for themselves. 

Quite candidly, I don't think one can point to very many of these cases in developing countries where they are going to be able to stand

on their own two feet, exclusively. I think they are going to need help for a long time. I would also say this about the Congo. I spent several weeks there some time ago. It has a lot more going for it than a good many other developing countries. Some of these developing countries don't have the wherewithal. As you look at the Congo and its resources, it is a question of how they develop their resources, how they harness them and how they really begin to develop the country, provided they can develop the technical know-how and receive the kind of help that they need. 

I am not going to make any optimistic predictions, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Sisco, on page 61 of the book it indicates the United States intends to give $65,000 to support the International Secretariat for Voluntary Services which will be 28 percent of the budget for fiscal year 1967.

It says this money is to be used only to finance international clearinghouse functions. Last year we had the Peace Corps legislation and it showed the salaries of the Peace Corps personnel supplied to the International Secretariat for Voluntary Services amounted to $78,000. 

Does this mean that the U.S. contribution would be $143,000 for the 

International Secretariat for Volunteer Service?

Mr. Sisco. I think I know the answer to that, but I will ask Mr. 

Parrott to come up to the table because he deals with these things on a day-by-day basis. The amount of money we are asking for this year is $65,000 as you indicated, Mr. Chairman, and is really to pay for the costs of this clearinghouse function. My understanding is that in addition there are eight American personnel that we make available to this Secretariat. We pay for their salaries. 

The plans at the moment are to reduce these eight to four. I think it is fair to say that in addition to this $65,000Mrs. BOLTON. Would the gentleman yield? Are those secretaries, or what is their function?

Mr. SISCO. The function is the usual kind of Secretariat function, the preparation of documentation, putting together information and that kind of thing. Mr. Parrott, would you like to add something to that?

STATEMENT OF RAYMOND PARROTT, DIRECTOR, NATIONAL VOLUNTARY SERVICE PROGRAMS, PEACE CORPS 

Mr. PARROTT. Yes. The total operation of the International Secretariat is funded in two ways: through cash contributions—and this is the reason we are here today; and second, through providing personnel that are detailed to the Secretariat by the United States and by other countries on a nonreimbursable basis. 

This last year the United States detailed to the Secretariat eight people, seven from the Peace Corps and one from AID. Four other countries contributed personnel to this operation too. Of the eight people that the United States details, four are what we call professional staff who are knowledgeable in the field of information gather-

ing, the collation of information, and the dissemination of information, and four are in what we call the secretarial-clerical field. Because of the rather large input of U.S. personnel, our policy for next year calendar year 1967-will be to reduce the U.S. personnel detailed to the Secretariat to three professionals and to urge the Council which meets in the United States in May, to take the costs of four secretarial people out of operating funds. 

This is one of the reasons we are asking for a slight increase in our cash contribution. We think that this will result in a more equitable distribution of personnel costs. I think it is the current plan of the 

Secretary General that by 1970 that total personnel costs can come out of the operating budget. At the present time ISVS has been able to get significant contributions of personnel from other countries to help offset these costs.

Chairman MORGAN. Last year with the annual hearings on the 

Peace Corps, in the committee report-we had a paragraph in the committee report and I want to read it. 

The committee is strongly opposed to any encouragement by the United States of the expansion of the International Secretariat for Volunteer Service and recommends that the number of Peace Corps employees assigned to the International Secretariat for Volunteer Service on a nonreimbursable basis in fiscal year 1965-three professional and four secretarial level persons should be the ceiling on Peace Corps financial support to the International Secretariat for 

Volunteer Service in fiscal year 1966.

Do you mean that you are still going to have seven people from the 

Peace Corps, too?

Mr. PARROTT. The personnel arrangement is such that we are within the ceiling that the committee recommended of seven Peace Corps people. What we are saying is that we would like next year to reduce this from seven to three.

The cash contribution as you know comes out of the Foreign Assistance Act. 

Chairman MORGAN. What other contributions are there that are made? What are some of the other nations making contributions?

Mr. PARROTT. The International Secretariat operates on a calendar year basis. Their budget for 1965 calls for contributions from nine other countries. In addition one other country, namely, Sweden, has voluntarily pledged and paid $8,000. That Government does not sit on the Council which would be its privilege. It has elected not to join the Council at this time.

As far as the extent of contributions, for 1965 you will recall that the United States pledged and paid $22,400 out of a total budget of $150,000. The highest cash contributor was Germany, with $42,000. 

Other countries pledged or paid the following amounts: the Netherlands, $13,500; Sweden, $8,000; Denmark, $7,500; Argentina, $5,700; 

Norway, $1,500; Philippines, $4,500; Thailand, $1,000; Israel, $6,000.

For 1966 the way other countries operate is that they are now getting into the sessions in their own parliaments and legislatures in which they will authorize payments for this calendar year 1966. But the schedule as far as we know, is on target. Proposed contributions for

1966 are: the United States, $49,000; Argentina, $15,750; Denmark, $14,000; Germany, $31,500; Israel, $7,000; Norway, $15,000; Philippines, $7,000; Thailand, $7,000; India, $7,000; the Netherlands, $22,000. These are the amounts that they are being asked to contribute toward the approved 1966 budget. We expect to have better information in May at the Council meeting as to the likelihood of meeting these targets. But the International Secretariat is most optimistic.

I have talked to many of the Council members myself. Since this budget for calendar year 1966 of $175,000 was approved by the Council by unanimous vote, it is our feeling that these figures will be met.

(The information follows:) INDIAN SELF-HELP MEASURES 

India's economic achievements since independence have been substantial as a result of India's efforts to help herself. For example, the country is generally recognized as a pioneer of economic planning. It established a comprehensive planning organization and, despite its enormous development needs, determined to generate economic and social growth without resort to authoritarian methods. 

India's determination is reflected in its efforts to mobilize its own resources. 

During the past 15 years, savings have been raised from 5 percent to 10 percent of national income, and investment from 5 percent to almost 14 percent. The 

Government has been courageous in raising taxes, and inflation has been largely contained, except for the most recent period. Furthermore, though U.S. and other free world assistance has provided the critical margin of capital needed by India for her overall economic growth, India now finances about 80 percent of the cost of development activities from its own domestic resources. 

In the critical area of food, the Indian Government has taken several steps to combat the distribution problem, such as developing plans for better transportation facilities, making spot checks on grain storage facilities to uncover hoarded supplies and encouraging farmers and merchants to cooperate in bringing grain to the market. However, the main solution lies in the ability of the 

Indian Government to help themselves, which they are doing with increasing effectiveness.

The Indian Government also realizes that increased agricultural production will little help India's economic progress unless it is able to reduce its birth rate. Prime Minister Gandhi made clear at a National Press Club meeting during her recent visit to the United States that India is undertaking positive action in population control when she said, "We have launched a vast family planning program. The magnitude of our effort will be evident from just two statistics. Eighteen thousand family planning centers are actually operating in the country today. We have also increased the budget for family planning twentyfold." 60-929-66-pt. 4-2

Mrs. BOLTON. Do Russia and France contribute to the Secretariat? 

Mr. Sisco. The Secretariat? I am not aware that they do. You are talking about the International Secretariat for Volunteer Service?

Mrs. BOLTON. Yes, and whatever-is there no other voluntary- 

Mr. Sisco. In terms of the ISVS fund they are not contributors. 

Mrs. BOLTON. They wouldn't be.

Mr. Sisco. The only contributors as indicated earlier 

Mrs. BOLTON. Is Sweden the only one that has paid in on that?

Mr. Sisco. No, there are those that have been named. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Did France have some suggestions last year; budget reforms? What has happened to them?

Mr. SISCO. Yes. Last year at the General Assembly the French felt that it was very important for the U.N. to take a very hard look not only at the question of solvency in the United Nations itself, but likewise at programs and budgets in the entire system of specialized agencies.

As a result of this French initiative, with the very strong support. of the United States, the General Assembly established a Committee of Fourteen experts to look into the question of U.N. solvency as well as these budgets and specialized agency programs. The Committee of 

Fourteen has completed the first half of its work. What it did was to look at the long-range situation as it results from the fact that the Russians and the French continue to be unwilling to pay for their past debts both on UNEF and the United Nations force in the Congo. 

What it came up with was a set of figures that had been submitted by the Secretary General which really clearly indicates that these arrearages are still very much on the books, that what is needed in order to bring the U.N. to solvency in the fullest sense is for these two recalcitrant members in particular to pay what they owe. 

At the same time the Committee of Fourteen looked at this problem in the short range. By the short range, I mean it addressed itself to the question of what is needed over the next year in the form of voluntary contributions, so as to assure that the United Nations can continue to operate with reasonable effectiveness.

It concluded that what would be needed in voluntary contributions was roughly something between $52 and $73 million during the next year. When I say it concluded this, I mean the majority of the members, including ourselves, concluded this, because the French and the Russians did not agree this is what was needed. 

What happened at this committee meeting was that you had a reflection of the fundamental differences between ourselves and the Soviet, which have existed over the past 3 or 4 years. The strategy of the 

Soviet Union and the French members was to try to get this committee to arrive at a judgment that the smallest amount of money would be needed over the next year in order to bring the U.N. to solvency. 

Their strategy was simply this: that if it could get this committee to say "Well, you don't really need a working capital fund, the U.N. really doesn't owe $55 million to the members that have provided services for peacekeeping operations in the past, the U.N. need not pay back credits to the members who have provided services, these wwww

operations in the past were illegal." If you took that line as the 

Russians and the French pressed, then you could come out with a conclusion that all that would be needed to restore the U.N. solvency in the next year would be $25 million, and Russia and France could make symbolic contributions.

The net effect would be that the Committee of Fourteen would write off the debts of the Russians and the French. We didn't fall into that trap. What we said was, and what the majority view of this committee was, that these arrearages are on the books, we are still going to press for collection, but it did not mean that the committee in any way wrote off these debts. In the long range the only way the U.N. is really going to become solvent is for these debts to be paid and for the money to come in on the regular budget. 

So the differences which have been in existence over the last 3 or 4 years were reflected in this committee. What implication is there for the U.N. in terms of future peacekeeping? I think the implication is very clear. The majority of the General Assembly is not going to apply the loss of vote sanction against a major power that has a fundamental political objection to a future peacekeeping operation. 

They are no more going to apply it against us than they would against the Soviet Union.

If this is a reality and a fact of life in the U.N. it is very regrettable that the principle of collective financial responsibility has been undermined, but for those countries such as ourselves who have seen how United Nations peacekeeping operations in the past have served the national interest of the United States, it means that the only effective peacekeeping operations that can be undertaken in the future will be those which have the political support of those who feel that a peacekeeping operation is important and those who support these peacekeeping operations politically will have to support them with substantial resources.

There are all kinds of ways in which future peacekeeping operations can be paid for. As far as we are concerned, we don't want to rest on any one option. Wherever there is a parallelism of interest between ourselves and the Soviet Union, we are going to try to get a future peacekeeping operation paid on the basis of the principle of collective financial responsibility. 

Let me give you an example. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Would you mind. He is pretty bad on us. He doesn't let us have time. I wonder if perhaps you would be good enough to brief it for me for the record. 

Mr. SISCO. Yes. (The information appears on p. 591.) 

Mrs. BOLTON. I would like to ask you what efforts are being made to keep us from contributing to Cuba? 

Mr. Sisco. If I understand your question, Mrs. Bolton, at least that aspect with which I deal, there have been some questions raised with respect to two projects which have been approved by the United Na tions Development Program for Cuba. 

I would like to respond to that very briefly in this way. At this past board meeting in January there was a project that was approved for

the University of Havana, faculty of engineering, to the tune of about $2 million. There were 82 projects approved in January 1966, including a project, for example, for the expanding engineering training at the National Technical Center in South Vietnam for about $1.5 million.

The United Nations Development Program has normally used the ground rule that projects are not approved or rejected for political reasons alone. If you look at the record of the U.N. Special Fund since 1959, you find that a total of 604 projects have been approved in 92 nations. Of this total of 604 projects, only 2 projects have been approved for Cuba, whereas 591 have been approved for free world countries.

We opposed the projects for Cuba, the one approved in 1961 as well as the one that was approved at this recent board meeting. When it came up before the Council in January, Mr. Roosevelt argued that Cuba's shortage of technicians and engineers is due mainly to Cuba's own policies which have caused large numbers of qualified people to leave the country. He said we did not believe that an international agency should be called upon to remedy this shortage. 

The Governing Council does not, as a matter of practice, take votes on individual projects. As a result the project went through and even if there had been a vote, of course we would have been outvoted on this simply because of the rule that projects are neither approved nor rejected on the basis of political reasons. 

Mrs. BOLTON. What would you reply to your constituents on this whole business when they write you in fury that we are doing things for Cuba?

Mr. Sisco. I think there is a very good reply, Mrs. Bolton, that is as you really look at this overall program and you look at 600 projects- 

Mrs. BOLTON. You have to look at the world.

Mr. SISCO. You have to look at the world. Frankly, we did all we could to prevent its adoption as it related to Cuba. If you address it only to Cuba, I think the answer is much more difficult. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Yes, and that is the problem we have.

Mr. SISCO. The U.N. Development Program has issued a paper which states that no U.S. dollars that we contribute will be used on these projects.

The question is asked, are not all contributions to the UNDP merged? 

If so, how can we be sure that no U.S. contributions are being used for Cuban projects?

The answer is this: It is true that 40 percent of the total financial resources of the Special Fund are supplied by the United States. But over 100 other countries also contribute to the Fund. Most of these contributions, making up the remaining 60 percent, are in currencies other than dollars. These contributions are held in separate accounts outside of the United States; they are not integrated with U.S. dollar contributions and their end uses are clearly identifiable. It is these non-dollar currencies which will be used for the Cuban projects. 

Mrs. BOLTON. That is just a manipulation of books, of course. 

Thank you very much. www

Mr. HAYS. Do you have any idea about how much the net worth. of the rest of the world is compared to us? How many times richer all the rest of the world is than we are? Ten, would that be a fair assumption? Or five? 

Mr. Sisco. I don't know the figures. I think you are absolutely right if you take the entire world and compare it to us. I think you can also look at it this way, that namely, we are the richest country in the world.

Mr. HAYS. That has nothing to do with it. If I am going into a venture and they ask me to put up $4 and there are 10 of those and they can outvote me and they put up $6, I am not stupid enough to go into that.

Mr. HAYS. You think the business in Cuba serves the interest of the

Mr. HAYS. Name one that is standing on its own feet, and you can't. 

The only ones that were standing on their own feet were standing on their feet before the U.N. was heard of. I support the U.N. I don't care for this ballyhoo that when stripped down doesn't stand up because it isn't true.

Mr. HAYS. It seems a little bit unreasonable to me. When I travel around I can't ever remember having nine people to back me up. I wonder if that is what they are here for, or whether they don't have anything else to do.

One other question, and then I am through. About this $4 to every $6. Have you made any real effort to get the other nations, who certainly have the ability, to put up more than $6, so we can put up less than $4, or increase the whole thing and get our share down to say a reasonable figure? Maybe 30 percent?

Mr. Sisco. In fact that is exactly what has happened over the last year, Mr. Hays. First of all this original target of this program established back in 1960 was $150 million annually. As a result of the fact that 110 other countries are willing and able to make specific contributions to this program, the annual target by the General Assembly this last fall was increased to $200 million. This year we contributed $65 million, the remainder coming from well over 100 countries. 

I think Mr. Hays, that you could point to a program in the U.N. where the United States is footing a major part of the bill and others aren't doing enough; but this is not one.

Mr. HAYS. What happened to this rule we had, I thought, that we couldn't put in more than 33 percent of anything?

Mr. Sisco. That rule is still maintained insofar as the regular assessed budgets are concerned. Our contribution to the U.N. is 32 percent. It does not exceed that and it can not exceed 33% percent by law. These are voluntary contributions which we have tried to keep around the 40-percent figure.

Mr. HAYS. I don't know if you will get it this year. I am going to offer an amendment to cut it down. I promise you won't get it after www

this election because there won't be the votes here to get it. You better cut your cloth accordingly.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. Mr. Chairman and Mr. Sisco, I would like to go back, if you could turn your memory back, to the point where Mrs. Bolton ran out of time. We were discussing alternatives with respect to peacekeeping operations and you were about to give us an example. 

Do you recall that line of thought? 

Mr. SISCO. Yes. 

Mr. ADAIR. Would you give us that example?

Mr. Sisco. I think I was saying, Mr. Adair, that as far as the 

United States is concerned, as you look ahead to the future peacekeeping operations, we want to retain as many options, as many different ways to finance future peacekeeping operations as possible.

Then I went on to say wherever we can do it on the basis of a broad collective financial responsibility principle, let's do it simply because of the fact this will share the burden in the broadest possible way. 

Then I said let us look at the four Security Council resolutions that were adopted on Kashmir quite recently-a cease-fire, withdrawal of forces. You have had a small United Nations observor group in 

Kashmir. This observer group happens to be one example, as small as it is, which is paid from the regular budget. 

In other words, each one of the countries has a responsibility to pay for it on the basis of a legal obligation and an every-member canvass as such.

Wherever you have got this kind of even a temporary parallelism of interest, and the Russians by the way are paying for this 

Mr. ADAIR. And the French. 

Mr. SISCO. Yes.

Where you have this kind of parallelism, let's do it because it will cost us less and it will get the maximum number of countries to pay for it.

Another example is wherever an interested government that is prepared to support a peacekeeping operation can do it, let them do it. 

The example I have in mind is West New Guinea. In that particular instance the Dutch wanted to release West New Guinea and in that particular instance a thousand Pakistani troops were sent under U.N. auspices to supervise the transition from Dutch rule to essentially Indonesian rule. In that particular case the interested governments paid for that, it didn't cost the U.N. anything. 

Where we can do that, let's do that. 

Mr. ADAIR. Did we pay a part of that New Guinea operation?

Mr. SISCO. Not that I can recall as it relates to the U.N., Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. That was the arrangement worked out by Ambassador Bunker? 

Mr. SISCO. Yes.

There are other options, as you well know. There are ways in which you can get a group of like-minded states who are interested in supporting a given peacekeeping operation. They can make some kind

of apportionment among them. So, again, this is a burden-sharing operation as such. 

Mr. ADAIR. Let me turn and invite your attention to page 74 in the briefing book. As I read the figures last year the appropriation for "International organizations and programs" was $144,755,000. You used $140,725,000. Do you agree with those figures? 

Mr. SISCO. Yes, sir.

Mr. ADAIR. Leaving a carryover of something more than $4 million. 

Mr. Sisco. $4.1 million.

Mr. ADAIR. Generally we always welcome these carryovers and are pleased when there is money returned. My question is, Why is there $4 million here?

Mr. Sisco. The principal reason is that we estimated that a larger amount was needed on the Indus water project than actually what was expended. 

Mr. ADAIR. The $4 million 

Mr. Sisco. The Bank did the estimating. By "we" I mean the 

International Bank, which is the administering authority. 

Mr. ADAIR. As I remember in your statement you said you expected to pay $33 million for that purpose for the coming year. 

Mr. SISCO. For 1967 ; yes, sir.

Mr. ADAIR. Does that include the $4 million, or is that in addition to the $4 million.

Mr. SISCO. The $4.1 million is within the whole chapter III program. 

In other words, the overall figure of $144 million as such. 

We have not specifically applied that $4.1 million carryover to the $33 million requested for the Indus Basin in fiscal year 1967. 

Mr. ADAIR. I would now invite your attention to page 62 where reference is made to the U.N. Institute for Training and Research. 

My question is, Why can't that work be done in existing educational institutions rather than setting up a new institution? 

Mr. Sisco. I am in full agreement with the thrust of your question, Mr. Adair. My answer would be this: Obviously by far the substantial part of the research in this field is being done in the private institutions. And it should be.

If there is a justification for this kind of a training program, it is basically that it is an in-house training program which is much more directly geared to the immediate and direct needs of the organization as such. For example, not only is it going to do research as one component, but there is also a training component. 

Our notion is that if you can train a number of these people through this particular program they will be in a better position to be effective within the secretariat.

Mr. ADAIR. AS I read what is set forth here, they are to be trained for service in the U.N. and trained also for service in their own governments. Do I 

Mr. Sisco. There is a great deal of interchangeability, as you know. 

And particularly I might add representatives from the developing countries. If you get a man from Africa to come in and get 2 or 3 years of experience in the United Nations Secretariat in New York, he ought to be in a position to go home and help run that government.

Mr. ADAIR. You make reference to the fact that there are trustees and executive director appointments. Who are those?

Mr. Sisco. The U.N. has just appointed the Director for Research.

Institution. I will be glad to submit the entire list.

Mr. SISCO. Twenty-one.

Mr. FASCELL. Thank you.

Over the past 4 years the Executive has programed $40 million more for Indus than was actually spent. The surplus for 1966 was over $12 million. Is there anything we can do to get better estimates out of the World Bank?

Mr. Sisco. I think the record is generally as you have indicated, Mr. Congressman. I think if one looks over the last 3 or 4 years, as you say, the estimates in most instances have tended to be larger than what was actually expended. I think this is something that we might very well take up with the Bank. 

There are apparently so many variables and imponderables on this one that the Bank has done what we consider a conscientious job in its estimate, but I think it is worth taking up with them again. 

Mr. FASCELL. One thing I think we ought to spread on the record here, and that is the difference in the U.S. share of UNEF cost for 

1966 and what is estimated for 1967. For 1966, chapter 3 funds are shown as $1.4 million, but the cost was more. 

Would you explain the difference between the 2 years and where the money came from last year, and why we are making a change this year?

Mr. Sisco. This year as indicated, Congressman Fascell, we are asking for $6.8 million. Last year the cost for UNEF not only came out in the form of a voluntary contribution within the framework of chapter III of the AID bill, but we likewise got an amount of money from the regular State Department budget itself. And while the table showing only voluntary contributions from the AID bill would indicate that this is an increase, the fact of the matter is that the amount of the U.S. contribution to the calendar year 1966 costs of UNEF is the same as is required for calendar year 1965 costs. The figures for our contribution to UNEF's costs in 1965 are: $2.3 million coming out of the AID bill and $4.5 million coming out of the regular 

State Department budget, making a total of $6.8 million. Mr. FASCELL. I don't recall- 

Mr. Sisco. The reason why we are asking for $6.8 million in chapter 

III of the AID bill for fiscal year 1967 is that we consider this basically a voluntary contribution. We are asking for no money to contribute to the costs of the United Nations Emergency Force from the regular State Department budget for fiscal year 1967. The $6.8 million is the total contribution that the United States would make in order to pay for the costs of the United Nations Emergency Force during calendar year 1966.

Mr. FASCELL. I want to be sure, Mr. Secretary, that we reconcile the figures with the testimony before the International Organizations Subcommittee. The difference appears to be $850,000. 

Mr. SISCO. Let me check that, if I could. 

Mr. FASCELL. There was nothing programed for Cyprus yet we spent $10 million last year. Your testimony this year indicates that 

Cyrpus will be funded out of the contingency fund, and yet Cyprus is not really a contingency. We know we are going to run out of funds at the end of this quarter to pay the costs of the Cyprus force. We know the problem still exists. We know it has to be funded. Why don't we go ahead and fund it?

Mr. Sisco. As I indicated earlier, Mr. Congresman, when we put together these figures some months ago our hope was that there would be sufficiently political progress toward a solution of the Cyprus problems and that the U.N. force would not be needed beyond July. Our present assessment is that we will need such a force beyond July 1. 

Our hope and expectation is that rather than try to guess at what a specific amount might be for the duration, that we might obtain the necessary funds from the AID contingency fund. 

Mr. FASCELL. How about the Inter-American Peace Force in the 

Dominican Republic? Have we any plans or program expenditure for that in 1967?

Mr. Sisco. I don't have an answer to that question. This [security deletion] figure that we came up with in 1966 is for the headquarters costs. The cost of the contingents are paid by the governments themselves. The estimate relates to the time that these figures were put together and the hope was that we wouldn't need any additional money. 

Whether that is the case or not today I think I would like to submit a later statement for the record on that. 

Mr. FASCELL. Thank you. 

(The information follows:) INTER-AMERICAN PEACE FORCE 

The special provision for the Inter-American Peace Force headquarters made by the Organization of American States extends only through fiscal year 1966, since it has been the hope that the Peace Force could be withdrawn from the Dominican Republic by that time. The actual date of withdrawal, however, will depend upon the circumstances existing in the Dominican Republic and is to be decided by the Organization of American States in consultation with the Dominican Government. It is possible that there will be a small requirement for funds in fiscal year 1967.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Mailliard. 

Mr. MAILLIARD. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I don't know whether before I arrived anybody discussed the

UNRWA item in here. I am still a little disturbed at the way in which this particular problem-rather than going away as it ought to do eventually-is becoming permanent as far as I can see. Not only that but you in your book here say, apparently with some pride, in any event the U.S. contribution will not exceed 70 percent of the proceeds from all governments. I find this an alarming figure. 

Mr. Sisco. I find it very unprideful. This is not something which the United States can take pride in. It is the fact that we have had to face up to the reality and for much too long a time. I would agree with you, Mr. Congressman- 

Mr. MAILLIARD. Why are we the only ones that have to face up to it?

Last year, as I recall, we arbitrarily reduced by some $800 million the authorization request. Has this been any great hardship?

Mr. Sisco. It has been a hardship in this respect, that the Commissioner General has had difficulty in administering the program. But in terms of the rations themselves he has thus far been able to get a sufficient amount to feed those that he feels are in need of them. 

We haven't really made any real progress on the fundamental question. This is the reason why this thing doesn't go away. What is really needed, of course, is a political solution to these basic differences between Israel, on the one hand, and the Arabs, on the other.

As regrettable as this continuing operation may be, I think it is clear that it is a very important element of stability in the Middle East and that if this were to be removed in one fell swoop the only thing that would result, I think, would be a major move toward instability. 

I don't think that would serve our interests in the Middle East. 

We in the executive branch, Mr. Congressman, are in full agreement that we should try to get the host governments to assume a greater amount of responsibility, that if we can in any way reduce this operation we should.

The difficulty is that as you get a number of these refugees removed from the lists, there are children of refugees which have to be fed. 

We have not made, quite candidly, as much progress as we would have liked on this whole question of the rectification of the rolls. 

Mr. MAILLIARD. How do we get into the position of contributing 70 percent of the total?

Mr. Sisco. I think it goes back to 1948 when the State of Israel was created, and when we sought to inject United Nations mechanisms; namely, the United Nations Truce Supervisory Organization as well as UNRWA itself, which feeds the refugees and deals with health and other matters. Therefore since we were so directly involved, we took the initiative at that time really in the establishment of this organization.

Mr. MAILLIARD. I recall in 1963 when I was there at the General 

Assembly we had some hopes of working our way, perhaps not entirely, out of this problem, but at least making some progress. I can't see that we have made any at all.

Mr. SISCO. We have had a reduction of our contribution, Mr. Congressman, from $24.7 million in fiscal year 1965 to $22.9 million in fiscal year 1966. That is not a great deal of progress. It is some. 

The difficulty is that rather than the refugees decreasing, given the fact that there are a large number of families and so on, the pressure is on the increase. So we are not doing any better than holding our own.

I think the only way in which this operation can basically be ended in a way which would serve our own interests in the Middle East, is when and if a solution can be found between Israel on the one hand, and the Arabs on the other. 

Mr. MAILLIARD. I would just like to express the thought myself that events would appear to date that the only way this burden is going to be lessened in any way is that we just stop supporting it quite so fully.

At least I can take some comfort in the fact that in this coming year's proposal there is a greater emphasis on agricultural products rather than cash. 

I would ask one more question: What percentage of the cash contribution are we able to make in otherwise blocked funds?

Mr. SISCO. Out of the $14 million cash contribution this year we are contributing $6 million in Egyptian and Syrian currencies. As you indicated, Mr. Mailliard, we had another favorable development this past year; namely, the Commissioner General indicated to us that UNRWA could use more agricultural products.

We gave them an additional $1.2 million worth of agricultural products on the understanding that this would reduce our cash con-

tribution by $1.2 million. So we gave them an additional $1.2 million in foodstuffs, but within the limits of our authorization and appropriation last year.

Mr. MAILLIARD. Would there be any possibility of getting some other countries, even if they won't contribute substantially out of their own money, to contribute out of some of their money that we owe them?

Mr. Sisco. Some progress has been made in getting other countries to contribute. We have had some indication fairly recently that 

Sweden has now decided to put up $2 million. This has happened in the last couple of weeks.

The Commissioner General has been beating the bushes strongly on this. The needs, as you well know, are very great indeed. He is trying to get others to contribute. The very fact that this has been a long ongoing program makes his job very, very difficult. 

I think it is very regrettable, as you say. The only answer I can see is some kind of political solution.

Mr. MAILLIARD. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Farbstein.

Mr. FRASER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Sisco, if you take the combined gross national products of the member nations of the U.N., do you have any approximation of the proportion that the U.S. gross national product bears to that total? 

Mr. SISCO. I think I will ask Bill Ziehl here to answer that. has been working on the relationship between the gross national product and our contribution. He is a real expert on it. He

3 $ Le

STATEMENT OF WILBUR H. ZIEHL, ADVISER, INTERNATIONAL ORGANIZATION AFFAIRS, U.S. MISSION TO THE UNITED NATIONS 

Mr. ZIEHL. If you take the gross national product as such, our percentage is about 44 percent. Of course, it varies over a period of time. 

Based on net national product and net national income our figure would be depending on all the variables, somewhere between 38½ and 

4012 percent.

Mr. FRASER. Using the gross national product it is around 44 percent? Using these other figures, it is between 38 and 40? 

Mr. ZIEHL. That is right.

Mr. FRASER. The reason I ask that question was it seemed to me when we contribute 40 percent to the U.N. development program, we are contributing roughly in proportion to our productivity in comparison to the productivity of the rest of the membership of the United Nations? 

Mr. SISCO. That is correct. 

Mr. FRASER. What are we doing to encourage the U.N. to acquire greater capabilities in planning and organizing staff, logistics, and the development of a permanent corps for international peacekeeping force?

Mr. Sisco. Mr. Fraser, one of the things that we have felt for a long time is that the staff within the Secretariat itself at the U.N. on the military side should be strengthened. The Secretary General has a limited number of military people available to him to render advice. It is not as strong as we would like. I think the reason for it is basically that, first, any substantial expansion would be costly; secondly, very candidly there has been a reluctance on the part of the U.N. Secretary General to expand it too broadly in view of the fact that there has been this fundamental difference between ourselves and the Soviet Union about what the role of the United Nations ought to be in the peacekeeping field.

That is on the staff side. Secondly, we have done a good deal to try to stimulate a number of other countries to earmark forces that would be available pursuant to a United Nations decision or a recommendation. 

Mr. FRASER. On that point, what specifically have we done? I know

Lester Pearson took some initiative in this field, but what have we done to encourage not only the earmarking, but the specialized training that would be appropriate for earmarking?

Mr. SISCO. On the specialized training in the last 2 or 3 years we have done relatively nothing. The reason for it, Mr. Fraser, is that this is really reflective of the differences of view over peacekeeping and we have not felt that it was politically feasible to press the U.N. to undertake any substantial training program of units. 

For this reason we have really concentrated on two areas; namely, strengthening the staff and encouraging other countries to earmark forces for U.N. peacekeeping operations. The Scandinavians-the

Danes and Swedes and Norwegians-have earmarked a group of over a thousand. We have tried to get countries to earmark these forces within their own armed forces.

Mr. FRASER. The United States has MAAG missions in many countries and military personnel attached to many other places, particularly Latin America. What are we doing to encourage our military personnel and other personnel to encourage earmarking in other nations if they are not moving ahead on their own initiative? 

Mr. Sisco. From time to time we have made diplomatic efforts, but these have been infrequent and low key for this reason. We have felt the best way to stimulate a number of these countries to do this is for such initatives to really come from what might be considered a much more impartial source, namely the U.N. itself. 

From time to time we have talked this matter over with the top officials of the Secretariat with a view to getting them to take steps. I have to say our own efforts, plus those of the U.N. have been circumscribed in this area because of the fundamental difference between the Soviet Union and the United States on peacekeeping operations. 

Mr. FRASER. In Latin America it would seem to me we could bilaterally encourage these countries to earmark units for potential service in the United Nations. I think Brazil had some troops in one of our peacekeeping operations. 

Mr. SISCO. They have troops in UNEF.

Mr. FRASER. One reason I am interested in this is because the inter- 

American force concept is not going anywhere. A new route might be renewed emphasis on U.N. peacekeeping and we might encourage some new thinking about it.

I would conclude by saying that there is quite a disconnection between this kind of thinking and the people in the field who are working with the military assistance programs. I have the impression that they are not very interested in this, and I have the impression that those who are interested in the State Department and in your shop don't have any effective way to communicate with them on this. 

Mr. Sisco. I think it is fair to say that those who are in the field, and I imply no criticism whatsoever, are concentrating on the bilateral relations. It is the responsibility of my own area to worry about the implications on a worldwide basis and to see what can be done to further multilateralize this thing. 

For example, you have, as you have indicated, a multilateral peacekeeping truce in Latin America for the first time in its history. We have wondered if it would be possible to get a number of these countries to make these things dual forces so to speak, earmarked in the first instance for the Inter-American Peace Force but in the second instance for the U.N. itself.

My own feeling is that this is something we may want to take up at the appropriate time. At the moment the Inter-American Force is quite heavily involved in the Dominican Republic and I think it would be premature to get into this at the moment.
