(The information follows:) EFFECTS OF MERGER OF THE EXPANDED PROGRAM FOR TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE AND THE SPECIAL FUND 

The creation of the U.N. development program has resulted in improved efficiency through the merger of the staffs of the expanded program of technical assistance (EPTA) and of the U.N. Special Fund, and the reduction of duplicating or overlapping positions. While previously there had been two governing bodies-the Technical Assistance Committee for EPTA and the Governing Council for the Special Fund-there is now only one governing body composed of representatives of governments, the Governing Council of the U.N. development program, with a consequent reduction in costs of meetings. 

In recent years the volume of resources contributed to programs and supervised by the staff of the U.N. development program has been rising rapidly. For example, projects approved for implementation by the two constituent parts of the development program in 1964 amounted to $132 million. In 1965 the comparable figure was $162 million, an increase of 22 percent. During the early part of 1966 alone, projects costing $154 million were approved. The increased level of program activity is expected to overtake the internal savings resulting from the merger. It has already proved necessary to add more positions as the level of assistance provided by the development program increases. It is, however, clear that increases in administrative costs will be less in the merged program than would have been true if the two parts were separately administered. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Mr. Sisco, I am sorry that I was not here in time to hear your statement. In perusing your written statement I was pleased to see some of the statements you have made with regard to the U.N. development program. You state that for every $4 we contribute to this program other governments put up $6. Is this a matter of policy, or are we eventually going to end up paying the whole thing?

Mr. SISCO. Our contribution can not exceed 40 percent of what the others put up. This is on a matching basis. I pointed out a bit earlier that this program has been so successful that whereas the General Assembly established a target figure of $150 million back in 1961, it has now established a new target of $200 million and this year alone we have got 110 other countries that have already made pledges and contributions toward that particular program. 

One other point which I mentioned earlier which I think is really important, Paul Hoffman looked at 22 projects quite recently and these 22 projects cost the U.N. program, not the American contribution alone, roughly $28 million. As a result of these 22 projects costing $28 million he has been able to stimulate $1 billion worth of capital investment in a number of these countries. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Regardless of the amount that is finally spent on these programs, we will make a contribution of $4 for every $6-

Mr. SISCO. It will not exceed that 40-percent relationship. 

Mr. ROYBAL. With regard to the International Agency for Research on Cancer, I think it is commendable that something is being done finally in this particular field. I am, however, concerned about another problem that weighs most heavily upon the youth of this Nation, and that is the problem of narcotics addiction. I have visited many of the schools in the United States and have been in conference with many school officials, particularly in California, that are very much concerned about this. What is being done about this particular problem since you have stated that we do those things that serve the national interest?

Mr. SISCO. This particular authorization request before you deals with over a dozen so-called voluntary programs. However, there is a U.N. narcotic drugs control section and we do make a contribution to the regular budget of the United Nations which supports it. This narcotics drug control section is really a modest amount involved, $75,000, and it is one of the on-going technical programs that the U.N. is involved in.

It is modest, but it is doing something about it. 

Mr. ROYBAL. What is its main objective? Is it to study the problem and forget it, or to be in a position to control it? 

Mr. SISCO. The Commission on Narcotic Drugs drafts and implements international conventions for the control of the traffic in narcotics.

Mr. ROYBAL. Which means there is a possibility that some time in the future we will be getting less and less narcotics from the Middle 

East, Europe, Asia, and other parts of the world.

Mr. Sisco. That is one of the purposes of this particular body. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Don't you agree that this is one of the biggest problems we have confronting the youth of this country? 

Mr. SISCO. Very much so, and with two children of my own, I can appreciate how as a parent this is really a very major problem. It is one certainly that we in this country as well as other countries have to be very concerned about. 

Mr. ROYBAL. I am glad you made that statement because the problem of narcotics addiction is not the problem of the poor, it is not the problem of minority groups, it is a problem of the American people. 

I think we ought to attack it as such. 

Mr. SISCO. I agree fully, sir.

(The information follows:) BUDGET FOR THE INTERNATIONAL ATOMIC ENERGY AGENCY The budget for the International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) for 1966 

totals $8,744,000. It is supported by assessments on the member states. In addition, there is the operational program, to which members contribute on a voluntary basis. Pledges to the operational program in 1965 amounted to $1,256,970.

Mr. MORSE. I would like to pursue it a little further. How well equipped is IAEA to undertake any extensive inspection actions? 

Mr. Sisco. The IAEA operational program has been concerned with exchange of information, a very substantial fellowship program and that kind of thing. IAEA has only just begun to get into the whole question of safeguards and if we get to the point where we really get a basic agreement between ourselves and the Soviet Union on this, we want the IAEA and we have made this quite clear at Geneva and elsewhere to play a very major role in inspection and in policing the safeguards. This would mean, Mr. Morse, a very substantial increase in the operations of the Agency. 

Mr. MORSE. Could you give me any estimate of how much it might cost for the IAEA to inspect those U.S. nuclear facilities, peaceful nuclear facilities, which are not now under IAEA inspection?

I will have to submit that for the record. 

Mr. MORSE. Two, I believe. 

Mr. SISCO. One or two. 

(The information follows:) IAEA INSPECTION OF U.S. PEACEFUL USES FACILITIES 

The Atomic Energy Commission has made a preliminary estimate of the costs that would be incurred by the International Atomic Energy Agency in applying safeguards to essentially all of the U.S. peaceful nuclear facilities. 

For this estimate it was assumed that Agency safeguards would be applied to power and research reactors and to fuel fabrication facilities, reprocessing facilities, and nuclear research laboratories. The facilities included in this cost estimate are those that will be in operation within approximately 1 year. 

It was also assumed that safeguards would be implemented by Agency inspectors stationed in the United States.

To safeguard these facilities our preliminary estimate indicates that the Agency would require approximately 70 additional inspectors and that the cost to the Agency would be in the neighborhood of $3 million per year. The present staff of the Agency devoted to safeguards operations includes 13 inspectors. 

It should be noted that many administrative and policy matters would require resolution before any decision could be taken to place all U.S. peaceful nuclear facilities under Agency safeguards. These matters have not been considered in preparing the above estimate, nor has the IAEA itself been consulted. 

At present four U.S. facilities are under IAEA inspection. 

Mr. MORSE. At the time of the article 19 dispute, and let me say I agree with you as to the realities of the situation now although I think we were ridiculous in getting ourselves into the position we did, was there any effort made to get from the Soviet Union or French any quid for our quo? Was it ever suggested that they make contributions, equivalent roughly to their deficits in the peacekeeping activities, to some of the independent agencies, such as UNICEF?

Mr. SISCO. Not that specifically, Mr. Morse. The way that was handled at the time of the 19th General Assembly was in this way: 

The whole focus of the preponderant majority was to try to get the General Assembly to resume its normal functions and the Soviets made all sorts of noises in the corridors to the effect that once the General Assembly resumed its normal work, that it, the Soviet Union, would make a voluntary contribution.

We hear this repeated from time to time, but the facts are that they have not made a voluntary contribution. We did not want to get into

the quid pro quo aspect of this for two reasons, really. First of all we felt, and feel, that the law was clear and it was not a question of what we would do in relationship to what they would do because after all for good reasons we have been the major contributor to these peacekeeping operations in the past. We didn't feel that from a negotiating point of view we should get ourselves in a negotiation where we say, "If you contribute the following, we will be willing to do the following." We don't really think there is this X, Y relationship. 

Mr. MORSE. I think we have to pursue it. I disagree with you, Mr.

Sisco. I quite agree in the future, and I believed before the article 

19 dispute, that if there were a peacekeeping operation which the U.N. advocated and supported which did not agree with U.S. interests, that we, the United States, would not and should not support it financially.

It seems to me this has been a predictable thing right along. I would also say that it is time to work out a formula whereby these kinds of situations can be anticipated. I would hope that might be pursued. 

One final question: What is our total expenditure for the United

Nations, including the several programs, voluntary, assessments and peacekeeping and otherwise?

Mr. Sisco. Very loosely it is $100 million on the regular budget and what we are coming up here with today is $144 million. We use the word "U.N." very loosely here. When you really look at these programs that are within the framework of chapter III of the AID bill, some are directly related to the U.N. and some aren't. They are multilateral, maybe that is a better word. It is basically that figureroughly $240 million in total. 

Mr. MORSE. It was certainly the fear on the part of some of the smaller nations of the world that Russia might get out of the United Nations which prompted, it seems to me, the political developments in the article 19 dispute.

Would a similar threat on our part prompt any great interest in the United Nations in lending a hand toward the solution in South Vietnam?

Mr. Sisco. I think a threat on the part of the United States to leave the U.N. would be a very serious step on our part. 

Mr. MORSE. We have a very serious situation going on in southeast Asia.

Mr. SISCO. Very true. I think if you really look at the Soviet view in this regard that I think they, as one looks ahead and looks at for example the whole Communist threat, the Chinese Communist threat, it seems to me that given the difference between the Soviets and the Chinese at the moment, the Soviets themselves for example have every reason in the world to stay within the Organization. 

If you recall a year ago what the Chinese Communists were saying is that they really want the U.N. reorganized. It was the Chinese 

Communists and the Albanians who were really the only two countries that applauded when the Indonesians decided to withdraw from the Organization about a year ago. What I am trying to say is as long as the two major powers, namely ourselves and the Soviet Union, are talking to one another within the framework of an international organization, I think that is a much safer situation from the point of view

of peace than a situation where either one of the major powers is threatening to get out of the one world organization that we have, as imperfect as it is. 

Mr. MORSE. I agree with that. You miss the thrust of my question.

My question is as to whether or not this course of action on our part which stimulates the United Nations to assume what I regard to be its responsibilities in Vietnam- Mr. Sisco. Insofar as Vietnam is concerned, I don't think this kind of threat would have any effect. I would like to address myself to the 

U.N. role in Vietnam if I could. If you go back a year ago the

President made a speech at San Francisco in which he called upon all the members of the United Nations individually and collectively to do what they could to move this problem from the battlefield to the conference table.

Just 2 months ago we convened the Security Council formally. The thing that came out of the Security Council was a majority consensus, not a formal action, summarized by the President of the Security Council that the majority were in favor of a peaceful solution of this problem and in favor of moving it toward negotiations within the framework of the Geneva accords.

The U.N. today, particularly in light of the financial and constitutional difficulties, is not in a position to substitute for American power in Vietnam. It is in a position hopefully, if there ever should be an agreement as we hope there will be to move this thing to the conference table, the U.N. should certainly be in a position where it can help supervise an agreement. 

As you know, Mr. Morse, one of the reasons why we didn't sign the

Geneva accords in 1954 was because of the fact that we were dissatisfied with the machinery that was established by the Geneva agreements. 

History has borne us out on this one because the International Control Commissions which are really organized on the basis of a troika, which gives the Communist member a veto over certain actions of the Commissions, have been largely ineffective from our point of view in both 

Laos and Vietnam.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I should like to join in welcoming Mr. Sisco to the committee. His appearance reminds me of the very busy 3 months I had at the United Nations last fall; I would like to compliment him for the fine job that he is doing in a very difficult and taxing area.

I have a few brief questions, Mr. Chairman. I noticed, Mr. Sisco, that you refer to the memorandum that President Johnson sent to the Secretary of State last month, with respect to applying to the international agencies the same rigorous standards of performance and budget reviews that we do to our Federal programs. You have spelled out what is to be achieved by this scrutiny. 

I wonder how effective such a survey is, and in what ways? How can one member nation do much about concentrating only on highpriority projects if the other members are not motivated the same way. 

Isn't this partly a problem that is beyond the control of the United States? In other words, is this memorandum a reminder to ourselves

that in these various agencies we should be doing more to control the growth and to concentrate on high-priority projects? Or is it an indication that we may even withhold support that we have been providing because there is not a sufficiently businesslike approach in the operation of these agencies?

Mr. SISCO. Mr. Congressman, the purpose of this memorandum is to reflect the President's direct interest in the whole question of programs in the United Nations system as well as the budgets. It is absolutely true as you say that no one country can control the action of these international organizations. We are the major contributor. 

We feel that the more active a role the United States plays in developing the content of these programs as well as achieving reasonable budget levels, rather than excessive growth in the budgets, to that extent we think we are quite frankly not only playing the major role but the major influential role in these organizations. 

What we have done informally, we have put together a so-called Geneva group which is a group of the major contributors of the United Nations system. We meet regularly with these people with a view to trying to get them to act in concert with us in influencing the Directors General not only in the development of what we consider to be reasonable and sensible programs, but likewise to convince the Directors General that we are willing to support reasonable increase, but unwilling to support unreasonable increases.

This is a way, frankly, that we have developed to try to keep the growth pattern within bounds because every one of these organizations has now become much larger with preponderant majorities made up of Africans and Asians. We feel the major contributors must play a decisive and active part in the actual decisionmaking process both as they relate to the program as well as the budgets. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I couldn't agree with you more, Mr. Secretary, about the importance of getting reasonable and sensible programs. As a participant in the budgetary committee I saw at first. hand about the nature of the growth of some of these agencies. However it still strikes me as a problem, even if the major contributors should band together in deciding what they are willing to finance, to get as big a group as is represented on these agencies to agree with what we consider reasonable.

This is basically the contest between the "haves" and the "have nots." 

What you are suggesting is that the haves must be more aggressive in deciding what are the high priority projects, how rapidly we feel an expansion can be made and financed. You suggest the development of a reasonable course of action, in order to persuade the countries which have the votes not to proceed more vigorously than we are willing to support? 

Mr. SISCO. That is right. For example, you have got now a United

Nations Trade and Development Board. If this Board of 55 mem bers is really going to make any basic changes in the trade patterns of the world between the advanced countries on the one hand and the developing countries on the other, it is sheer nonsense for an Afro-Asian majority to adopt a symbolic resolution which the 20 advanced countries who represent 75 or 80 percent of the economic resources of the world are going to completely disregard.

In other words, you are not going to get changes in policy by swirling majorities either in the General Assembly or in some of these other bodies.

Quite frankly we are making it absolutely clear that the way to arrive at a consensus is to have a practical dialog between the advanced countries based on the notion that it really takes two to tango, based on the notion that consensus means practical dialog between the givers and basically the recipients. This is our way, quite frankly, of trying to play a more active and a more decisive role in the decisionmaking process in all of these organizations because we are all in favor of reasonable growth of these organizations because they do serve our national interest.

We are not in favor of steamrolling majorities that kick up budgets in excessive amounts.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Purely as a personal observation, it would seem to me however that some of these international agencies might be utilized by the United States to a greater degree than we are presently utilizing them. In other words, the President is now suggesting on a bilateral basis increased efforts on our part in the field of health, food, and education. Might we not spend that amount of money at least as effectively by increasing our support to the World 

Health Organization or the FAO or some other international or ganization?

In other words, I would buy the argument that a multilateral approach in this area might be at least as good as the bilateral approach which the President is suggesting. I wonder how you feel?

Mr. Sisco. I agree with that fully. I am talking about what we would consider to be reasonable growth in these agencies. First of all the multilateral approach has two very important advantages from our point of view. First of all it helps share the burden in the 

World Health Organization, in the WHO, and other agencies.

Secondly, so many of these agencies are now involved in operational programs where technical assistance is involved. You need this kind of a worldwide pool that a number of these specialized agencies and a number of these technical programs within the framework of the U.N. can provide. No single country, including the United States, could provide this kind of technical know-how. So it has some very great advantages.

We think the multilateral programs help share the burden and what we want to be sure of is that the programs that are developed make sense and have worthwhile objectives, and frankly, we want to be very sure that for every dollar that we put into these organizations we are really getting our dollar's worth both in efficiency and administration.

This is one reason why we are suggesting an increase in the U.N. development program. Here you have a unified management and what we want is the specialized agencies to go to this central coffer, to the central agency for more and more of its money because in this way we are assured of better administration and greater efficiency. 

We know precisely where the money is going and it has the same effect. It has the effect on the specialized agencies of keeping the agency budgets on the administrative side within reasonable bounds. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. You haven't really touched on the question of why we don't utilize, for example. the World Health Organization to

. 

express our increased interest in the health problems of other countries, rather than do it on a bilateral basis. This would seem to indicate that perhaps we don't think the international agencies are doing as good a job as they should be doing.

This would seem to be an unfair reflection on their capacity to discharge these responsibilities. I don't imagine that is really the case. 

Mr. Sisco. With respect to the World Health Organization itself, on which I have been doing a good deal of work, we are in the forefront in having the World Health Organization play a major role in eradicating communicable diseases. We at this last board submitted a fullfledged program for smallpox eradication to the tune of $22 million. 

We succeeded in getting the World Health Organization board to take a decision to put this program on the regular budget which means that every one of the members of well over 100 countries have to make a contribution to this program based on their regular scale of contributions.

This is the kind of thing which we are taking the lead on within the 

World Health Organization. In the World Meteorological Organi zation we are now facing up to the substantial advantages of the world weather watch. Every one of our farmers is interested in this in this country.

Now the WMO is moving the actual operation of this world weather watch. We are in the forefront of multilateralizing this for one reason. There are many countries that have contributions to make in services and in kind and likewise we want the WMO to share in the cost.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. If I could impose on the committee's time for just a few more minutes, Mr. Chairman. If the proposed amendments were not accepted, would there be any effect on the Indus Basin project? (The information follows:) PROPOSED AMENDMENTS RELATING TO CONTRIBUTIONS TO INDUS BASIN DEVELOPMENT FUND 

H.R. 12449, the bill to amend further the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, includes the following provisions:

Section 108(a) amends section 301 (a) of the act to authorize funds appropriated for international organizations and programs to be used for dollar-

. 

repayable loans in connection with the Indus Basin development fund administered by the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (the World Bank). These loans would be subject to the restrictions on development loan terms contained in section 201 (d) of the act. 

Section 301 (a) now authorizes contributions from funds appropriated for international organizations and programs to be made only on a grant basis. 

This authority is now used to make grants for Indus Basin development. 

In 1964 the U.S. Government made a commitment in the form of an executive agreement, subject to appropriation of the necessary funds, to loan to Pakistan $51,220,000 for Indus Basin development, to be administered by the World Bank. 

Although this loan could be funded under the authority of section 201 of the act, by funding it under international organizations and programs, both grants and loans for Indus Basin development would be consolidated in one chapter. 

Section 108 (c) amends section 302 of the act, which relates to the authorization for international organizations and programs, by creating separate subsections for the existing grant authorization and for the new Indus Basin loan authorization.

First, a new subsection (a) substitutes for the obsolete fiscal year 1966 grant authorization a grant authorization of such amounts as may be necessary for each of the 5 fiscal years 1967 through 1971. 

Second, a new subsection (b) adds a new authorization for the appropriation of funds, in addition to other funds available for Indus Basin development, for dollar-repayable loans for the Indus Basin program. The funds are authorized for use beginning in the fiscal year 1968, when it is expected that the World Bank will request that the United States meet its 1964 commitment.

The committee meeets this morning in executive session for a continuation of the hearings on the foreign aid bills, H.R. 12449 and 

H.R. 12450.

Administrator for AID.

STATEMENT OF HON. WILLIAM S. GAUD, DEPUTY ADMINISTRATOR, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. GAUD. Mr. Chairman and gentlemen, I am pleased to appear before the committee today to discuss with you the President's authorization requests for the contingency fund, American-sponsored schools and hospitals abroad, and AID's administrative expenses.

CONTINGENCY FUND 

The contingency fund is maintained to meet urgent assistance needs that are either unforeseen or cannot be meaningfully identified in advance. The fund serves the important purpose of permitting a prompt response to emergency needs.

We use the contingency fund as sparingly as circumstances permit. 

With the exception of the current fiscal year, there has been a considerable reduction in both the amount of the worldwide contingency fund and its use.

The President is requesting an authorization ceeiling of $150 million for the contingency fund for each of the next 5 years, and for fiscal year 1967 an appropriation of $70 million. With estimated recoveries of $5 million, $75 million would be available in fiscal year 1967. This is clearly a minimum request, but we believe it is adequate. If additional requirements should develop, we would request an additional appropriation.

We do not regard fiscal year 1966 demands on our contingency funds as normal. All of the $89 million special contingency fund for 617

southeast Asia has been programed. Similarly, the original worldwide contingency fund for fiscal year 1966 (consisting of $50 million in new appropriations and availabilities of nearly $6 million) was quickly programed. The amount of $37 million was required for use in the Dominican Republic alone to support the efforts of the provisional government to establish and maintain a stable environment prior to the coming elections in June.

These funds provided budgetary support to sustain essential government operations, pay the salaries of government workers, and assist public works activities which helped stem unemployment. The 

U.S. assistance was vital to the effort to avoid chaos in the Dominican 

Republic. 

Another important use of fiscal year 1966 contingency funds was a $7.5 million commodity loan to Ceylon. Prior to the election of a new government in Ceylon and the subsequent settlement of the longstanding oil dispute with American oil companies, Ceylon was not eligible for U.S. assistance. The commodity loan was provided to help relieve Ceylon of its serious shortage of foreign exchange and assist the new government's efforts to achieve economic stabilization. 

Thus far we have used none of the $100 million supplemental contingency funds recently authorized and appropriated by Congress. 

We will use no more of these funds than is absolutely necessary. 

A breakdown of fiscal year 1966 uses of the contingency fund is shown in annex A.

AMERICAN SCHOOLS AND HOSPITALS ABROAD Sections 214 (a) and (b) of the Foreign Assistance Act authorize assistance to certain schools and hospitals abroad which are founded or sponsored by U.S. citizens. The United States has provided assistance under this program since 1959. 

The purpose of this assistance is to help a few selected institutions contribute to the economic and social development of the areas where they are located. As the Foreign Assistance Act states, these institutions serve "as study and demonstration centers for ideas and practices of the United States."

Each of these institutions is accepted by the government where it is located as a valuable contributor to the nation's efforts to develop men and women who will rise to the ranks of political and cultural leadership in their countries. In areas of professional scarcity they are trained to be doctors, nurses, teachers, public administrators, businessmen, agriculturalists, and specialists in other areas of importance to a growing society.

Our support for these institutions must be limited. We can help them to improve and function effectively on a self-supporting basis. 

But we all recognize the need to see these schools and hospitals carry on independently in the coming years.

The President has requested an authorization for these programs for the 5-year period from fiscal year 1967 through fiscal year 1971 of such funds as may be necessary. An appropriation for fiscal year 1967 of $11 million is requested, $4 million more than was appropriated last year. This figure compares with programs of $19.8 million and $17.6 million in fiscal years 1964 and 1965, respectively.

Almost 95 percent of the $11 million is now planned for five institutions: the University of the Americas; the American University of 

Beirut; Robert College, Istanbul; the American University in Cairo; 

and Project HOPE. 

Of the proposed $4 million increase, $2 million would be used to assist the University of the Americas in Mexico City to construct a new campus complex. We expect that private sources will contribute an additional $1.4 million for this project. The amount of $3 million is now planned for the American University of Beirut, an increase of $1 million over assistance provided this year [security deletion]. 

A breakdown of the actual program for fiscal year 1965 and fiscal year 1966 and of the tentative fiscal year 1967 program-is set forth in annex B.

ADMINISTRATIVE EXPENSES 

Funds authorized and appropriated for administrative expenses meet the costs of directing and supervising the AID program in Washington and overseas. This includes establishing program policies and standards, planning, supervising, and reviewing the actual implementation of these policies, evaluating programs and projects, and providing such key management services as accounting, inspection, auditing, personnel administration, management analysis, and administrative support. Because administrative funds cover the costs of directing and supervising the AID program, it is of the highest importance that we have sufficient funds for the effective management of our assistance programs. 

The President has requested an authorization for "Administrative expenses" for the 5-year period from fiscal year 1967 through 1971 of "such funds as may be necessary." This language is consistent with that used in the past for Department of State administrative expenses authorized under section 637 (b).

We believe this type of request to be particularly appropriate for administrative expenses. Numerous factors-over some of which we have no control affect the amount of administrative expenses required in a given year and result in increased costs, the amount of which cannot be accurately predicted in advance.

For example, our fiscal year 1967 appropriation request for administrative expenses is $57,387,000. This, together with $750,000 estimated to be reappropriated from prior year unobligated balances, will make available in fiscal year 1967 a total of $58,137,000. 

This is $1,333,000 more than this year's requirements. Most of this difference is due to increased costs over which the Agency has no control. These include rises in oversea wages and price levels, periodic within-grade pay increases and the Federal pay raise enacted last year. 

A small increase of $350,000 is being requested to support increased initiatives in agriculture, health, and education. However, this increase is partially offset by anticipated economies estimated at $150,000. 

The annual levels of our administrative expenses have been rising for the same reasons that account for the fiscal year 1967 increase inflation abroad, Federal personnel procedures, and Federal pay raises.

We have no reason to believe that they will not continue to rise in the coming years. We therefore consider it essential to request an authorization for the next 5 years for "such funds as may be necessary."

One further point on this subject: Our request for fiscal year 1967 administrative expenses includes funds to support our operations in Vietnam on a level equal to that of our original fiscal year 1966 program. We will manifestly need additional administrative funds for our Vietnamese program in fiscal year 1967. For this reason, the 

President is requesting authority to use for administrative costs, if it becomes necessary to do so, supporting assistance funds available for use in Vietnam. A determination by the President under section 

610 (a) of the Foreign Assistance Act would be required before such a transfer could be made.

The Congress granted similar authority to the President in the fiscal year 1966 Supplemental Authorization Act. 

I will be glad to answer your questions on these or any other matters relating to the AID program. 

Mr. Chairman, I would like to add one thing more to my statement, if I may, before turning to your questions.

I would like to emphasize again for the committee, as Mr. Bell and others have before me this year when testifying here, the importance which we attribute to the administration request for a 5-year authorization for the AID program.

I have just come back a couple of weeks ago from a trip to the Far 

East. I went to Laos, to Thailand, and Vietnam. I talked to as many of our people overseas as I could, individually and in groups, out where we had our AID officials. In talking to them I found that they were interested primarily in two things when we talked about the situation on the Hill:

One was the personnel legislation-the Hays bill-which was passed by the House last year and on which the Senate is now holding hearings.

The other was the request for multiyear authorization. 

Mr. Bell and the other witnesses have testified about why we want the multiyear authorization. I was very much interested in the reasons which these men overseas gave me when I pressed them on it. Their primary feeling was that the AID program has gone on from year to year; each year it goes through a struggle here on the Hill and in the newspapers; each year the AID bill is treated as though it were something that was going to expire at the end of the year. 

We are asked all the time the question of why is it that the AID program has so little acceptance throughout the country and in the public mind. My feeling is that one of the main reasons for that is that we treat it as if it has no acceptance, we look at it each year as if it were something new and debatable.

My own feeling-and this feeling was buttressed and supported by many of these men that I talked to overseas-is that if you are in favor of the AID program, if you believe it is something we should continue, the biggest boost you can give it from a morale standpoint is to put it on a multiyear basis. 

In terms of morale of our employees, in terms of its acceptance, its recognition by the other nations whom we are trying to encourage to give more aid, in terms of our ability to use this to achieve better self-help performance, this single factor-the multiyear authorization-in my judgment will do more than anything else.

As I say, this, plus the Hays bill, which the administration is supporting and hopes to get through the Senate, are the two things that seem to be most on the minds of our people in the countries that I visited. (The charts referred to in Mr. Gaud's remarks are as follows:)

Coordination are paid out of administrative funds and part out of program funds?

STATEMENT OF WILLIAM O. HALL, ASSISTANT ADMINISTRATOR FOR ADMINISTRATION, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVEL- OPMENT 

Mr. HALL. Part of them are paid out of administrative and part out of program. Those directly attributable to program are paid out of the program funds. Most are paid out of administrative funds. 

There is a group that is tied to regional programs. That group is paid out of program funds. The balance are paid out of administrative funds. 

Chairman MORGAN. What would be the percentage, Mr. Hall? 

What percentage would you say are paid out of program funds?

Mr. HALL. I don't have that percentage in my mind, Mr. Chairman. 

Could I supply that for the record or perhaps I can calculate it here. 

(The information follows:) PERSONNEL FUNDING FOR AID'S OFFICE OF PROGRAM COORDINATION 

Our estimates for the Office of Program Coordination include provision for 122 employees performing administrative functions and 10 employees performing program functions. The 10 program funded employees are engaged in analytical research and operational activities to improve the process of development planning in the lesser developed countries at the sector and project level. The resulting percentage relationships would be 92 percent administrative and 8 percent program funded employees.

Chairman MORGAN. Are not most AID personnel paid out of pro gram funds rather than administrative funds?

Mr. HALL. Most of the people in Washington are charged to administrative funds. The people that would not be charged to administrative funds would be people like the people in the International Training Division, some of the people in our supply section, some of the people in our shipping section, who can be directly tied to country programs. They would be program funds. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hall, take the figure of $57,387,000, total request for administrative expenses, and then add what is spent for program funds. What would be your total bill for running this program?

Mr. HALL. Mr. Chairman, I will have to pull that out of the book. 

Chairman MORGAN. You can supply that for the record. (The information is as follows:)

BREAKDOWN OF TOTAL REQUEST BY AID 

The $57,387,000 requested for administrative expenses includes $38,289,300 for salaries. The $2.4 billion in program funds requested includes $68,056,700 for salaries. The total estimates for salaries is $106,346,000. Supporting costs for these personnel are $9,342,600 for administrative expenses and $31,318,400 for program expenses. The program expense figures include direct-hire technicians but do not cover costs incurred by technical assistance contractors. 

Chairman MORGAN. How do you decide which expenses are to be charged to administrative funds and which are charged to program funds?

Mr. HALL. This is a decision that was made some time ago before 

I came with the program, Mr. Chairman. The line that we have tried to follow has been that things which can be tied to a specific project or a specific country program we charge against program funds. 

Things that are of a general character, for example, the Controller's office, all of our senior people, all of our mission directors, deputy mis sion directors, the AID Administrator, the Deputy AID Administra tor, the Assistant Administrators in Washington, people who are overhead are charged against administrative expenses.

Our personnel office is charged against administrative expenses. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Hall, we have always put a limit on administrative funds. Is there any possibility that by using program funds you can get around the limitation that we put on administrative funds? 

Mr. HALL. No, sir; we watch this very carefully. I personally approve any major move from administrative to program. We would also bring it to this committee.

We did bring last year or the year before a shift to correct certain funding situations; we moved some people from administrative to program and from program to administrative. 

That is the last change we have made. It is a close question when you get in the fields as to whether you will charge an automobile, for example, to a project or to administrative expenses. We have our people who approve these items and do watch this closely, and when there is a close question, they bring it to me. It is in final analysis a

subjective decision, but I try to watch this carefully and avoid any unjustified shift from administrative expenses to program expenses. 

Chairman MORGAN. I have read this section on administrative funds in the presentation book. I can't find the total AID payroll anywhere here. I would like for you to furnish that for the record. 

Mr. HALL. I will be glad to do that, Mr. Chairman.

(The information follows:) PRESIDENTIAL DETERMINATION ON TRANSFER OF VIETNAM SUPPORTING ASSISTANCE FUNDS TO ADMINISTRATIVE EXPENSES ACCOUNT 

The proposed amendment to section 402 providing for transfer of Vietnam supporting assistance funds to the administrative expense account would not -exempt such a transfer from the requirements of section 610(a) of the Foreign 

Assistance Act. 

The amendment, since it would be inconsistent with and a later enactment than section 610(b), would exempt such a transfer from the provision of section 610(b) which prohibits transfers to the administrative expense account. 

The amendment is not inconsistent with section 610(a) and thus the transfer provided for in the amendment would be subject to the provision of section 610(a) requiring a Presidential determination that a transfer is necessary for the purposes of the act. 

Chairman MORGAN. I have one more question, Mr. Gaud. 

I see you make your case for a 5-year authorization for administrative funds on the basis of the rising costs, due to inflation, Federal personnel procedures, and changes such as the Federal pay raise. You say:

We have no reason to believe that they will not continue to rise in the coming years. Therefore we consider it essential to request an authorization for the next 5 years for such funds "as may be necessary." 

Chairman MORGAN. You are aware that Congress, including this committee, and especially the Appropriations Committee of the House, frown on such open end authorizations. I suspect that it will take more than the three things mentioned here; inflation abroad, Federal personnel procedures, and Federal pay raises, to change their minds on a 5-year open end authorization. We might be more receptive if we had some figures that we could project, rather than have an open end authorization. 

Mr. GAUD. I can see your worry. I can appreciate the problem. But it is pretty hard to sit here today and calculate with any degree of accuracy what our administrative expenses will be over the next 5 years. That is why it seems to us reasonable to ask you to authorize such funds as may be necessary. -we Let me just add-and this is as obvious to you as it is to mearen't any longer in the position that we were in in 1961 where a new agency was coming to this committee without any experience and without your knowing how it was going to behave. This Agency has been in existence for 5 years. For 5 years we have come up here with a request for administrative expenses which the committee generally has considered to be reasonable. We have actually established a relationship with the committee, we have established a precedent, a practice, and it doesn't seem to me, sir, given the choice that you have here of trying to estimate in detail what our administrative expenses will be 5 years from now and going ahead on this basis, this doesn't, to me, seem to be an unreasonable choice, but the committee has to decide for itself. 

Chairman MORGAN. I can see the problem and your flexibility. I can assure you, however, that when we start the markup I think all open end authorizations will be in difficulty.

Mr. GAUD. May I say a word about some of the other categories, if 

I may:

Development loans, of course, were put on a 5-year basis in 1961. I hope with that precedent the Congress will be somewhat easier on this account.

Chairman MORGAN. With a figure? 

Mr. GAUD. That is right. We are asking for a specific figure on that. The same way on Alliance for Progress. The open end authorizations that we are asking for are relatively few. 

We are asking for an open end authorization of "such funds as may be necessary" for technical cooperation. There again we have been in business for 5 years.

The committee knows that these programs don't fluctuate greatly. 

This is open ended only in the sense that you can't say with precision how much you will need. We don't have here a fund that fluctuates very greatly.

For supporting assistance we recommend a specific figure, with the exception of Vietnam. There, obviously, we are unable to predict any detail what we will need. in For voluntary contributions to international organizations we ask for an open end authorization. Again, we are dealing with a specific series of requirements and there is a good deal of history on those. 

For American schools and hospitals abroad we ask for an open end authorization. This is a relatively small amount. 

As the committee can see, we are doing our best to reduce the number of organizations which get help under that section. We are trying to put the remaining ones on a self-sustaining basis, because sir, as I said last year, we would like to limit and reduce the use of this particular authorization as much as we reasonably can. 

Then we have the survey of investment opportunities, a very small program-for which we are asking an open end authorization. We have the administrative expenses and then we have the southeast Asia program, a new part of the bill, which we hope in future years will be used to carry out the plans and projects that President Johnson outlined in his Johns Hopkins speech last spring. At the moment we have no plans to come to the Congress for any appropriations under that particular authorization. It is just impossible to predict to what extent we will apply for appropriations in the next few years. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Gaud. 

Mr. Frelinghuysen.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I would like to welcome Mr. Gaud to the committee again, and follow up the questions of our chairman, and ask, first, specifically about some administrative expenses.

Mr. Gaud, in Taiwan there has been no AID program this year and none is contemplated for next year. 

Mr. GAUD. That is right.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Yet I find there are administrative expenses listed in this fiscal year of almost $500,000 and for next fiscal year $156,000. Why is this?

Mr. GAUD. Because, Mr. Frelinghuysen, we have some loan projects that are still being completed. We have accounting and auditing jobs still to do there. They are getting Public Law 480 assistance.

We have auditing functions to perform with respect to that. It is a question mainly of auditing, although not entirely so. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Does that account for the expenditures in 

Burma as well? I know there are AID programs for this fiscal year for Burma amounting to $300,000, and administrative expenses come to $133,000. For next fiscal year the program for Burma is $200,000 and administrative expenses are almost as high, $147,600.

How do you explain such high administrative costs with such a small program?

Mr. GAUD. The program is small and no new money is going into it. We are completing projects that have been underway for a long time. We have a young man who is the AID representative in Burma who is in charge of completing these projects. 

This staff is quite small; auditors and accountants. That is what accounts for the people there, Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. HALL. He has a total of 8 Americans and 27 foreign nationals. 

That is both administrative and program, sir. The administrative is 

4 American nationals and 11 Burmese nationals.

Mr. GAUD. We have two men and two secretaries on the administrative side, as I recall it.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. All of these salaries are paid out of administrative funds? Aren't some paid out of program funds?

Mr. GAUD. There are four administrative people who are paid out of administrative funds. There are four people, technicians who are working on these projects, who are paid out of program funds in 

Burma. There are eight Americans, all told.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. The expenses of administering and running it may actually be considerably higher than the amount- Mr. GAUD. The engineers who are working on the project there will be included in the program costs that you gave. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Before my 5 minutes runs out, Mr. Chairman,

I would like to get back to the justification for both the open ended authorization and for a long-term authorization.

Mr. Gaud, I am sure you know that my record since I have been in 

Congress, which is 14 years now, has not been in opposition to foreign aid. 

Mr. GAUD. That is so.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't find anyone, including yourself, if I may include you, who has made much of a case for either an open ended authorization or a long-term authorization. You have just suggested that it is impossible, in effect, to predict what you are going to need in the way of administrative expenses for the next 5 years. So you say, "Because we are asking for a long-term authorization we are asking for no dollar limit."

To my mind the answer to that situation is not to give you a longterm authorization, and to include a dollar limit.

You have suggested that there is some psychological problem in the field if we don't change the system we now have. Yet what is to be gained in any specific way by simply allowing you to make the decision with respect to the amount that you plan to spend in any one period. Since you have to come to Congress to get an approval of that decision anyway, I fail to see why eliminating one of your ports of call accomplishes much.

In other words, so long as appropriations are on an annual basis, you are not going to escape the necessity of having Congress scrutinize what you propose to do. Nor do I think you should want to escape that scrutiny. I don't see why you want to escape the scrutiny of the

Foreign Affairs Committee. What is accomplished, specifically, by the change? Why should we not get a dollar figure as we do in other areas? Why is it essential for you not to abide by a dollar figure which you, yourself, are actually establishing when you come and request the funds? We establish the ceiling on the basis of what you say you are going to need. Why isn't that a pretty good system?

Mr. GAUD. Ordinarily it is easy for us to predict for the year ahead what we are going to need for administrative expenses or for other programs. For some of these programs, however, it is very difficult for us to predict, and it is true of administrative expenses, precisely what we are going to need 2, 3, 4, 5 years ahead.

We could solve that problem easily by asking for a very high limit on the authorization. I don't think that is playing fair with the 

Congress or with the committee. So that it seems fairer to us to admit quite frankly that we can't tell what we are going to need for administrative expenses 3 or 4 years from now, and ask for an open ended authorization, knowing all the time we are going to have to come back to the Congress, and ask for an appropriation in a specific amount. Also, as I am sure you remember, we have said that if this committee or the Foreign Relations Committee in the Senate wants us to come up and justify a program on a year-to-year basis before you, we will also do that.

It is a practical question. We are not trying to get away from specific figures. It is just a practical question of how can you estimate this with any precision 2, 3, 4 years ahead. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I couldn't agree with you more. You can't estimate it. That doesn't seem any excuse for issuing you a blank check. Because you can't estimate expenses, we need some restraint, we need to check on how the programs are changing. If, as you anticipate, expenses are going to keep increasing, it seems to me we need you to come back each year and tell us each year that they keep increasing. 

If there is one way for the foreign aid program to get in troubleand this is what you say has been the case on the past annual basis-it seems to me it is to ask for too much freedom.

This is what you are trying to do. I think you aren't accomplishing anything. I don't believe all this talk that you can't negotiate effectively with recipient countries on a long-term basis because you must go through the ordeal of an annual appeal to Congress. You know you have to go through the annual ordeal, and so do they. It doesn't seem to me to make a particle of difference whether you have a longterm authorization or not.

Mr. GAUD. This is what it comes down to. There are obvious disadvantages, and you have brought them out, in having an open ended authorization. The question is, Do the advantages of having a multiyear authorization, outweigh the disadvantages of coming in for an open end authorization for some items?

You feel that they don't. I feel that they do, that the advantages of a multiyear authorization are considerable. 

Let me say, quite frankly, Mr. Frelinghuysen-and I have made this clear in my opening statement-I think these advantages are largely intangible. I don't think you can point to very many specifics and say that a multiyear authorization is better for this reason, that reason or another specific reason. I think it is largely an intangible matter. It is not only the people overseas who I am talking about, but it is the whole attitude of the country toward this program. 

If this program, like many other programs were accepted by the Congress as something that would continue from year to year, as necessary if that acceptance were given the program, I think it would do a great deal to change the attitude of the people in this country toward foreign aid, and it would put the whole thing on an entirely different footing.

Beyond that there are other arguments. Mr. George Woods, for example, the president of the World Bank, feels very strongly-he has told me that a multiyear authorization would make it easier for the United States to deal with these countries to which we are giving a considerable amount of aid and encourage self-help and performance and the like.

I feel myself that in the European nations which we are trying to get to give out more aid each year, people who are against foreign aid use over and over again, and use effectively in their countries the fact that the United States goes through this agony every year of whether they will or will not have an aid program, and that it deters our effectiveness with these other countries. 

I can't quantify that. That is not a specific thing. You believe it or you don't. I believe these things deeply. I believe these are sound reasons for putting this on a multiyear program. 

You don't. I do. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Your whole assumption, if I may say so, Mr.

Gaud, is in favor of long-term appropriations. I see no way in which simply authorizing for a long period of time, with the necessity of annual appropriations, is going to accomplish the very laudable objectives of getting others to do more to help themselves or help other recipient countries. I fail to see how you can argue in fact I disagree with you--that they don't understand our procedures and that they would understand if we only had long-term authorizations. 

Mr. GAUD. Mr. Frelinghuysen, as far as I know, there is not a single program or a single agency in this country which has appropriations for more than 1 year. Our process, which has grown up and been established over a period of 150 years, is an annual appropriation for every program, and an annual budget by the President. We should have an annual appropriation. We are in favor of an annual appropriation.

I am trying to meet your point that we can't achieve my objective without going the whole hog and having a multiyear appropriation as well as a multiyear authorization. I disagree with that because under the American system of government you never have a multiyear appropriation for anything. You do have a multiyear authorization for most things. All we are asking is that it be put on the same basis as most other government programs.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I know what you are asking but I see no reason why we should give it.

Mr. WHALLEY. You say you think $75 million is adequate-

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fountain. 

Mr. FOUNTAIN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Gaud, with respect to the hiring of personnel, your table shows that you estimated total on-board employment as of June 30, 1966, to be 15,850, and the total employment of 1967 is about a thousand more. I wonder if you would explain the reasons for that increase?

Mr. GAUD. The increase is due almost entirely to the situation in 

Vietnam. As far as the Agency as a whole is concerned, Mr. Fountain, if you will exclude Vietnam, the number of our direct-hire personnel has been decreasing. They are increasing very substantially in Vietnam.

For example, on June 30, 1965, the total of our direct-hire personnel in Vietnam, both U.S. nationals and foreign nationals, was 

1,629. As of June 30, 1966, we estimate they will total 2,365. By 

June 30, 1967, we estimate they will total 3,221.

That is a doubling, almost an exact doubling of people in Vietnam between June 30, 1965, and June 10, 1967.

This throws all of our personnel figures askew. 

Mr. FOUNTAIN. What percentage of these personnel is permanent?

Mr. GAUD. None. They are all being taken on on a limited basis. 

Mr. FOUNTAIN. Full time- 

Mr. GAUD. They are all full time. But they don't get any tenure. 

We are going to be faced with quite a problem as people come back from Vietnam, having served a tour out there, or as the requirement for personnel in Vietnam decreases, because we are going to have a lot of people who have spent some time with us, many of whom will want to stay with the Agency and the odds are that we will not be able to place them in the Agency because there are so many people working in Vietnam.

We are going to have a replacement problem with them.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. What portion of the commitment made by the 

President at Honolulu is included in this bill? 

Mr. GAUD. It all is, as far as we now know. This bill covers all of our anticipated commitments on the economic aid side, including the commitments that he made at Honolulu.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. What is the total amount in the bill for Vietnam economic aid? 

Mr. GAUD. $550 million.

In a number of cases they have been improving the terms on which they give aid.

Mr. GROSS. You know, Mr. Gand. I have attended practically every session of this committee on this bill this year, and in previous years.

Mr. Farbstein. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Gaud, do we have any plans at all whereby local currency can be used, excess local currrency can be used within the country? I have particular reference to Egypt, where we have the American University. The proposed fiscal year 1967 calls for a grant of $1,200,000. 

We have $21 million in local excess currrency. 

Has anybody considered that maybe we could use any part of that for the American University in Cairo? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir. The $1.2 million that we are asking for the

American University in Cairo is made up of $1 million in local currency, and $200,000 in dollars to cover foreign exchange costs of the school. So $1 million of that is in the shape of local currency. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you.

In Turkey I think we have some excess foreign currency. Do we use any of that?

Mr. GAUD. In Turkey, as a matter of fact, we don't have any excess local currency. All of the local currency for U.S. uses we can get our

hands on in Turkey is needed to pay U.S. expenses of the Embassy, of the AID mission, of our troops there and the like. So the question doesn't arise in Turkey.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Have we considered at all the idea of excess local currency located in a particular country that no longer needs our assistance or grants or foreign aid of any nature, selling some of its products to other countries in the immediate vicinity of that country and being paid with that local currency, like Taiwan, for instance, where we have $8.8 million. Taiwan is a pretty successful nation today, I understand, and they manufacture some fairly important products.

Why can't we buy some of those products and send them to countries in the immediate vicinity of Taiwan and pay Taiwan with the $8.8 million that we have in local currency there? 

Mr. GAUD. I am not sure we have explored that possibility with respect to Taiwan. We have tried it in a number of other cases and we find that we haven't been able to get very far with it because what you are really asking the country to do is forgo an opportunity to earn foreign exchange, and most of these countries in which we have excess currencies need foreign exchange pretty badly.

The excess currency countries today, as I am sure you know, are India- 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I know what they are. 

Mr. GAUD. Poland, Burma, the United Arab Republic, and a few others mainly countries which have serious foreign exchange problems. That is the basic hitch in doing what you are suggesting. To the extent we can, it makes eminent sense to do it. We have tried with 

Israel, Pakistan, and one or two other countries, to work out arrangements whereby we could use our local currency to pay for exports to other underdeveloped countries. It is a very sensible idea. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Have you tried following out my suggestion with relation to countries in the area insofar as Israel is concerned? Has 

Israel turned down the supplying of the Arab countries with manufactured products?

Mr. GAUD. I don't know if we have suggested to Israel that it supply the Arab countries.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Couldn't we save some money in aiding these Arab countries without using up American or hard currency? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes. As far as Israel is concerned, Israel is a special case because we have found another way of turning our local currency, Israeli pounds, to good advantage. We have made a number of loans. 

They are quite sizable in amounts. We have made loans with the consent of the Israeli Government to outfits like the Hebrew University, the Weizman Institute, and a few other institutions of this sort, where we lend them our Israeli pounds and they repay us in dollars. So we are converting our currency there into dollars through that device. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I understand that is so. That is a worthy project whereby we can obtain hard currencies or put to use our local currency. 

I think this entire idea should be further studied and developed. 

Also, that hereafter thought should be given to the idea that any further contracts that are made shall contain clauses to the effect that when the country that is the recipient of our assistance under Public Law 480 is no longer entitled to aid of that type that then that local

currency, that excess currency be used for the benefit of other countries that are presently being aided by our currency, so that no longer shall we be in a position that we have to come to them for consent to use our own money.

Mr. GAUD. I agree. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. And see whether or not you can work that into any future contracts.

Mr. GAUD. I agree. May I throw something on the table here if I may. We are attacking that particular problem in another way, as 

I am sure you know. We plan, the Congress willing, if they adopt the food for freedom bill with this feature in it--over the next 5 years we plan to shift all sales of Public Law 480 to dollar repayment, starting this year at about 20 to 25 percent. The net result of this will be that we will stop piling up more local currency in the future, and hopefully we will work ourselves out of this thing. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I would also suggest at least your organization think about putting a clause as suggested in the agreement. 

Mr. GAUD. It is a good idea. We will think about it. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. We have plans, I understand, for a $300 million binational foundation in India; is that correct? 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. By what authority? 

Mr. GAUD. The Food for Peace Act, the Public Law 480 law, con tains a provision which authorizes us to make grants of U.S.-owned local currency with the consent of the Agriculture Committees. That provides that if we propose to make a grant, we lay the proposition before them and it sits there 30 days and if they don't object, we go ahead. That is what the law provides.

In fact, sir, before the President discussed this particular matter with Mrs. Gandhi, we not only discussed it with the members of those committees, but we discussed it with quite a number of other Representatives of the Congress, and we found no objection to it. So we went ahead and used the authority contained in Public Law 480. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. This is with the Agriculture Committee? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Mr. GAUD. I think actually, sir, it was a 4-year authorization- 

Mr. MORSE. Discount it by 20 percent.

Mr. GAUD. The Alliance for Progress program, of course, was announced by President Kennedy initially as a 10-year program. The 

United States embarked deliberately and avowedly and openly on a 

10-year program.

That was followed up by a 4-year authorization. It seems to me that the combination of those two factors gave the Alliance considerably more permanence and considerably more standing and more authority than other parts of our program which are authorized on a year-to-year basis. 

Mr. MORSE. Then why can't you achieve the purposes that you seek to achieve by some kind of Presidential announcement? 

Mr. GAUD. I don't think that would be enough. In the Alliance you had both ends of the avenue committed to a long-term program. 

Mr. MORSE. You didn't have them committed all the way. By your own statement, you had it committed for 5 out of 10 years. 

Mr. GAUD. That is right. But there is a whale of a difference between 4 or 5 years and 1 year. 

Mr. MORSE. Tell me the advantages that have accrued to the program over the advantages of the other programs administered by you?

Mr. GAUD. If the program is recognized as continuing from year to year, we feel-I know that George Woods of the World Bank and others feel-that it is easier, and you can be more effective in dealing with these countries. They know if they take the steps which you urge them to take that on the other side of the fence we are going to be able to support them. Many of the efforts that we make today- 

Mr. MORSE. How do they know that when they don't know there is going to be any appropriation from the Congress? 

Mr. GAUD. They don't know it 100 percent. As I said earlier, it is perfectly normal and conventional and customary under the American system of government for an authorization to be over a long period of time and appropriations annually.

There is no way of making an appropriation for more than 1 year. 

This is in the- 

Mr. MORSE. That which you recited as the first advantage fails.

Mr. GAUD. It doesn't fail because everyone recognizes that you never have more than a 1-year appropriation, whether it is State, Defense, foreign aid, or any other program. They accept this and are used to this.

The aid program as it is handled today goes far beyond that. It differs from most other programs of the Government. 

Mr. MORSE. Tell me the advantages.

Mr. GAUD. I have been telling you. I may not be persuasive, Mr. 

Morse, but I have told you what I believe the advantages to be. I will repeat myself. 

Mr. MORSE. Tell me what the advantages have been in the Alliance for Progress under a 4-year program over and above the advantages of the other programs which you are responsible for. Enumerate them for me, if you will, sir.

Mr. GAUD. I think the Alliance has had a much greater degree of credibility in the eyes of the other members of the Alliance because it has been a multiyear program. The Latin Americans, the Inter- 

American Development Bank, the others engaged in this effort have not had to wonder from year to year whether the Congress of the United States was going to reauthorize the program. 

There is an element of stability there, an element that you can rely on, of assurance, that is not present if you are going to go on a yearto-year basis. The benefits as I said earlier are largely intangible, but this is an intangible business we are in, trying to get countries to take hard decisions, trying to get countries to make the best of their resources and the resources we are giving them. Confidence is very important.

Second, about the character of the aid itself which is the construction of buildings there. It seems to me to be something that the country itself should be concerned with. And third, the need, if it is not an economic need, I don't see any political necessity for doing anything, but if there were a desire to do something of this sort in the hemisphere, there are many countries that are much more needy from this point of view than Mexico.

Mr. GAUD. Those are good questions, Mr. Monagan. I would like to go into it in a little detail; the main answer lies in the nature of the authority that is contained in section 214 of the act. We have here a program which is quite different from any of the rest of our programs. Most of our programs, as you say, relate to giving aid to developing countries.

Section 214 directly and specifically and expressly authorizes us to give aid to American institutions located abroad. The University of the Americas is a school that was founded in Mexico- Mr. MONAGAN. Those have had reference more to primary schools- Mr. GAUD. No, sir. Some are hospitals, some are colleges and some are secondary schools, such as the International College in Beirut and Athens College in Athens, which, even though they are called colleges, are really secondary schools. This Üniversity of the 

Americas was founded by Americans in 1940. They came to us 2 years ago to ask us to help them build some new buildings. 

We appointed a committee consisting of the president of the University of Wisconsin, the president of the University of Houston, the vice president of the University of California and the vice chancellor of the University of Hawaii who went down and studied this institution and came back and recommended to us that we help them on two conditions: one was that the university would prepare long-range plans, financial academic, physical plans, acceptable to AID; and second, that it would demonstrate its ability to raise its share of the cost of constructing these buildings.

They have come up with a plan that we think makes sense. They are in the midst of a fund-raising campaign. They have raised $700,000. Their target is $1.4 million, which they hope to reach by this summer. If they reach that target, we propose to make a grant of $2 million.

We have gone into their plans for the future. We believe this institution will be self-supporting. We have no intention of getting involved with any budget support for it. It is only a question of helping them build their plant.

Mr. MONAGAN. I understand that. It is that which gives me concern, not entirely because of this particular project, but because of the vistas that it would open up. At any rate I have stated the points that concern me.

(The information follows:) U.S. ROLE IN SOUTH VIETNAM ELECTIONS 

In the view of the Department of State, an important political process has been undertaken in South Vietnam. Hopefully, this process will lay the groundwork for creating lasting political institutions which provide for orderly political change and which are responsive to the will of the Vietnamese people. This is a Vietnamese process in which the United States has no intention or desire to interfere. The United States supports this process and will respect its outcome. 

In the interim, until a new government emerges, the United States will continue to support the present government as the legitimate government of the Republic of Vietnam. 

[Security deletion.]

(The information follows:) ASSISTANCE TO THE AMERICAN UNIVERSITY OF BEIRUT AND THE POLISH CHILDREN'S HOSPITAL 

Relatively large amounts have been granted to the American University, Beirut, since fiscal year 1959 ($33 million) because it is a principal center for higher education in the Middle East. Approximately half the grants have been made to assist in the construction of a new medical center. AUB has provided important educational and training services for AID financed participants over many years. For these reasons, the U.S. Government has devoted important assistance to this institution.

U.S. support to the Polish Childrens' Hospital has totaled $10,475,000 from fiscal year 1961 through fiscal year 1966. The construction of the hospital has been completed and it was dedicated in December 1965. The $175,000 grant in fiscal year 1966 is to finance a feasibility study of a proposed research laboratorybuilding, a nursing school, and residential facilities. It is not anticipated that the feasibility study will be completed in time to warrant an estimate for any fiscal year 1967 funds. 

Mr. FULTON. One other thing is, why is the Children's Hospital noted for such a small amount? This is in fiscal 1966, not any after,. when these colleges and universities keep going on?

That is all, Mr. Chairman, and thank you.

Mr. GAUD. We will put that in the record. (The information follows:)

CONTINGENUY FUND CARRYOVER 

It is impossible at this time to estimate the amount of unobligated contingencyfunds, if any, remaining at the end of the current fiscal year. It is possible. that situations will arise throughout the world which will necessitate the use or transfer of a large part or all of currently available contingency funds. We hope, of course, to use these available funds to the minimum extent consistent with U.S. interests. Whatever contingency funds remain unobligated at the. end of fiscal year 1966 will be carried over into fiscal year 1967.
