Mr. GAUD. Right. 

Mr. CAMERON. Shouldn't we have some limitation in this area?

Mr. GAUD. Subject to two things-and let's talk about the facts of life, as far as that particular category of funds is concerned. 

The size of the amount of money that we spend on this item, year in and year out, depends primarily on two things: How much we do for the Polish hospital and how much we do for the American University of Beirut.

We know they have building plans and we may well be supporting them. We should not put them down to such a low level that we can't support the few institutions that we want to support. 

We don't want to bring any new organizations into this category if we can avoid it. We are bringing one in this year, the University of the Americas. Sure, we could put a limit on that, but essentially we would want to be able to cover these two or three institutions that have building plans. 

Subject to that, yes, I agree with you.

Mr. CAMERON. I guess I have no further questions. I think the point is well made, though, that Mr. Morse made, that this proposal was not necessarily clearly thought out before it was brought up here, because you have made the statement several times, repeatedly, that you have put limitations on all those areas where limitations could reasonably be done, and we have found three more where limitations would be reasonable.

You are making a substantial change in foreign policy—— 

Mr. GAUD. Don't misunderstand me. This proposal was carefully thought out. If we can't get it the best way, what we believe to be the best way, I am prepared-I will be reasonable and discuss it with you. We think this is the best way to go about it. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I would like to follow up the discussion that has taken place here this morning with respect to the long-term authorization, the contingency fund and some of these other problems. 

First of all, Mr. Gaud, I think it was most unfortunate and unfair, even if you said it facetiously, that you indicated that much of the reason the AID program is in trouble is that you now have to come to this committee every year. That is a most unhappy choice of language in describing the problems that you may face before this committee. 

Mr. GAUD. May I say a word on that, sir?

If I gave that impression, it was unintended. I think one reason that the AID program has so little acceptance here and abroad is that the Congress authorizes it only on a year-to-year basis. I did not means to imply--and if I did, I apologize for it-that we have trouble with this committee. I don't mean that.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I would think the foreign aid program has as much acceptance as it does because we authorize it on a year-to-

year basis. Surely we can disagree in a reasonable manner, and be careful of what we say.

You have indicated, Mr. Gaud, that we ought to put limitations on wherever we reasonably can, yet the bill before us, in effect, removes almost all limitations. About the only limitation we have left is an arbitrary limitation on the contingency fund. This, as you have indicated, really means absolutely nothing at all, because all you have to do is come back for a supplemental. 

If you need more money for contingencies, all you have to do is come back. I would guess in the next 12 months that you are not going to be able to live within the $75 million which you are now requesting Half of that amount of money was used for one country, the Dominican Republic, in the current fiscal year. 

We can anticipate the same kind of problems around the world as we have had in the current 12 months. In the case of contingencies you could justify more easily removal of any ceiling than you can justify removal of a ceiling in a field such as adminstrative expenses. 

As I understand your argument for removing the ceiling and providing open end authorization for administrative expenses and perhaps I misunderstood you; I could hardly believe my ears you feel it would be difficult for us to accept a ceiling that would be sufficiently high to be reasonable over a 5-year period. 

This, in my book, is a red light against giving you an open ended authorization. If administrative expenses are going to be up so high that you think we wouldn't approve them, if you gave a dollar level, and for that reason you are asking for no ceiling, this is a good reason in my book not to go along with your request. 

Mr. GAUD. That wasn't again I am afraid I didn't make myself clear. I don't know how high they are going to go in the next few years. It is unpredictable. As we have already seen this year and last year, it is unpredictable. The increases are due primarily to pay increases, inflation, that sort of business, plus what has happened to the Vietnamese program.

What we are trying to do here is present the committee with a scheme which we think is reasonable, which we hope you will think is reasonable, which will make it unnecessary for us to come back for an authorization every single year. That is the objective. That is the name of the game, so to speak.

If we went along with the present figure on administrative expenses or a figure a couple million dollars higher, we could be sure that we would be back up here-unless current trends change-in 3 years at most. We are trying to avoid that.

One way is to put an arbitrary high ceiling on it, which I don't think makes sense and I'm sure you don't. The other is to make it open ended.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Why do you envisage a ceiling so high in 5 years that it wouldn't be acceptable today? I don't see any difficulties in coming back and making a change to increase a ceiling-Lord knows we increase the debt limit often enough. We do it because necessity indicates we have no choice. If we have no choice but to authorize a higher ceiling it would seem you could come back in 2 or 3 years. 

We are reasonable men and women and we can give you the answer that you are looking for then. To argue that you can't give us a

dollar figure because it would be so high that we would reject it seems to me a reason not to give you what you are asking. Why should we let you go ahead without coming back to us specifically for approval of a big increase in the expenditures in this area?

On the basis of past experience, you could project a slight increase over the next 5 years, such as has taken place. If it isn't slight, indicate it isn't going to be slight, and get us to worry about it with you now.

Mr. GAUD. We don't see anything that is going to make this thing go up by any large amount.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Why not give us a ceiling? If there is any way to get your program in trouble it is simply to say that we don't know what the future holds and we don't want to be held to a projection that may not be realistic. If the future proves you were right, and the increases are drastic you would be in more trouble than if you came back then and said "The reason we are asking for an increase is because circumstances have changed."

I say this as a friend of the program. I am not trying to make your job more difficult. I think you are making your job more difficult, as a number of others on the committee here have said. You are running real risks, and I agree with Mr. Morse, that you may get a curtailment in the development loan authorization and the Alliance for Progress authorizations because of your request for programs that cannot justify as easily the need for long-term authorization. 

Mr. GAUD. May I say a word on the administrative expenses. Our administrative expenses have not gone up drastically year by year.

They have gone up steadily because of factors we can't control. I expect this will happen in the future. I don't know. 

Certainly I am unable to predict with any accuracy precisely what they will be 5 years from now. Here we are today in the spring of 

1966. The committee has seen us operate for 5 years. They know what our history has been. They can see what we have done with administrative expenses. You can figure out whether our requests have been reasonable over the 5-year period.

I was hopeful that we had established enough credibility with the committee with respect to administrative expenses and technical cooperation funds that the committee would be prepared to say that "Yes, this is all right. There is no need to put a figure down when you can't figure the expenses precisely."

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Why not project a ceiling that would not be unduly restrictive, and which you could point out at a later date is out of date, if it should prove to be so?

Could I get back to the contingency fund? We talk about recoveries in the current fiscal year of $5 million. Would this be available in the next fiscal year without further congressional action? 

Mr. GAUD. Prior to the supplemental appropriation, we had nearly $56 million available, with an appropriation of $50 million. The $6 million in additional availabilities we are entitled to use, and we do use without going to the Congress for any additional authority.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. How about the amount that is not used in the supplemental? Is that going to be available if it isn't used this year on these unevaluated projects? 

Mr. GAUD. That will have to be reappropriated. Those are not noyear funds.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. The recoveries do not have to be reappropriated, but the money that is not actually earmarked and recovered must be? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, as you say. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The gentleman's time has expired. 

Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Mr. Gaud, I hope you won't feel I am beating a dead horse here. I would like to pursue a subject of continuing interest to me.

If one were to look at the nonmilitary, noneconomic side of the Vietnamese struggle over the past 6 years or 8 years, is it your judgment that we have learned anything that is of use in other countries, such as Thailand and other developing nations around the world? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir; I think we have.

Mr. FRASER. Who is responsible for assimilating that learning and applying it here in Washington?

Mr. GAUD. Our Control Program Coordination Office. Certainly, also the people who are running the Agency such as Dave Bell and myself are. We have a number of staff offices that are involved in this. 

For example, let's suppose we have learned something in the agricultural field. The way the Agency is set up we have people with a central staff responsibility for agriculture whose job it is to see, keep in touch with all the various regions Mr. FRASER. I was trying to exclude both the military and the economic. 

Mr. GAUD. I beg your pardon. I misunderstood your question. 

Mr. FRASER. Let me start over again.

The military is an identifiable operation in Vietnam. I recognize it has many aspects to it. The economic side involves many aspects, supplying commodities and a whole range of programs. But I want to leave these aside now and ask whether we have learned anything in terms of the way that we can become usefully involved in the provinces- Mr. GAUD. Rural development, building local institutions, and so forth? 

Mr. FRASER. Yes. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes; I think we have learned a fair amount in Vietnam, in East Pakistan, Thailand, Brazil, and in a number of places. 

Mr. FRASER. In Honolulu there is a training center for people who go out to Vietnam.

Mr. GAUD. Yes. It is primarily for provincial representatives and assistant representatives.

Mr. FRASER. The role of the provincial representative is partly to facilitate economic aid? 

 It is not limited to that.

Mr. FRASER. My impression is that there is considerably more to the provincial representative.

Mr. GAUD. As I see it, the purpose of the provincial representative is to encourage in its broadest sense the development in his province. 

It means the development of local institutions. It means the development of local political institutions and economic development. means social development, social progress. It means development in the broadest sense of the word.

Mr. FRASER. Do I understand then as far as Vietnam is concerned that AID fully accepts the primary responsibility for what I call political development?

Mr. GAUD. I wouldn't I have never thought about the question of whether we accept primary responsibility for it, but we recognize that it is an integral part of our job, to foster political development in this broad sense.

Mr. FRASER. Is there any other agency that is accepting a major role?

Mr. GAUD. I would think the State Department would feel this was one of its primary preoccupations and objectives. 

Mr. FRASER. Do they have any personnel in the provinces who are trained and in a position to do anything of this kind? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes; they do in limited number, but there is an office that is attached to the Embassy called the Liaison Office headed by General Lansdale. He has a staff and this is their primary and direct responsibility, Mr. Fraser.

Mr. FRASER. He doesn't have any provincial representatives, as I understand it.

Mr. GAUD. There is no resident staff in the provinces. 

Mr. FRASER. Are we giving training to people going into Thailand equivalent to that given the provincial representatives going to Vietnam?

Mr. GAUD. No, sir. The only countries for which we have trained people in this way so far are Vietnam and Laos. 

Mr. FRASER. I have the impression with respect to Thailand that we are doing much better there than we did at a comparable stage in Vietnam.

I also have the impression that we aren't fully drawing the conclusions and developing the concepts which we ought to be able to, based on this experience in Vietnam, and effetively applying them in our efforts in Thailand, whether it be in terms of training people to go into Thailand or even more important, putting something in Washington in charge of Thailand.

John Nuveen wrote an article a few years ago in which he in effect advocated, among other things, that there ought to be in Washington a sort of a counterpart to the country ambassador, a person who draws together all of the responsibility and capabilities of the various agencies, but who in Washington stands as the person responsible as the counterpart to the ambassador for what happens. I know there are interagency committees. I have always thought a committee was a poor way to make anything happen, particularly with respect to Thailand.

It seems to me there is nobody in Washington that you can put your finger on and say that person-short of those at the very top level, the President and the Secretary of State that this person is the one with the day-to-day responsibility for all aspects of what is happening in Thailand, including this political side—the noneconomic and nonmilitary side.

I really feel strongly about this. I think if we can develop our capabilities outside of the straight economic and military side, if we can learn these lessons and apply them and adapt them, we could do much better with far fewer bullets.

I don't know whether you want to comment. I am making a speech.

I don't know how we are going to move on this. This is my concern. 

Mr. GAUD. Let me say a few words, if I may, about both of the propositions that you have been discussing.

First, are we adequately set up in AID to deal with the broad question of development, including political development? 

We do not have in AID, as you know, any office-I will take that back-in our Program Office we have a group that is working on this subject. We do not have in our Agency a central group comparable to the engineers, the lawyers, the agriculturalists, who deal with this problem. We are doing a good deal more thinking about it, and are a good deal more conscious of it now than a year or 2 or 3 ago. 

On this recent trip I made out to southeast Asia I discovered that the man in our Thai mission who is in charge of rural development in Thailand will finish his second term out there and will be coming back to the United States this summer.

I mentioned to him the importance, as I saw it, of his coming back and working in Washington and occupying such a job, at a central place in the Agency, where he would work on these rural development questions-political development, social development, economic development-on an agency wide basis, and try to pull together the experience we have had in these various countries and at the same time disseminate that experience to the other parts of the Agency. 

I agree with you. I think it would be useful. 

Τα move to your second question as to whether Washington, the executive branch as a whole is set up adequately to deal with the question of Thailand or whatever it may be: To put one man in chargelet me just give you my personal views to take one man and say "You are in charge of Thailand across the board, State Department, Department of Defense, AID, USIA, CIA, Agriculture Department, so forth and so on," this has real problems.

Look at it from our standpoint, for example. We have so many dollars to program for the entire world. We have to keep the programing authority in AID. We can't give it to 50, 60, 70 country directors or individuals who are in charge of particular countries. 

Each one of them will want more when you put them all together than the pie will sustain.

You have to keep some programing functions, some central control, in AID. I think that the same thing is true of most other agencies around town.

Nevertheless, the President has taken a step in the direction in which you want to go, as I understand you. He has recently issued a directive to the executive branch setting up in the State Department a senior interdepartmental group, SIG, we call it, which will be chaired by the Secretary of State and have on it representatives of all of the agencies doing business in the foreign field. To this group under the leadership of the Secretary of State is given a great degree of control, a greater degree than any single agency has had in the past in this area. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I would like to advise that the second round has begun. If any of the members desire to come back, the Chair will recess, but otherwise, I think-

WEDNESDAY, APRIL 27, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:20 a.m., in room 

2172, Rayburn House Office Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding. 

The committee meets this morning for a continuation of the hearings on the Foreign Aid bills, H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450.

Our first witness this morning is the Honorable Gilbert Rohde, president of the Wisconsin Farmers' Union, for the National Farmers Union. 

Mr. Rohde you have a prepared statement. You may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF GILBERT ROHDE, PRESIDENT OF THE WISCONSIN FARMERS' UNION, FOR THE NATIONAL FARMERS UNION 

Mr. ROHDE. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

With me also this morning is Mr. Reuben Johnson, who is director of legislative services here in Washington, D.C., for the National 

Farmers Union. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Rohde, I believe that most members of the committee are well acquainted with Mr. Johnson. 

Mr. ROHDE. Thank you, sir.

We have prepared a statement which I should like to read and at various times I would like to depart from the statement to inject some pertinent information that I think the committee might be interested in.

The members of National Farmers Union are interested in the wellbeing of farm people all over the world. This interest is practical economics as well as humanitarian.

We believe that no nation can continue to be rich and secure simply by isolating itself from the poverty and hunger of surrounding nations and that our Nation's agriculture cannot hope to prosper and achieve more for the producers so long as most of the world's farmers can hardly feed themselves.

As a practical matter, we believe that the soundest kind of a food program is one in which all nations share the benefits as well as the responsibilities. We feel strongly that only when there is a worldplanned food program can there be a sound foundation for developing a world peace. And whatever encourages other farmers to produce more, and in the process to improve their own well-being, contributes to world peace. 657

That is why we give strong support to the efforts of the Agency for International Development to assist the people in the new and developing nations throughout the world. While we reserve the right to criticize some of its operations in an effort to improve the kind of assistance we are providing, we at the same time recognize and praise the far-reaching achievements of AID to date. 

Our members, too, identify food for freedom and AID as performing much the same kind of assistance, that of helping people in other lands. They do not differentiate, for the most part, where the assistance of the USDA leaves off and that of AID begins. And in this concept of measuring the total impact of our assistance there is considerable merit. Certainly hungry people are not concerned about the compartmentalized nature of our foreign assistance, but only the total impact.

The food-for-peace program, as Congressman Alex Olson said- Enables us to substitute a partnership concept (for one of food dumping), so that our farmers cooperate with those of other countries to meet the growing needs of the world. Our farmers will be able to furnish from their abundance the immediate assistance that people in other countries need to win the war against hunger. Our farmers will be able to do their part within the broad framework of a global self-help program that will assist farmers in other countries to gradually increase the productivity of their land. 

In this connection, the Farmers Union is encouraged by the importance that AID is now placing on agriculture and cooperatives in its assistance planning.

We note that for fiscal year 1967, AID proposes to invest about $152 million in programs to speed agricultural growth in developing countries. That is one-third more than is being spent currently and onehalf again as much as was spent in fiscal year 1965. We commend this as a good start. But more needs to be done more money, additional personnel and more effort. In this effort, we are trying to reach 80 percent of the people with about 20 percent of the money available for foreign assistance.

Even so, we would like to point out that it is not only the amount of money, but how it is to be used, which will determine how effectively we are in fact reaching and helping the people in other countries. 

In this connection we have just examined the report on AID's cooperative assistance that was recently printed as a staff report to the 

Banking and Currency Committee's Subcommittee on International

Finance. The accomplishments for the past fiscal year are encouraging, and the participating cooperatives should be commended for their part. Certainly, we in the Farmers Union take considerable pride in the contribution we have made in manpower, membership participation, and in other forms of assistance. 

Reference is made to section 601 of the Foreign Assistance Act of 

1961, better known as the Humphrey amendment, to encourage cooperative developments. We take special pride in that amendment, because the Farmers Union and its affiliated cooperatives pressed for such a measure for a long time, and finally prevailed on the Vice President to give it the necessary active support to make it a law. 

First, we would like to know in what ways the Farmers Union might assist AID in expanding the cooperative assistance, recognizing that cooperative development is part of the overall country assistance and varies from nation to nation.

. 

Secondly, we would like to know in what specific ways AID intends to utilize what the law says it should to speed the development of agriculture around the world. Are there any efforts made by AID to recruit cooperative assistance from U.S. sources; any plans for explaining-because most of the people still are woefully lacking in basic information about our foreign assistance-what the needs are; any effort to encourage a better understanding and an appreciation of what cooperatives are uniquely fitted to do?

In making our own assistance available, we wish also to add that people and organizations in the other so-called advanced countries also are providing assistance. The AID cooperative report suggests that.

In our own case, Farmers Union for many years has worked very closely with farm and cooperative organizations of more than 25 foreign countries, who are members of the International Federation of Agricultural Producers. For many years we exchanged information, and at one time there were serious thoughts of creating an international pool of highly skilled and technical people in agricultural and cooperative subjects. We still think that such an idea would be extremely valuable to establish a people-to-people relationship, rather than government to government. 

Our members have supported, and in some instances have pioneered, ideas-ranging from a world food bank and world food reserve to plans for land reform and resettlement-that bit by bit are being accepted into the orthodox thinking of even some of the more conservative countries.

We have made these efforts, as we said earlier, because we believe that they make good sense, for our own farmers as well as those in other countries. And we say this without in any way detracting from the strong humanitarian sense, the spirit of the golden rule, that prevails among our members and farm people generally. 

We refer to this because in addition to the people our organization has contributed to help AID carry out its agricultural programs in South America, Africa, the Far East, and in the Near East and India, our own members have made a fine contribution to better understanding between peoples.

Nearly 300 of our Farmers Union families have opened their homes to foreign agricultural trainees brought to this country by AID, under an arrangement with our Farmers Union. They have been hosts, taken these young farm leaders in as members of the family. They have been guests, and these families have not charged a cent for board or room or any kind of services. They have gone out of their way to show them what American farm life is like, how democracy works, the politics of the community and county, the operations of cooperatives and how they fit into the life of the farmer and the business community.

We are proud of the contribution-over and above the dollar equivalent of board and room-that these families have made to AID's program. This is the kind of people-to-people relations that I think 

AID should encourage.

Mr. Chairman, if I might depart from our prepared statement for a minute to relate and share with you some of our own personal experiences as we have known them to be in Wisconsin. For the past

several years our organization has participated and cooperated with our national organization in bringing Latin Americans to Wisconsin farm homes. We first determine what families would like to have them in their homes for a period of 6 weeks. We then assign these young people, these Latin Americans, to these homes. 

They have a very limited knowledge of the English language, and it is surprising how easily and rapidly they are able to communicate with each other.

Our Latin Americans are considered not as hired men but as guests, guests of the family, but they do participate in all of the activities that the family engages in with respect to their social affairs, their work habits, and so on.

We feel that this has had a tremendous impact on these people. 

The Latins help in fixing the machinery, doing all the chores around the farm. We have found in many instances that the thing that impresses them most is how hard our farmers work. 

We think in many instances when they get back home and are able to adapt more readily to the work habits they have gained a considerable amount from that.

Last year we held a special experimentation. Our organization has camping facilities of considerable size in Wisconsin, and we had some 90, all of the Latin Americans that the Farmers Union sponsored, at our camp for 1 week. We held a special Latin American night in our home city of Chippewa Falls in cooperation with the chamber of commerce of that city, where we asked the city folks to invite one of these Latin Americans into their home to be a guest for one evening. 

The impact of this was considerable in our small town of 18,000. It gave our city friends an opportunity to learn firsthand of some of the pressing problems that our Latin Americans have in developing their economy. Our people in our hometown are looking forward to such an event this year, when we will have nine Latin Americans participating in our program in 1966.

To continue on with our testimony, we do not care to dwell on the need to bring assistance directly to the people in the rural areas, rather than let a few drops trickle down from the top. There is general agreement, we believe, that this was the weakness in Vietnam; we reached too few, with too little, too late. Certainly there is no need to repeat this in other countries.

In this broad sweep, we believe there is general agreement on what should be done, and we are certain that there are recommendations and proposals and suggestions by the hundreds among the files of position papers in AID. Therefore, there is no need to add to the already mountainous accumulation.

The potential of cooperative development, which is still greatly in need of emphasis, will have its greatest opportunity to prove itself when the functional experiences of operating cooperative personnel is recruited to work within the overall framework of country development.

The gestures to stimulate greater agricultural production will be meaningless until the man with a hoe, who raises the food, is reached, and believes that what is offered to him is a good thing. Cooperatives, along with education and training, can speed the process of acceptance.. www

Farmers Union-and our affiliated agricultural cooperatives-stand ready to assist in whatever ways we can to make agricultural assistance a genuine success.

We appreciate very much the opportunity to present our views. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Rohde, for a very good statement.

I agree with your statement, Mr. Rohde, concerning the people-topeople program. I believe that the people in the Agency for International Development should pay more attention to this approach. 

I have a feeling that our agricultural approach in some of these underdeveloped countries is not up to standard. 

I feel that the Farmers Union, by bringing people here from Latin America to live on an American farm, where they can watch American farmers in operation and see the long hours they put in, is rendering a worthwhile service. If reports on these observations could be carried back to some of the countries in Latin America and in Africa, I think it would be helpful.

The Farmers Union is to be commended for this type of operation. 

I am aware that your organization has strongly supported the economic portion of this program, and we thank you for being here today. 

Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

May I ask just one rather, to me, fundamental question: Do you think if our farmers were relieved of some of the restrictions on their acreage, they would be able to do a great deal more for the world? 

Mr. ROHDE. Madam Congressman, I feel a little bit as though this question- 

Mrs. BOLTON. Would you mind getting a little closer to the mike?

We are both having mike trouble. 

Mr. ROHDE. I feel a little bit as though this question might be loaded from my point of view. 

Mrs. BOLTON. I don't mean it to be. 

Mr. ROHDE. I am sure you don't. 

Mrs. BOLTON. I have a farm myself.

Mr. ROHDE. Our interest is how to get more food to them. Our primary interest as a farm organization and as a farmer is how we are going to reward the farmer properly for any additional efforts that he may make in expanding his production.

I am a Wisconsin farmer. Our economy is struggling along at about 

82 percent of parity. We are rather disturbed about this and are doing all we can to improve the situation. When we think in terms of how we might be able to provide more food, this suggests perhaps that we need to be working and bending our efforts toward developing a national policy which would properly reward the farmer in an economic way for his additional efforts. Unquestionably the food is needed. There are limitations as to how much can go to a particular area at any particular time, but a more important question to the American farmer is how are we going to reward him or enable him to receive a parity of income for his efforts. 

Mrs. BOLTON. There are two or three things that disturb me in what you say. I would be very sorry to feel that the farmer had changed enough to feel that he should be what you say "rewarded" further than he is. I do have a farmer son, so I know something about the situation.

It would seem to me that we have much needed to know from the Government how much surplus food we have. Some of us have been deeply troubled by the words that have come to us, that our surpluses have been drawn down very far, and that our generosity to other countries has been such that it has made it very difficult. 

Mr. ROHDE. I don't think the American farmer has changed his mind with respect to what he wants to do. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Of course he wants to live and he wants to improve his living for himself and his children. That is natural. 

Mr. ROHDE. We, as farmers, also feel that we are subsidizing to a greater extent than the other segments of our society in this total program. 

Mrs. BOLTON. That has always been so.

Mr. ROHDE. I would guess it had been. We think it is highly unfair that the American farmer should be expected to make this contribution far in excess of any other segments of our society that are enjoying- Mrs. BOLTON. What is your feeling about the possible value of increasing his ability to use his land on which he is restricted today?

Mr. ROHDE. Only on a voluntary basis. In the country where I come from, he is making his own decision whether he wants to take some land out of production or whether he doesn't.

Mrs. BOLTON. I am just speaking about taking it out of production. think that is the way for the Government to function? 

Do you

Mr. ROHDE. As I interpret it, the farmers have been attempting to get themselves in a position where agriculture as an industry can behave generally like other segments of our economy do. Because of the great number of farmers who operate independently of each other on the land, they are not able to do what industry does, for example, in connection with determining the amount to be produced, regulating their production so to say and then administering the price. 

So the farmer is dependent upon his Government and in cooperation with his Government is attempting to develop a system whereby we more nearly behave as industry does. This has been the main thrust of our efforts in this field. 

Mrs. BOLTON. You feel, then, that the farmer is the one who completely decides how much we shall have?

Mr. ROHDE. No, I guess the Government has been participating in helping make these decisions too. 

Mrs. BOLTON. That is why I am asking. If the farmer were permitted more wheat, I just use that as a general term, would we not be able to do a good deal more for the world and wouldn't the farmer be in a better situation?

Mr. ROHDE. I suppose this is a very controversial question. I am not familiar with what the CCC code is now. Maybe my colleague, Mr. 

Johnson, has some information or more information than I have on it. 

There have been huge surpluses in this area. The wheat farmer has not been at a parity with the economic level. 

STATEMENT OF REUBEN JOHNSON, DIRECTOR, LEGISLATIVE SERVICES, NATIONAL FARMERS UNION

voice of the person who lives in the rural areas, we have to do more than simply send over specialists to give technical advice.

Mr. ROHDE. May I just say to you that the technical specialist is a very important and key individual in these programs. I am not familiar with the case that you referred to in Nigeria. 

Let's move to Latin America. In some areas one of the most important things that needs to be done is to provide ways of irrigating their land. Of course this requires specialized training. The thing that we have learned from our Latin friends who have come here is that their communities, their villages, people in them are very sensitive to other people coming in there and doing things for them, or telling them what to do.

We are of the opinion that their agricultural production and the acceptance of new ideas will develop more readily if one of their own people has the ability to show them how to do things, and discusses with them ways and means of helping themselves. It is important that they have help, advisers who work at that level. In many instances, at least in some instances, the problem that we find of people accepting new ideas and techniques, new ways of doing things which we think would benefit them are viewed with suspicion, not readily acceptable because this is an outsider telling them.

The National Farmers Union agreement with AID is to assist in securing competent personnel for rural cooperative projects abroad, develop cooperative leadership and to provide teachers and training facilities for cooperative classes. 

The contract is held in behalf of the U.S. members of the International Federation of Agriculture Producers which has membership in 32 countries. The U.S. members are the National Council of Farmer Cooperatives, representing most of the commodity cooperatives in the United States, the National Grange, oldest of the U.S. general farm organizations, and the National Farmers Union, with extensive cooperative affiliates in the Great Plains region. 

The projects are carried out under Latin American region and worldwide agreements. The U.S. organizations and the foreign farm organizations are prepared to furnish technicians and cousultants upon the request of AID. The advisory committee to the National Farmers Union, composed of representatives of IFAP, Food and Agriculture Organization, and International Labor Organiza tion of the United Nations, meets annually. 

Regional and country projects have been established to train people for cooperative positions and agricultural leadership in Africa, Latin America, and the Far East. The Farmers Union has asigned agricultural and cooperative specialists to Kenya, Uganda, Dahomey, Tunisia, and India. It has held hundreds of local cooperative meetings and conducted leadership seminars in Uganda, Kenya, and Tunisia. It has made evaluation studies to determine the feasibility of livestock and grain marketing cooperatives in Ecuador. U.S. training teams have helped to coordinate cooperative rural work in Latin America. 

A third group of 83 Latin American farm people was invited from Bolivia, Colombia, Peru, Chile, and Venezula to live with farm people in the United States. This group spent 3 months on U.S. farms, learning about agriculture, community life, and cooperatives, and 2 months in classes at Jamestown College in North Dakota and the International Cooperative Training Center at the University of Wisconsin. This project's main purpose is to develop community leadership for campesinos organizations, particularly as land reform and resettlement go into effect.

The Farmers Union furnished a technical cooperative specialist to the Afro- 

Asian Rural Reconstruction Organization. This Organization with a member ship of more than a score of Asian and African countries is concerned with the development of independent cooperative organizations and farm organizations. 

Currently the Farmers Union is sending cooperative and credit specialists, organizers and teachers, to Uganda, Kenya, the Philippines and is reviewing with AID opportunities for giving assistance in Korea, Vietnam, Tunisia. Ethiopia, Liberia, and Nigeria.

Mr. GROSS. In connection with the questions and statements by Mr. 

Fraser, of Minnesota, I have before me a letter from an Iowa farmer that I received only a few days ago. He had returned from a trip to Africa. I want to read one paragraph. He says, "It is very important that Europeans stay in Africa. The African definitely needs guidance and help to come into the 20th century. For example," he says, "I visited a dairy plant in Arusha, Tanzania. The manager showed me his records. One farm which produced 300 gallons of milk per day when operated by a European farmer, is now, 90 days later, delivering 55 gallons of milk a day."

I assume, speaking now on my own, I assume that milk is a product much desired in Tanzania. I wonder how they will get milk for the 

Tanzanians if the Government is going to take over the dairy industry of that country and demolish it in that fashion. 

I don't know how much is in this bill for Tanzania. I don't know whether the Farmers Union is counseling the Tanzanians, but the technical assistance we are giving those people, if any, is not very productive if they are going to drive the Europeans out of the country who have the know-how to produce the food for the country. 

If that is happening, this program is an unmitigated failure.

Speaking of the student exchange business, Mr. Rohde, we have had thousands upon thousands of students come to this country. Perhaps some good has come from this; I don't know. However, the world situation seems to get worse, despite the hundreds of millions and billions of dollars that we have spent on this program, including the student exchange and exchange of people from foreign countries to this country and our sending people over there. We don't seem to be making very much progress. I don't know of an oligarchy or a military dictatorship that has been eliminated in Central or South 

America or the Caribbean area, do you? 

Mr. ROHDE. Let me comment on that, if I may.

The people whom we are bringing here through the program with AID are not students. These are campesinos, people who live in villages, and they are not farmers. They are between the ages of 20 and about 35. Many of them are married. They are screened by our people and determined to be leaders in the community with some degree of stability with respect to their habits, their attitudes toward their fellow man.

I haven't been to any of the Latin American countries, but I read articles. I have been under the impression that the Latin American countries are undergoing some kind of a revolution, social revolution. and that if we aren't able to be as helpful as we can in all areas of people-to-people communication, getting those people to understand a little bit about our cultures, our systems, methods and so on, that a more violent revolution may take place there.

Mr. GROSS. It may take place anyway, Mr. Rohde. 

Mr. ROHDE. I would prefer to deal with the matter more positively and hopefully to prevent a violent revolution.

Chairman MORGAN. Our next witness is John O. Teeter of Pfizer 

International, New York City, appearing for the Chamber of Commerce of the United States.

It is a real pleasure to welcome you again to the committee.

STATEMENT OF JOHN O. TEETER, VICE PRESIDENT OF PFIZER INTERNATIONAL, ON BEHALF OF THE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES 

Mr. TEETER. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. I hope we can once again be helpful to you and the committee.

This morning I am here to present the views of the Chamber of Com merce of the United States on the Foreign Assistance Act of 1966. 

I am chairman of the chamber's foreign operations review subcommittee and also serve on the national chamber's international commit-

tee and its joint subcommittee on foreign aid, composed of representatives from six chamber policy committees. I am accompanied this morning by Jay H. Cerf, manager of the chamber's international group, and by John C. L. Donaldson and Jack Gilbert of the staff.

In presenting the national chamber's position on foreign aid, I will emphasize that the chamber:

Recommends a balanced program of foreign assistance geared to long-range development.

Encourages increased emphasis on private resources in furthering foreign assistance objectives, and on reduction of Government programs after these programs have served their catalytic purposes. 

Supports selective aid to those countries determined to help themselves.

I will also present to the committee proposed amendments to the Foreign Assistance Act to implement these positions of our national federation.

Foreign aid in review: As you know, the concept of foreign assistance has been broken down into four major categories military, economic, social, and political or institutional assistance. Since 1945 the 

United States has directed its efforts toward various aspects of foreign assistance according to the demands of the time. Last year your committee and the Senate Foreign Relations Committee discussed at length various approaches for establishing effectively administered aid programs designed to achieve U.S. foreign policy objectives. Two foreign aid bills, H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450, have been introduced by your chairman to implement the discussions carried over from the last session of Congress.

In addition to these two bills, various bills directly related to foreign assistance have been introduced in the fields of agriculture, international health, and international education. These bills underscore the current emphasis being given by the administration to human needs in establishing criteria for foreign assistance programs. 

Our federation commends, in general, these newly emphasized elements of foreign assistance. We urge, however, that these programs not be viewed as a substitute for long-range economic development. 

We are concerned, also, that this year's legislation not seem to relegate proper emphasis on private sector participation in foreign assistance to a minor rule. This concern causes our federation to urge that you give appropriate consideration to a balanced foreign assistance program that takes into account economic as well as social and political measures to help developing countries to help themselves. 

In consonance with the administration's emphasis on the improvement of activities and programs that influence human attitudes, we cannot stress too strongly the fact that self-help efforts through private initiative are the driving force that motivates emerging countries to develop, on their own terms, their resources-human and material-to solve their problems.

Funds for development: Support for the principle of foreign aid does not necessarily imply approval of all the various programs known as "foreign aid" nor of every budget authorization request. Foreign aid administraion must be continually improved to yield budgetary savings.

The national chamber recommends a reduction of at least $187 million in the funds requested for economic assistance in this budget. This means that not more than $2.28 billion should be authorized for fiscal year 1967 economic assistance.

Included in this $2.28 billion figure is $500 million requested for supporting assistance to Vietnam. The national chamber supports assistance to Vietnam in view of the fact that our Government and Congress, who have access to documentation on operational costs, have deemed necessary military assistance to Vietnam, for the purpose of resisting Communist aggression and domination in the national interest of the 

United States.

The necessity for providing funds covering increased military operations in Vietnam, the uncertainty of future military requirements and the acknowledged inflationary pressures, generated in part by the increase in spending for Great Society programs, other than in Vietnam, should be held at not more than the level of fiscal year 1966 appropriations. 

Private enterprise and foreign assistance: Government-to-government programs are needed initially to build the infrastructure for participation by U.S and foreign private sectors in developing foreign economies. These Government programs should be phased out after they have served their catalytic purposes. 

To establish free and viable economies in countries that want to develop rapidly but often lack the human and material resources to support this development, the national chamber recommends that the Foreign Assistance Act of 1966 be amended to provide that: 

Foreign participation in private enterprise projects should be encouraged, when feasible, as a means of meeting nationalistic concerns in negotiating such agreements. Business efforts to conclude equitable agreements should be fully supported by the U.S. Government.

I cannot emphasize too strongly that private enterprise and Government cooperation in efforts to locate opportunities for development of free and viable economies is a necessary prerequisite to improvement of local conditions. A stable and growing economy is a thing which in a developing country can provide markets for U.S. goods and services. 

A mechanism is needed to bring the business communities of the United States and the aid-recipient countries into workable agreements that serve as tools for balanced economic development. To meet this need, the national chamber recommends that the Foreign Assistance Act of 1966 be amended to provide that: 

A small, permanent advisory board of U.S. businessmen, with responsibility for mobilizing private resources for oversea economic development, should be established.

Although President Johnson referred in his foreign aid message to the importance of increased reliance on private sector participation in the development of emerging nations, we feel that more needs to be done to implement such pronouncements. 

Highest priority should be given to the recommendations of the so-called Watson report and to the International Cooperation Year

Conference recommendations for involving the private sector more in foreign assistance. We are aware that some action, although limited,

has been taken on the Watson report recommendations. These activities have been reported to you by various AID officials. 

At all U.S. Government levels, coordination of foreign economic policy and programs is long overdue. The expansion of the foreign assistance program this year to increase responsibility for various and new food, health, and education programs in various Government agencies emphasizes the immediate need to coordinate these programs if we are to implement effectively our foreign policies. 

The chamber recommends that this committee encourage the establishment of a coordinating mechanism at no lower than the Assistant 

Secretary level that should (1) have full presidentially delegated authority and support, and (2) provide adequate and specific opportunities for U.S. business views to be registered and applied in the development and execution of foreign economic policy and programs of our Government.

The role of foreign investment in economic development: Last year, I testified before this committee that direct foreign investment is one of the most effective means of gaining and preserving access to oversea markets that would otherwise be lost to the United States. 

My company, Pfizer International, has found this to be particularly true. We are heartened to see that other companies in the past year have expressed a similar view.

I want to emphasize again that investment in such developed countries as those of Western Europe and Japan often provides the wherewithal for U.S. companies to invest in the developing countries and to assist these emerging nations along the road of self-improvement to the point where they have the opportunity to enjoy the fruits of free and viable economies.

In dividends it earns abroad and in U.S. exports it generates, direct overseas private investment is one of the most important sources of payments received in the U.S. international accounts. 

Restriction of the free movement of private goods and capital is not the solution to balance-of-payment problems. Remedial measures should be oriented to expansion, rather than restriction of world trade and investment.

The national chamber urges Congress and the U.S. Government to take these facts fully into account so that business will not be asked or required to conduct its operations for a protracted period in ways that do not maintain it fully competitive with international business. 

To encourage more U.S. companies to develop and expand their foreign markets, the national chamber recommends that the investment survey provisions of the Foreign Assistance Act be amended to provide:

That the making of such survey shall be approved by the President. The U.S. 

Government shall make a 100-percent advance for local currency expenditures from U.S.-owned local currencies, wherever feasible. If the project goes forward, the person conducting the survey will make a 50-percent reimbursement in dollar equivalents to the U.S. Government. 

The investment survey section of the act should also be amended to provide that if the project does not go forward, the person conducting an investment survey will:

Repay 25 percent of the total survey cost in dollar equivalents.

This amendment would have the beneficial effects of improving our balance of payments and rewarding the investor who goes ahead with a project.

National chamber positions on 1966 AID recommendations: 

Special emphasis has been placed by the administration this year on five categories of foreign assistance that have received individual attention in past years. These categories include: (1) Self-help measures to encourage emerging nations to solve their own problems; (2) Separation of economic assistance from military assistance; (3) Multiyear authorization of aid funds; (4) Agricultural improvement to meet food needs and to deal with population increases; (5) Multilateral and regional assistance.

Self-help: The chamber has long emphasized the importance of giving people, through the self-help measures, a sense of their own responsibility in the community of free nations. The attached chamber policy statement on foreign assistance points out that self-help criteria should be rigorously applied to the point of conditioning additional assistance on the quarterly fulfillment of written progress agreements.

To establish a procedure in foreign aid negotiations and to emphasize the importance of putting recipient countries in the position of solving their own problems, the national chamber recommends that the Foreign Assistance Act of 1966 be amended to provide that: 

Before allocating, reserving, earmarking, committing, or otherwise setting aside funds for use in any country under this title, the President shall require that recipient government representatives, after consultation with private sector representatives, submit a quarterly progress report with respect to the selfhelp and reform measures planned and achieved by the recipient country. 

Separation of military and economic assistance: The national cham ber has long advocated that the cost of military assistance should be allocated as a part of the Department of Defense budget. Present commitments on the Sino-Soviet periphery must be honored. We also urge that other industrial nations should bear more of the cost for military assistance programs.

Some students of foreign aid have said that military assistance must be included in the same package with economic assistance in order to "sell" economic assistance. This thesis was perhaps valid several years ago. However, the time has come for economic assistance to stand on its own merits. Long-range economic development of emerging countries must be presented in its true colors and not disguised in the gaudy wrappings of short-term palliatives. 

Multiyear authorizations: Long-range economic planning and development must be emphasized in foreign assistance programs. To accomplish this goal, a 4-year authorization of foreign aid funds, subject to annual appropriations and review by appropriate congressional committees, is desirable.

Such a long-term authorization would provide the continuity that is needed to establish effective aid programs. Authorizations should

be extended to 4 years, instead of 5 years as recommended by the administration, to allow each administration 2 years to evaluate and revise, as necessary, foreign assistance programs in the national interest of the United States.

Agricultural improvement: Our federation welcomes, in general, the new emphasis placed by the administration this year on food requirements and the need to improve agricultural techniques to meet these requirements.

Relief provided by Public Law 480 programs is a temporary measure. A condition of this type of assistance should be the demonstrated improvement of agricultural techniques and productivity that can alleviate the pressing problem of population increase. 

This major new direction of foreign economic assistance must make it perfectly clear to recipient countries that such assistance is temporary and designed only to meet their basic nutritional needs while they correct the situations that cause food shortages.

Another major emphasis of foreign economic assistance should be to provide funds for birth control programs, when such funds are requested by less developed countries. 

Multilateral and regional assistance: Multilateral development financing and technical assistance should be emphasized as an essential instrument for U.S. foreign policy. The private sector can serve a useful role in this field, particularly in regard to technical assistance programs.

International institutions, including regional development banks, in collaboration with other industrialized free world nations can play an increasingly important role in achieving foreign economic development objectives.

U.S. bilateral aid programs also serve a useful purpose. The national chamber recognizes a continuing need for a substantial U.S. bilateral program until acceptable multilateral programs are developed.

Conclusion: In summary, to provide necessary assistance to the developing countries and to further U.S. national interests, the national chamber urges this committee to consider favorably the proposed amendments to the Foreign Assistance Act of 1966 contained in this testimony. These amendments will insure continued refinement of a foreign aid program to Provide effective coordinating mechanisms to represent and present U.S. business and Government views on foreign economic policy and programs.

Increase reliance on private sector assistance, and reduce Government programs accordingly.

Base loan commitments or demonstrated self-help and reform measures.

In conclusion, Mr. Chairman, I request that the attachments to this statement be made a part of the official record of your hearings. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection it is so ordered. ww

STATEMENT IN FURTHERANCE OF POLICIES ON MUTUAL SECURITY I. BASIC CHAMBER POSITION ON FOREIGN ASSISTANCE 

(a) Foreign aid as a means toward foreign economic and social development continues to be a proper element of U.S. foreign policy. Its objectives and principles have long been endorsed by the chamber. (b) Refinement of the program must provide an effective base for the exercise of private enterprise and initiative in foreign economic and social development. Progress should follow the effective guidelines established by the AID Administrator. (c) Program objectives should be continually clarified as they relate to U.S. foreign policy and to U.S. and foreign private enterprise. The U.S. public demands clear justification of its foreign aid investment. 

II. CHAMBER PROGRAM PROPOSALS 

(a) Long-range economic planning and development must be emphasized. 

To accomplish this goal, a 4-year authorization of foreign aid funds, subject to annual appropriations and review by appropriate congressional committees, is desirable.

Although long-term aid investment seldom has dramatic impact, in general it can yield far higher returns than short-term "gimmicks." Economic planning need not mean a government "planned economy" in underdeveloped countries. 

Recent efforts at country development planning have shown the need for private foreign investment. Foreign economic development programs must place increased reliance on private resources, and reduce government programs after these programs have served their catalytic purpose. 

Private enterprise and government should cooperate in their efforts to locate opportunities for the development of free and viable economies that can improve local conditions and provide markets for U.S. goods and services. In particular, foreign participation should be encouraged when feasible as a means of meeting nationalistic concerns. In negotiating such arrangements, business efforts to conclude equitable agreements should be fully supported by the U.S. Government. 

A small, permanent advisory board of U.S. businessmen with responsibility for mobilizing private resources for overseas economic development should be established. (b) Government incentives to U.S. private foreign investment serve a useful purpose. But a more positive approach is imperative, centering around the

need to build an economic and political framework in which the profit motive can operate at full potential. Priority must be given to removal of disincentives that discourage private investment. Certain provisions in the Revenue 

Act of 1962 on taxation of foreign income are punitive and self-defeating. International antitrust standards and an international patent system should also be developed.

The United States should suspend foreign assistance to any nation that takes discriminatory or confiscatory action against U.S. private investment in such nation without taking appropriate steps to discharge its obligations under international law including prompt and full compensation or relief. Courts in the United States should determine and apply principles of international law in appropriate cases to encourage the development of an international legal system that will contribute to the improvement of the investment climate, and the protection of private property abroad. (c) Development loans should receive priority. The trend toward government lending to, and for the express development of, private institutions and organizations should be accelerated. Terms of development loans should take into consideration the overall debt management position of recipient countries. (d) A limited program of military assistance to selected allies is essential to total short-range free world security. Military assistance should be held to a minimum, particularly in Western Europe and Latin America. The cost to the 

United States of such assistance should be accountable to the Department of 

Defense. 

(e) Multilateral development financing and technical assistance should be emphasized as an essential instrument of U.S. foreign policy. In many instances, this approach is more practical and effective than the bilateral approach. Private international consortia should be utilized to the fullest extent possible. Congress and the administration should propose new means to achieve U.S. foreign economic development objectives through international institutions, including regional development banks, in collaboration with other industrialized free world nations provided the United States has voting rights commensurate to its contributions. Such programs must assign a major role to the private sectors of both donor and recipient nations, and the administrative cost of such programs must be equitably shared by the respective national governments. The chamber recognizes a continuing need for a substantial 

U.S. bilateral program until acceptable multilateral programs are developed. (f) Aid should be selective. The administration should apply great selectivity in choosing which nations and projects are to be aided from among ever-increasing requests for assistance. Assistance should be based on results. 

Self-help criteria should be rigorously applied, to the point of conditioning additional assistance on the quarterly fulfillment of written progress agreements.

Emphasis must be placed on technical assistance programs that can put aid recipients in the position of solving their own problems. (g) The food-for-peace program under Public Law 480, title II, serves in general the best interests of U.S. foreign policy. Relief provided by Public Law 

480 programs is a temporary measure. A condition of this type of assistance, and a major new direction of foreign economic assistance should be demonstrated improvement of agricultural techniques and productivity that can alleviate the pressing problem of population increase. Another major aid emhpasis should be to provide funds for birth-control programs when such funds are requested by less-developed countries. U.S.-owned local currencies should be used where available to finance investment surveys and the development of vocational training facilities. (h) The Alliance for Progress must be moved forward along the sound lines recommended by its Inter-American Committee (CIAP). A broader concept of the Alliance should be developed by encouraging other member countries to contribute appropriate financial and technical assistance for Latin 

American development.

The objectives of the Alliance must be realistically determined with emphasis on regional market integration and the availability of external resources. 

The Alliance must be coordinated through other institutions and programs. 

Alliance programs must create a stable atmosphere in which private investment and initiative, local and foreign, can operate beneficially.

(i) The aid task must be justly shared. The United States should continue to enlist greater cooperation from other industrialized nations in assisting less developed countries. The Development Assistance Committee of the OECD has an important duty as an instrument to serve this purpose. (j) Effective foreign assistance programs can only be implemented by competent personnel. Private enterprise urges, and is willing to cooperate with 

AID in, the maintenance of such standards. The administrators of the program must have the legislative authority to improve the quality and reduce the number of AID personnel, both domestic and overseas. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you for your statement. 

Mr. Teeter, I noticed on page 3 of your statement you recommend a cut of $187 million in funds requested for economic assistance. I don't see any place where you break this down. 

Where specifically do you recommend cuts?

Mr. TEETER. We have not specifically broken it down. In the following paragraph you will note that we went along with the request so far as Vietnam is concerned.

The cut actually would apply to countries other than Vietnam. 

This would be countries in the Near East, southeast Asia, and Latin America. This we feel is not a substantial cut, but it was our feeling that we would like to see appropriations kept to the level of the previous year.

The $187 million cut therefore comes in these areas, rather than Vietnam itself where we feel, as we stated here, it is impossible for us to really get into the depth of the problems because we do not have access to information about the military situation. We would go along with the administration's request in this regard. 

Chairman MORGAN. Your recommendation then is that we should stay within the limitation of last year's appropriation figure? 

Mr. TEETER. Yes.

Chairman MORGAN. This cut of $187 million should be spread by the Executive over those countries where you feel there are no acute problems.

Mr. TEETER. We felt it was not as important to increase fund levels for these countries as to keep funds at last year's level. Our policy statement reflects this position. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Teeter, on page 7, your last paragraph, "National chamber positions on 1966 aid recommendations": No. 1 refers to self-help measures to encourage emerging nations to solve their own problems.

When you come to No. 2 you recommend separation of economic assistance from military assistance. Is this any change in the chamber's position, or has this been your annual recommendation?

Mr. TEETER. This has been our annual recommendation, Mr. Chairman, as you may recall from previous testimony. We have felt that military assistance is one thing, and economic assistance is something different. They do come together to a certain degree when you have situations such as in Vietnam at the moment.

We felt economic assistance should be put in its own budget category and should stand on its own two feet for this reason. We know your committee reviews these matters every year, and of course reviews the military matters as well, but from the standpoint of budget categories, this has been the national chamber's recommendation, and

this is consistent, to specifically answer your question, with respect to our previous policy. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Teeter, this committee over a period of years has had strong feeling that both economic assistance and military assistance should be in a single package. Many Members of Congress believe that the economic part of the foreign assistance program over a period of years might be in serious trouble if it didn't have the military assistance running interference for it on the House floor. 

Does the chamber feel that there would be any danger of serious cuts in economic assistance if there is a separation? 

Mr. TEETER. I don't. It is the feeling of the chamber, and my own personal feeling as well, there wouldn't be any danger of serious cuts. 

I feel economic assistance is in a better position to stand on its own merits. Certainly programs going ahead throughout the worldand with greater emphasis which we hope will take place now on cooperation between Government and business-will strengthen the position of the economic assistance. 

If this takes place, I think the economic assistance program can pretty well stand on its own merit without having to be tagged onto the coattails of military assistance. I know we have discussed this problem before.

I really feel, and this is the feeling of the chamber, that economic and military assistance should each stand alone.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Teeter, during the discussions of the cham ber and on your meetings on foreign aid has there been enough consideration of this matter so that you can definitely tell the committee that most of your members feel a separation would in no way harm economic aid?

Mr. TEETER. That is true, sir. This was the very definite consensus of our last international committee meeting which was held this past January. I acted as chairman of this meeting. 

We did have representatives from all parts of the country and all phases of business activity. We specifically discussed this point, and this was the consensus of the meeting. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Teeter, let me refer to No. 3 on page 8 where you recommend a multiyear authorization of aid funds. Is this any change in the chamber's position? 

Mr. TEETER. Yes. We have been thinking about this for some while, particularly since the 5-year authorization period has been suggested. 

We feel an annual review is extremely important. As we pointed out here, we do not go along with the 5-year period, but rather the 4, which again would be subject to annual review so far as the appropriation is concerned.

We think this is consistent with what we in business would term good long-range planning. In business you certainly can't plan too well ahead one year at a time, particularly in a domestic or an international business or a combination of the two. 

So long-range planning I think we accept as all must have which will benefit us in the long run. review every year would be absolutely essential. on this. something that we 

Again, budgetary 

We would insist 

Chairman MORGAN. You still don't buy the 5-year- 60-929-66-pt. 4- -8

Mr. TEETER. No, we do not. We felt the 2-year review here, which would allow the program to be reviewed and revised, as I stated in my testimony, would be the appropriate thing. So we would then. make the authorization for 4 years.

Chairman MORGAN. I yield to Congressman Hays. 

Mr. HAYS. What you are advocating, Mr. Teeter, is that this committee abdicate its responsibility and turn the annual review over to the Appropriations Subcommittee, because I am afraid that is what would happen. 

Mr. TEETER. I would not advocate that. 

Mr. HAYS. That is what you are advocating if you want us to make a 5-year authorization. You don't think this committee is going to come back and hold hearings every year if we give them 5 years or if they are even going to come up and tell us about it? 

Mr. TEETER. To answer your first point, we did not go along with the 5-year authorization. 

Mr. HAYS. OK, 4 years then.

Mr. TEETER. That is a new policy statement this year of the U.S. chamber. It has been thought about for a considerable time. I would still feel that this committee and the Senate Foreign Relations Committee should review these matters as necessary, and every year so far as the actual appropriation of the funds is concerned. 

Mr. HAYS. If the chairman will yield further, Mr. Teeter, let me point out to you a fact of life. You don't see too many people here this morning, and we are here to authorize money now. If we had already given them money for 4 years, first, you wouldn't see any members of the committee here except the chairman at that annual review, because he is conscientious and would be here, and second, you wouldn't find anybody from the Departments up here, except about fourth-raters, because they would think to themselves, "Why should we bother with those fellows? We are done with them for 4 more years." 

Mr. TEETER. You make a very good point. I am not completely familiar with the workings of your various committees. I do feel that if this be the case, then there should be some change made whereby your committee would definitely have the right to review aid programs every year. 

I would feel very strongly on that. 

Mr. HAYS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Teeter, continuing on this multiyear authorization. On page 9, the last paragraph, where you say:

Such a long-term authorization would provide the continuity that is needed to establish effective aid programs. Authorizations should be extended to 4 years, instead of 5 years as recommended by the administration, to allow each administration 2 years to evaluate and revise, as necessary, foreign assistance programs 

What do you mean "to allow each administration 2 years to evaluate"? Do you mean each Congress?

Chairman MORGAN. Each Congress should? 

Mr. TEETER. Yes.

90th and 91st Congresses.

Mr. TEETER. Correct. This is more or less a check-and-balance review. The administration would also evaluate the aid program in a similar manner. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Teeter, I notice you don't mention any specific amounts of military assistance this year. You apparently accept the $917 million recommended by the administration for fiscal year 1967-generally the chamber recommended cuts in each section of the bill in previous years. This year your approach is a little bit different.

Mr. TEETER. That is correct, Mr. Chairman. We had up until last year, as you know, recommended specific cuts. In this instance the important factor is Vietnam, and support of our efforts in southeast Asia. 

We still feel that wherever possible cuts might be made in other areas of the world-such as in bolstering up some of the smaller Latin American armies, if this is still going on, and tightening up in Europe by having our allies bear more of the military expense in the European countries through NATO and so on. 

Because of the fact that we do not have access to specific records, particularly with what is going on with regard to southeast Asia, as you do, we did not feel qualified to make any recommendation on Vietnam other than to go along with the requested amount. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Teeter.

I have read the amendments that you have recommended rather hurriedly. I can assure you that when the markup of the bill starts, the committee will give consideration to your suggestions. 

Mr. TEETER. Thank you very much.

You are always rather a breath of fresh air.

I have just one. On page 2, "A small permanent advisory board shall be established." Who is going to establish it and how?

Mr. TEETER. There have been various committees that have made reports. Sometimes these reports are slow in coming in, for good reasons I am sure. The mechanics of this board would have to be worked out I believe

Mr. TEETER. We are recommending the establishment of this board.

There should be a high-level, permanent board established. It would continually review economic assistance and be available to consult with the Government insofar as aid matters are concerned. 

Mrs. BOLTON. It sounds excellent, but I didn't want it to drift in on some pink cloud.

Mr. TEETER. This is something we would like to discuss further with the appropriate people in the executive branch and it would be helpful if something like this might come to pass.
