Chairman MORGAN. You can have the additional time when your turn comes, Mr. Adair. 

Mr. Hays.

Mr. HAYS. I don't want to belabor this 4-year authorization too much. I do think we have a responsibility to make a case either for it or against it as to our views. 

The main resistance to the President's proposal here to lengthen the term of Congress to 4 years has been that the Congress ought to stay as close as possible to the people. The way you keep it close to the people, you have them run for reelection every 2 years. 

Your proposal is a little bit better, perhaps, than the administration's proposal, but they propose to take it away from two whole Congresses so they don't have any chance to do anything about it; that is, the Foreign Affairs Committee, the committee responsible.

Your proposal would take it away from one Congress. In other words, if we adopted your proposal, the Congress elected in November would have nothing to say about foreign aid, period, except through an Appropriations Subcommittee, the membership of which is not likely to change.

So consequently it seems to me, I am prepared, and I so stated, to go along with a 2-year authorization, but I am not prepared to sit here this year or any year and deny some future Congress the right to have the say on whether or not they authorize a foreign aid program, and how much they authorize. 

Mr. TEETER. I think you make a well-taken point. What we were trying to emphasize here was the continuity of this program which we feel is important. As I pointed out, in business we are very much concerned with long-range planning. This doesn't preclude the fact that each year there is a serious budgetary consideration. It is still my feeling that we would have to have the budgetary consideration every year, even though the authorization was there, before this could be approved and passed through these committees. 

It also should be looked at, I think, by the President himself, and specific recommendations should be made by him each year. 

Mr. HAYS. The problem you run into, Mr. Teeter, maybe you are not familiar with it, up here, is once you get an authorization the Appropriations Subcommittee feels duly bound to do something and to come within somewhere near the range of the amount authorized. 

It seems to me there is no bar to making plans, long-range plans. 

For example, I am chairman of the subcommittee that has jurisdiction over the foreign buildings program. We give them a 2-year authorization. But they are planning 5 or 6 years ahead. If they plan responsibly and reasonably they can be pretty sure they can come before this subcommittee, and whether Mrs. Bolton, Mr. Adair, and I will be here the next time they come nobody knows, but they feel pretty sure they can come before the subcommittee, whoever is on it, and if they have responsible plans, they will get their authorization.

I don't see any reason why the foreign aid people couldn't do the same, except they would like to be out from under this for 4 or 

5 years so they wouldn't have to come back.

That is in effect what it would amount to. I would think your group would want to take a pretty long look before you get too far out on a limb on this, because, one, it isn't going to pass, and two, I don't think you really want to advocate it if you examine it.

STATEMENT OF JAY H. CERF, MANAGER, INTERNATIONAL GROUP, CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES 

Mr. CERF. I would like to echo Mr. Teeter's comment that the chamber committee felt very strongly that accountability to the House committee is essential and should under no circumstances be reduced. I think as you did, Mr. Teeter. I would like to admit that our joint subcommittee was operating on a premise which may or may not be valid.

We would respect your judgment on this. I think we would want to bring this back to our committee. But the premise is clearly that the AID people would not be any the less concerned or to use your term, Mr. Hays, would not feel "out from under" when the Damoclesian sword of coming back in 2, 3, 4 years was hanging there.

Therefore as I say, I am not arguing this point. I do think I am fairly reflecting an assumption on the part of our people that they would come each year and feel mighty accountable to you knowing they would have to come back.

Mr. HAYS. I just don't think it would work that way, because most bureaucrats are here to stay. Congressmen will come and go, and they will tell you that pointblank on occasion. I can tell you from experience it is much easier to call down to AID now and get them on the phone than it is after the bill passes the House. 

They are not responsible in a 6-month period, so I don't know what causes you to think they would be on a 4-year basis. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Teeter and gentlemen, I want to add my voice in very strong support of the position that Mr. Hays and others have taken. I think we all recognize what you are trying to do, but I feel most strongly that what you recommend, if carried out to the letter, would not do that.

Just take this for example: it is the function of this committee to set the general policy and set dollar limitations within which as Mr. Hays points out the Appropriations Subcommittee operates. 

Suppose we are legislating this year in the context of the present situation in Vietnam and we draft legislation with that in mind. 

Certainly in the next 4 years there will be a vast change in the 

Vietnam situation. But yet you see you have your policy framework and your dollar levels, the dollar ceilings set in that context.

I just think that it would deny the flexibility which this program ought to have.

Again I say we see what you are driving at, but I think the conclusion was reached in the absence perhaps of a full consideration of the way the Congress actually operates. Further upon the long-

term planning we have said in this committee many times that we not only had no objection to it but in fact we strongly encouraged it.

I would point out that in previous testimony upon this bill there have been references made to plans running into the 1970's. So I think that for purposes of planning the AID organization does not in fact actually need this long-term authorization to which you have made reference. They can plan ahead. There is no reason why they shouldn't. 

The history of this program has shown that in spite of what some of us think it should be it is a continuing program. There is nothing in the past to indicate that they shouldn't plan ahead. They should, and to a considerable degree they have done long-term planning. 

I am very sure that no private business would want to pin itself down to a 4-year program which was pretty inflexible in the future. 

I would strongly urge that you take another look at this problem. 

When the national, the U.S. Chamber reaches conclusions of this kind, are they submitted to the various local chambers for comment in advance? In other words, does my chamber in Fort Wayne,

Ind., get a copy of your statement with a request for comment so they can give their views on it?

Mr. TEETER. The material is submitted, and there is an annual meeting coming up, an annual convention, which takes place this coming week, and all the delegates to that are completely advised, they know the basic policy which has been set by the chamber and they are able to vote on this policy at the annual meeting. 

I can tell you that there are many lively discussions, because with 50 States and many members, you know you can have some rather divergent views. 

Mr. ADAIR. Turning quickly to another matter, you have recommended that military assistance not be a part of the foreign aid bill-by that, I mean the economic bill; do I understand you correctly on that?

Mr. TEETER. Yes; this is consistent with our policy in which we tried to separate the two. Of course this was on a matter of accountability. 

Mr. ADAIR. Let me then ask you, and this is a serious question and one which we have to ask ourselves, do you think that military assistance as known in this program has a greater relationship to foreign policy or military operations?

My question concerns military assistance as in this program. 

Mr. TEETER. In this program I think of course there may be degrees of relationships, particularly when you think of Vietnam. I think the military assistance there is far more related to the military, the defense side. 

Mr. ADAIR. That has been taken out.

Mr. TEETER. Correct. But in general there is certainly a corelationship between the two. We have felt that any military assistance, any military aid given is really something that is under the Department of Defense and should be under their budget. 

Mrs. BOLTON. We have sometimes felt that the whole training of the military takes them out of a constantly reasonable aspect of foreign affairs. I hope you considered that too a little bit.

Mr. ADAIR. I would urge you in that same relationship to give further thought to this, that the military assistance as we speak of it

here, is more predominantly related to foreign policy than to military operations. If you would agree with my point of view upon that, then you might want to reconsider the conclusion that you reached that the two should be separated, because if it is related to foreign policy, then it seems to me that the basic responsibility would be in the State Department perhaps in conjunction with its AID organization.

Mr. TEETER. I would agree that all military policy is certainly part and parcel of our foreign policy, and that organizations such as NATO, SEATO, CENTO, and so forth are certainly within the framework.

Mr. ADAIR. I would point out again-I think we have recognized this, our willingness to see the Vietnam aid program now divorced from the general military assistance program. 

If I have one more minute, Mr. Chairman, on page 4 you recommend an amendment to the Foreign Assistance Act. Mr. Teeter, I confess 

I don't fully understand what you mean by that amendment.

Mr. TEETER. May I explain, Mr. Adair. We had discussed this at length. Let's use the phrase "joint ventures." There has been a great deal of emphasis on the value of joint ventures abroad. 

I think joint ventures do have a great deal of value, although there is a particular problem that those of us in business do run into sometimes in trying to find an appropriate partner to establish a joint venture within a particular country. 

This is particularly true in the developing nations. In the more developed countries it is essential that you do have a joint venture- France and Japan are specific examples of this. I don't think there should be any discrimination against a firm wishing to do business in a particular country.

In other words, what I am trying to say is, there should be no method of forcing him to really take a local partner, because sometimes they are just unavailable. Maybe an example would serve the point. In Nigeria we established a pharmaceutical operation, a plant on a small scale. There was really no one there that we could go into local partnership with.

When it came to agricultural products, the manufacture of feeds, there was a local man who had experience and background. This did become a joint venture which was established in Nigeria. There has been an undertone we have felt, and this has been felt by the members of our chamber committee at its meeting, this was rather deeply discussed in January, that sometimes pressure is brought to bear on business that it must really go into a joint venture. 

Mr. ADAIR. We are aware of those pressures and have ourselves observed them in many places. I would just ask if your committee in discussing this point took into account the developing attitudes in AID with respect to extended coverage under the guarantee program? 

Mr. TEETER. Yes; this was an element for consideration.

Mr. ADAIR. You are aware that there are some real efforts now being made in AID in this respect to further local participation? 

Mr. TEETER. Yes. We are quite aware of this. 

Mr. ADAIR. Finally, I would conclude by saying, as I am sure you also know, all of you gentlemen, that this committee has been quite friendly disposed to provisions in the foreign aid program to strengthen the participation of the private sector of the economy and

year after year we have done things which we thought would be helpful. sir.

Mr. TEETER. We are quite aware of that, and it is very important, Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Teeter, first I want to compliment you and the chamber for these careful statements, and the support you have been giving the foreign assistance program.

Secondly, I want you to know that the more I have learned about foreign aid and the problems of the developing world, the stronger I support the private sector development. I am becoming a passionate advocate of giving the people authority to solve their own problems, whether it be through the use of governmental units or private units, and through means which are as close to them as possible. 

I think the development of the strong private sector is one of the best ways to accomplish this. I am for you 100 percent in your interests along that line. 

Thirdly, I want to say that I am very grateful for your support of the long-term authorization. I have served on this committee I am in my fourth year, and I have a lot to learn. I know other members of this committee have had much more experience than I have, but the truth of the matter is that the hearings that we conduct on the Foreign Assistance Act every year of necessity are of a general nature. 

We deal in worldwide and regional terms. I can't understand foreign aid unless I get to the country level. It doesn't mean anything to me. I haven't the foggiest notion of whether it is working. 

We can't, as we sit here year after year, go to a country level when we are responsible for each new authorization. We have to look worldwide, and regionally, otherwise we would never get through the hearings.

I passionately hope that we will get a long-term authorization so that we can then begin to focus on a country level and actually see what is happening to the spectrum of foreign aid operations. In this respect I am sure I differ from some of my colleagues. 

I have some children who are soon going to be of draft age, and many of my constituents have children in Vietnam. I don't think it is any longer a luxury we can afford to go on in substantial ignorance of what is happening in these countries and looking at the tools that we have available to bring about changes there other than through the kind of violence that we are experiencing in Vietnam. 

As a matter of personal responsibility I have wanted to come to all of these hearings, but it is really difficult to learn much about what the program consists of. I am for your position 100 percent. This is my view.

I would now like to ask a question. The President of the World 

Bank recently made a statement some months back in which he pointed to the growing gap between the rich and the poor nations. You are familiar with this.

As you know, the United Nations has set a target of 1 percent of 

GNP as an aid level. What would you say about that? Because I notice you are talking about cuts, you are not talking about expanding the program to the levels to which it might be suggested by the 

President of the World Bank.

Mr. TEETER. I am aware of the comments you have just made, and the statement made in connection with the World Bank. I don't think there is any doubt in my mind, or the minds of any of us, that this foreign assistance problem requires a great deal of money. It is perhaps the proper utilization of that money that is the important thing.

Certainly far more money could be expended on foreign aid to build up the economy for the future-the economy of the world, I am talking about so that these nations could really emerge properly. 

Can we do it at this time, or do we have to do this gradually? Ithink we have to be perfectly sure these nations meet the proper criteria before we can expend any greatly expanded funds in these areas. 

Of course we can look back to the Marshall plan as an example. We spent a higher percentage of our gross national product on foreign aid for the Marshall plan, which was rebuilding a war-torn economy, something that existed which we knew could be put back together. 

Now it is a little bit different. We are getting into virgin fields and are trying to rebuild the country from the ground up, rather than something that did exist. Perhaps as time goes on we must have a larger foreign aid program. I would hope the details of this could be worked out.

We are working now within the framework of what has been requested by the President and the AID administration. I feel that the minor cut that we recommended here is more or less in line with our current thinking.

Looking to the future, I can't help but agree with you that an expanded foreign aid program is probably essential. We must be perfectly sure that this money will be properly used in the countries involved.

Mr. FRASER. The basic rationale for foreign aid is that we hope this will encourage countries to follow or move into the path of democratic practices to develop strength in democratic institutions. 

Do you think that we need to look and reexamine our assumption that economic aid automatically leads to that result? 

Mr. TEETER. Economic aid per se might not lead to that result. I think there is a great deal to be done in the education of the people in these various countries. Perhaps economic aid is a good steppingstone because, if you bring about the proper economic institutions which will hopefully improve social health and welfare, you do start to develop a nucleus of a good society which will bring about free political institutions and proper training as well. 

There is a tremendous need for well-trained individuals in Africa. 

In our own company we have extreme difficulty in trying to get the proper native personnel to staff our organization. There is a crying need for trained personnel in many of these countries where you have a few topflight politically trained individuals who are at the very top of the government, the weakness being in the civil servants who are pretty much inept, unfortunately. A great deal of training is needed along these lines.

Mr. FRASER. I gather you would agree we need to look beyond straight economic development to some of the things that may be prerequisite to this like training and education? 

Mr. TEETER. Yes.

Mr. FRASER. And perhaps be more concerned about the structure of the political and social institutions in these countries as well? 

Mr. TEETER. Yes. 

Mr. FRASER. I recognize the problems associated with that. 

Mr. TEETER. The problems in these countries can be entirely different. It would be difficult for us to, let's say, bring people here to train them under an American system of government and send them back and expect them to do a good job in their own country. This might help. Each country has to develop its own system. 

Mr. FRASER. Thank you very much.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Whalley. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Teeter, page 7, your suggestion of using local currencies seems to be a good idea. Do you have any idea how much the United States would benefit in balance of payments? 

Mr. TEETER. I can't specifically answer that question. I have not done specific research on that. 

Mr. CERF. I would rather ask Mr. Whalley. Do you know, sir? I think it is a terribly difficult question to answer. I know the problem of overuse of local currency causing internal inflation is a variable that has to be taken into account in using local currency. Theoretically this is measurable. I don't know the answer.

Mr. WHALLEY. Do you have any idea how many countries are getting aid? Do we have adequate local currencies that could be used for the purpose? Have you made a survey, or is this just an idea as to using local currency?

Mr. CERF. We have not made a specific survey. We have seen figures on local currencies. We know they are huge in the case of 

India, which is the area where it is most often proposed that local currency usage be enlarged.

We also know they are fairly large in some of the Latin American cases. I don't have those figures, sir. They are available-we have them in our office, as you know. I don't have them in front of me. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you very much.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Gross. 

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Teeter, I want to join with those of the committee who have spoken against the 4-year program, and suggest to you that there would probably be some unhappy people in your own company if you suspended your board of directors for a period of 4 years without any particular accounting to the stockholders, if you turned the affairs of your company over to the president and top echelon officials for a period of 4 years to run as they saw fit.

I don't believe you could sell that program to Congress. I assume that you personally and the chamber of commerce are concerned by the inflation, deficits, and the debt of this Government, are you not? 

Mr. TEETER. Very much so, sir.

Mr. GROSS. Yet you suggest putting this program on a 4-year basis. 

I would think this program ought to be phased out and done away with, Mr. Teeter. It is contributing substantially to the debt, deficit, and inflation. I think it is time that this program was cut back, not secured by a 4-year authorization on the part of this committee, but cut back and cut out.

This program was supposed to have been wiped out in 1952. You recall that, I assume? 

Mr. TEETER. I am well aware of that.

Mr. GROSS. Like Topsy, it has grown and grown. It is still with us, with approximately $7 billion unexpended in the pipeline and now an additional $2.6 billion being asked for in this bill. When are we ever going to get rid of this monster that we have on our backs in the form of the foreign handout program? Is the formula for foreign aid to start a war someplace? We fought and defeated Japan, Germany, Italy, and then expended billions of dollars on each of them, yet they, in turn are giving us little or no help in the matter of suppressing Communist aggression and financing around the world. 

What is this that we are undertaking here? How long is it going to be with us? Is it going to bankrupt this country, or contribute to the bankruptcy of this country?

I would like your philosophical viewpoint of this program. 

Mr. TEETER. I don't know how philosophical I can be, Mr. Gross, but let me go back a little bit. I don't think any of us like to be in a position of giving away money and feel it is going to bad use. To give a man on the street a 50-cent piece, and have him go into the nearest saloon, is not a very pleasant thing. 

I would hope this is not so much the case. Under the Marshall plan 

I think it was essential to rebuild the war-torn economies in Europe. 

I know in some ways that backfired, in that it strengthened the European economy and Europe perhaps has not assumed even today its full share responsibility so far as the underdeveloped world is concerned.

I feel, and the chamber has certainly recommended for a long time, that more of the share of the aid burden should be borne by countries that are able to do so. 

Mr. GROSS. You used the expression that "our allies" have not joined with us as they should. How do you propose to get them to do this? 

The witnesses come here from the State Department and the AID organization and say, "It is regrettable and it is unfortunate that they are not doing as they should in many, many ways." How do you bring them to act? How do you get anything out of them? 

Mr. TEETER. It is perhaps difficult. I know that France, in spite of some of the other problems we do have with them, they are giving a certain amount of foreign aid. I do not know the exact figures. 

Certainly with respect to the African countries, France has been doing some work down there. Germany has been involved in aid. 

I would like to see more of this. In our policy statements here in our own deliberations on foreign aid we have made such strong recommendations. 

How it is accomplished is something which must take place, I am sure, at a high level. I would hope our top administration-let's talk of the President, the State Department-could convince the other important powers of the world of their responsibility insofar as foreign aid is concerned. The United States should certainly not bear the entire brunt and the burden of this. 

Mr. GROSS. It is or is not?

Mr. TEETER. It should not bear the entire burden. We are bearing a great share of it. Consistently the chamber has recommended over

the years a reduction in foreign aid so that it could be quite realistic. 

I am not unmindful of what Mr. Fraser said a few moments ago. I think again, to just go back to that point, that development assistance is a world responsibility insofar as aid is concerned. I think that the entire developed world must assume the responsibility of aid to the underdeveloped nations so they can achieve a degree of development to make them important nations of the world and so they can improve their education, health conditions, sanitation, and so forth. 

I think this is quite important. Again, we have been consistent in our cutting of foreign aid funds. To be very realistic, the foreign aid bill, when I first testified on it, was up in the range of $4.8 billion. 

This was shortly before the Clay Committee was appointed. 

It was cut back to $42 billion. I had a very definite feeling at that time as did other members of the chamber, that this figure was entirely unrealistic. When the bill was cut below that, the fact of the matter was borne out the figure was unrealistic. Consistently we have been trying to pare this thing down and get it more oriented toward economic development through the business community. 

Mr. GROSS. I appreciate your past recommendations for cutting the program, and you are making the recommendation again to cut this bill. As far as I am concerned, a cut of $187 million doesn't go far enough. This program, as far as I am concerned, is not producing results around the world. The fact that the military assistance program has to be, by the statement of the chairman of this committee, tied to this in order to carry the economic program is proof positive that the economic program won't stand on its own feet. It hasn't, and it won't.

Mr. TEETER. Sir, I feel that the economic program can stand on its own merits if it is properly handled and properly developed. Dr. 

Cerf had one comment.

Mr. CERF. Again, Mr. Chairman, I wanted to emphasize in another way, Mr. Gross, something Mr. Teeter had said. That the national chamber shares more of your concern than might be suggested, and in particular, Mr. Gross, the question of what sometimes is called burden sharing among our sister developed countries in the foreign aid field.

I thought, sir, it might be of some interest to the committee to point out that the Atlantic Council of the United States, whose Honorary 

Chairmen are Presidents Truman and Eisenhower, and the Atlantic

Institute, whose chief is General Norstad, are receiving great support from the national chamber right now in a specific effort that it is making the Council is to have its next meeting in a few weeks in Geneva-to address some of these very problems, about business cooperation in the Atlantic community in the directions, Mr. Gross, that you are talking about, and getting the governments of these countries to come in in a more coordinated fashion and strengthening the 

Development Assistance Committee which is an annex of the OECD 

whose very purpose-I don't think it has accomplished its purposeshould be to get more countries to come in together in sharing these burdens.

Mr. GROSS. My only comment, and then I am through, Mr. Chairman--my only comment is that we have had 18 years of talk, and not much action. The trickle-down process has not worked. This money

has not reached the people. We have spent $130 billion in this program in one way or another, and we are in more trouble than we have ever been around the world.

Mr. TEETER. To back up what Mr. Cerf said, the chamber policy is that the aid task must be justly shared. The United States should continue to enlist greater cooperation from other industrialized nations in assisting underdeveloped countries. 

This bears out what Mr. Cerf has said. I admit, Mr. Gross, this is a difficult situation. We should keep pushing for it and certainly the national chamber will do so. 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Any further questions? 

Mr. FRASER. I just wanted to make a comment that I think there is no question but what we need to get other free world countries to help in aid. As you pointed out, France is devoting a larger share of its GNP to aid than we are. I think the Western European countries as a group are contributing about the same portion of their gross national product to foreign aid as is the United States. Of course, they have less money, on a per capita basis, so in some respects they are doing more than we are. This is my understanding. The AID has a chart here showing about a dozen countries who are contributing, our part being about 60 percent. 

My guess is that would be about the proportion of our GNP bears to the total of those contributing countries. 

Mr. CERF. They define the terms differently.

Mr. FRASER. On aid- 

Mr. CERF. What we would call a relatively high interest loan, development loan by Germany, we would not in our dictionary call that foreign aid. The Germans might. 

Mr. FRASER. Except that I think the DAC of the OECD has been working on this. They exclude, for example, loans that are 5 years, which would tend to be more of the short-term commercial loans. 

Mr. GROSS. Why not use net national income rather than gross national product? Mr. Teeter's company doesn't deal in gross national product. When his company issues a financial statement it is on the basis of net income for the year. I don't buy this phony gross national product business.

Mr. FRASER. After I have been around as long as you have, I will become that sophisticated.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, gentlemen. 

The committee stands adjourned until 10 o'clock tomorrow morning. (Whereupon, at 12:02 p.m., the committee adjourned, to reconvene at 10 a.m., Thursday, April 28, 1966.) (The following statement has been submitted for inclusion in the record :) 

[Western Union Telegram] DENVER, COLO., May 2, 1966. 

Hon. THOMAS E. MORGAN, Chairman, Foreign Affairs Committee, House of Representatives, Washington, D.C.: 

The League of Women Voters of the United States continues it vigorous support of economic aid to the developing countries. We believe the request for a 

5-year authorization which is consistent with the long-term prices of economic

development, would enable your committee to concentrate more deeply on programs and policies.

As your committee well knows, league support has focused on development assistance both capital and technical. Separate consideration by Congress of economic assistance would in our view clarify for the public the rationale for development aid. We welcome the President's new initiatives in agriculture, education, and health, and greater emphasis by AID in these fields. We believe these initiatives can revitalize our assistance programs. But renewed emphasis on technical assistance should not be made at the expense of needed capital assistance. We're increasingly concerned with "barebones" requests for economic aid.

There is mounting evidence that greater amounts of assistance could be absorbed in many of the developing countries. The need for economic growth in the developing world is so great and the moral obligation of the "haves" to help the "havenots" is so pressing that we believe that the time has come for an enlargement of U.S. efforts, along with those of other prosperous countries. 

We should now set an example for the world toward stepping up development assistance.

In view of the minimum nature of the request, we urge your committee to authorize the full amounts requested for development loans, technical cooperation, the Alliance for Progress, and contributions to international organizations. 

We regret that this wire must substitute for our testifying before your committee. Our entire board is in Denver for our national convention. 

Mrs. ROBERT J. STUART, President, League of Women Voters of the United States.

THURSDAY, APRIL 28, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:15 a.m., in room 

2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

The committee meets this morning in open session for a continuation of the hearings on the foreign aid bills, H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450.

Our first witness this morning is Mrs. A. G. Patterson, national chairman of the Committee on Foreign Relief of the National Council of Catholic Women.

STATEMENT OF MRS. A. G. PATTERSON, NATIONAL CHAIRMAN OF THE COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN RELIEF OF THE NATIONAL COUN- CIL OF CATHOLIC WOMEN Mrs. PATTERSON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I am Mrs. A. G. Patterson, of Austin, Minn., national chairman of the Committee on Foreign Relief of the National Council of Catholic Women.

It is a privilege for me to come before this committee to speak in support of H.R. 12449, the proposed legislation amending the Foreign 

Assistance Act of 1961, because of my organization's interest and participation in the international programs. 

At our 32d national convention, held here in the Nation's Capital in 

November 1964, we adopted the following resolution:

We see in the foreign relief program of the National Council of Catholic Women an opportunity to implement this hope of the late Pope John XXIII. 

Therefore, we will continue and extend our efforts to collect funds and clothing for the relief of the sufferings of the poor in new and old nations of the world, the needs of refugees from civil strife and communism, and the vast throngs of responsible men and women who look to us to help them to help themselves. 

This is a quotation of Pope John XXIII, allocution, January 16, 1961: 

We would like to hope-and in our prayers we ask this favor from God-that once the legitimate aspirations of people for liberty and independence have been satisfied, the richer will aid the poorer, the stronger support the weaker, the more advanced hold out a helping hand to the less developed, and in the end that all will feel themselves brothers, for all are sons of the same loving Father who is in Heaven.

The National Council of Catholic Women is a federation of 14,000 organizations of Catholic women throughout the United States and 693

American military installations abroad, with a total membership of about 10 million women. Our programs are carried on by a small staff in Washington and thousands of volunteers across this country. 

The foreign relief program was formulated in the years immediately following World War II to assist the victims of war in Europe. Funds and clothing collected by our women were allocated and distributed by War Relief Services, NCWC, now Catholic Relief Services, NCWC, the agency of American Catholics formed to bring relief to all in need, regardless of race, color, or creed.

In his message of February 1, 1966, to the Congress of the United States, President Johnson refers to our economic assistance programs in this manner:

They are the proof of our devotion to the works of peace. 

Pope Paul VI, in his historic address to the United Nations on 

October 4, 1965, pointed out thatpeace *** is built with the spirit, with ideas, with works of peace. 

There is no word in the English language-or any language for that matter so precious to a woman as the word "peace."

We also have been fully aware and justly proud of the fact that our Government's economic assistance programs since 1945 have been a major factor in the preservation of peace in many areas of the world, and they must continue.

President Johnson has said, in his message already referred to: 

In this spirit of cooperation, I propose that the United States offer to join in new attacks upon the root causes of world poverty. 

The incessant cycle of hunger, ignorance, and disease is the common blight of the developing world. This vicious pattern can be broken. It must be broken if democracy is to survive.

At the present time our efforts to aid the poor in the developing nations include medical care and training in child care and nutrition, provided for poor mothers at maternity clinics in about 80 countries, local food packages for poor families, support of orphans and children in poor families in Korea, training for young girls of refugee families in Hong Kong, scholarships for young girls in secondary schools in Africa, one of many self-help programs throughout the world, and, of course, constant collection of new and used clothing for children. 

This is the kind of cooperation between voluntary groups, our Government's vast resources, and the multilateral programs of the United Nations which is needed to overcome the problems of mankind in this technological age.

But we feel that the goals for which all of us are striving can be achieved only through well developed programs, which in many instances will require a long-term commitment. Therefore, we fully support the President's request for a 5-year authorization of our economic assistance programs at a figure no less than he has requested. 

In closing, let me say that the foreign aid program of the United States is the conscience of the American people, our response to the amazing generosity of a loving Father who sees us as the custodians of His great gifts with which we may feed His poor, clothe His naked ones, heal His sick, house His homeless, educate His illiterate, so that the glorious world He has created for all His creatures may survive in peace. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mrs. Patterson.

Mrs. Patterson, according to your statement here you indicate that your organization is a federation of 14,000 organizations with a total membership of about 10 million women.

How much of a campaign do you carry on among these 10 million people to acquaint them with the foreign aid program? 

Mrs. PATTERSON. For our foreign relief work? We have a constant campaign. We are divided into dioceses, parishes, small groups, and while I am leading the committee at the national level, I have constant communication with my counterparts on the other levels, sending them material, giving them information of the needs, telling them what is pertinent to our program in, for instance at Vietnam, at the moment, or India at the moment, and asking for their help. 

Chairman MORGAN. Do you send out any literature to your organizations on the economic portion of this bill? 

Mrs. PATTERSON. Yes, sir. We have been keeping our members constantly in contact with what has been going on in Washington; we have been sending information to them telling them what is going on here in Washington and asking their support, asking them to write their Congressman, if you will, and keeping them up to date on the bill. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mrs. Patterson. 

Mr. Gross? 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mrs. Patterson, you speak of peace being precious to a woman. I am sure that peace is precious to all citizens, including women. I am sure "peace" is a welcome word. It would certainly be welcomed by the servicemen now fighting in Vietnam.

We have spent $130 billion on foreign aid since the end of World War II and still we have no peace. Doesn't it seem to you and to your organization that the mere fact of spending billions of dollars on foreign aid, and this is a multibillion dollar bill you are endorsing here today, has not brought about peace in the world or anything approaching peace. Does this concern you? 

Mrs. PATTERSON. What would we do without it? What would we have done if we hadn't spent this money? Where would the world be today? Aren't we making some steps toward peace?

Mr. GROSS. We fought a long war in Korea with 35,000 men dead. 

We have lost close to 3,000 men in Vietnam and I don't know how many wounded; I suppose 15,000. We don't have peace today, despite all the protestations of President Johnson and others.

Mrs. PATTERSON. We have peace in some parts of the world, Mr. 

Congressman. South Korea is free. 

Mr. GROSS. No; South Korea is still under a military armistice. 

There isn't even a peace treaty signed in South Korea. 

Mrs. PATTERSON. We are not fighting there. People are not dying.

Mr. GROSS. We have a considerable number of troops manning the lines in South Korea at terrific expense to our taxpayers. Are you not at all concerned with the debt, deficit, and inflation that is causing grave concern in this country? Are you not concerned by the unconscionable burden of debt that is being heaped upon the generations to come? Has your organization given consideration to this as well as a questionable peace? 

Mrs. PATTERSON. I am very aware of it. I am a taxpayer myself.

I know probably in my city, in my State, the taxes per capita are about as high as they are anywhere in the country. 60-929-66-pt. 4- -9

Are we going to let all these people go by the board? Are we going to forget that anyone else lives in the world? Are we going to

Mr. GROSS. The fact is that there is no proof that by a continuation of the foreign aid program you are going to avert war. 

Mrs. PATTERSON. I feel by the education of these people-civilization has passed millions of these people by for years and years-I feel that by educating them, making them self-sufficient, giving them a dignity, something to live for, that we can make a better life for them and make it possible for them to run their countries which they are just taking over and are for the most part in no condition to run from an educational standpoint.

We have got to start somewhere. I have found in my experiencego around begging, telling people of the needs for these people in other parts of the world that some people say to me, "Yes; but we have a lot of poor people in our country, too, whom we should be helping." We are all members of the same family, no matter what we think, and I feel that we have a responsibility to help everyone in the world.

We can't sit here with all the wealth in the world and feel we have it made, that we are going to stop paying taxes and let the rest of the world go. If you are not thinking of it from a moral standpoint, then 

I think you must think of it from a standpoint of security for the 

United States. 

Peace you say can't be gained by dollars, but I am afraid we disagree, Mr. Congressman. I feel that by helping these people to help themselves, that we can put them on their feet and we can make a better world. It has been proven in places where we have been working.

Mr. GROSS. I don't want to prolong this, but I would like a bill of specifics wherein it has been proven that foreign aid has rescued any country.

Mrs. PATTERSON. It has been proven that foreign aid will educate people, train them to become self-sufficient, train them, educate them so that they will know- 

Mr. GROSS. I don't want to prolong this, Mrs. Patterson, but I hope you realize, and members of your organization realize, we have more debt-you speak of the prosperity of this country-we have more debt than the rest of the world combined. I hope you give some attention to the ability of the United States to police and finance the world in your consideration of the promotion of the continuation of foreign aid.

Mrs. PATTERSON. I believe we have given consideration to that and we will in the future.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Rosenthal. 

Mr. ROSENTHAL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mrs. Patterson, I want to commend you first for your statement, with which I wholeheartedly agree, and probably more important for the time and effort and attention that you have given to this matter. 

I want to tell you as far as I am concerned I am extremely grateful that you have taken the time to come here and appear before the committee.

In my judgment the most useful thing citizen groups can do in a democratic society is to appear before congressional committees and

Sometimes we find make their voice known and their views heard. a sterile atmosphere. We only hear from one side-the administration people or others in Government. 

My own judgment is that in order to have a democratic foreign policy it has to be democratically made, and what you are doing is participating in the actual workings of democracy by appearing here. 

So the only comment I want to make, Mr. Chairman, is to express to 

Mrs. Patterson and to the National Council of Catholic Women, my deep appreciation for the great attention they have given to this subject, and for the time they have taken from their own personal commitments, which I know are many, to appear here. 

Mrs. PATTERSON. Thank you very much.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fulton. 

Mr. FULTON. I would like to compliment you as well as your or ganization on an excellent statement. It is people such as you, your officers, and members, who make the service in public life worth while by backing these good causes and saying that peace is people, and that we must be our neighbor's keeper, rather than be our dollar's keeper.

It is a very hard position to take, both for you and your organization as well as people in public life. As a matter of fact, the ladies that you represent, 10 million of them, are the average American taxpayer. Most of them aren't rich. Most of them have families, responsibilities, and obligations, and when they pay the taxes this often deprives taxpayer's families at home.

For your women generally, this is something of a sacrifice that they take the responsibility for these people in other countries, in other lands who are either oppressed, starving, without clothes, and without any education whatever. 

Mrs. PATTERSON. I am very proud of them and I have found that there is no one more generous than the American woman and/or the American man; even after they have paid taxes, they will dig down in their pocket and give us the money to add to what the Government has given to foreign aid.

Mr. FULTON. This is in addition to the charitable contributions of the good Catholic women who are members of your organization, to their local parish, to the diocese, and to the National Catholic Welfare 

Organizations. That is right, is it not?

Mrs. PATTERSON. That is right. 

Mr. FULTON. I believe that your statement of policy should be commented on particularly because it strikes me as being a general approach to peace that we should all adopt whether we are in public life or private life, and in citizens' voluntary organizations as well. 

I like particularly the statement on page 2 of your statement:

Pope Paul VI in his historic address to the United Nations on October 4, 1965, pointed out that "Peace is built with a spirit, with ideas, with works of peace." 

I believe that Pope Paul VI, by his journey, gave the United Nations a new lease on life. His address helped raise the spirit of the 

United Nations so that the nations will look on the long-term view, and work for the basic progress of mankind. 

So I believe from the point of view of the Catholic Church and its members and the United States, as well as we U.S. citizens of sister 

Christian churches, it was wonderful to have Pope Paul VI come,

make the trip, and make this presentation before the United Nations; would you agree with that?

Mrs. PATTERSON. It was a thrilling day. 

Mr. FULTON. I like, also, the statement on page 1 of Pope John

XXIII in his allocution of January 16, 1961. With the chairman's permission, I would like to insert that in the record at this point. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection. 

(The quotation follows:) QUOTATION OF POPE JOHN XXIII, ALLOCUTION, JANUARY 16, 1961 

We would like to hope-and in our prayers we ask this favor from God-that once the legitimate aspirations of people for liberty and independence have been satisfied, the richer will aid the poorer, the stronger support the weaker, the more advanced hold out a helping hand to the less developed, and in the end that all will feel themselves brothers, for all are sons of the same loving Father who is in Heaven.

Mr. FULTON. Do you feel that is a statement of basic policy that you recommended the Government as well should adopt? Your good organization has adopted it, your members have, and the church has. 

Mrs. PATTERSON. I am here for that very reason, Mr. Congressman, urging that our Government do just this. 

Mr. FULTON. With the chairman's permission I would like to have something of the budget of your organization and your activities in various parts of the world put in the record so we can have this information for the public interest. Would you supply such a statement? (The following has been supplied for the record:)

Hon. THOMAS E. MORGAN, NATIONAL COUNCIL OF CATHOLIC WOMEN, Chairman, Foreign Affairs Committee, 

House of Representatives, Washington, D.C. 

Washington, D.C., May 5, 1966.

DEAR CONGRESSMAN MORGAN: When Mrs. A. G. Patterson, of NCCW, testi fied before your committee last week in favor of H.R. 12449, Congressman Fulton requested that information on the extent of our overseas program be incorporated in the record. Mrs. Patterson has requested me to make this information available to the committee.

For the calendar year 1965, contributions to the Madonna plan totaled $33,578.47.

The Madonna plan is a project by which funds are made available to maternity clinics for medical care and training in nutrition and child care for needy mothers. In some instances, salaries of doctors and nurses are paid from the funds, and equipment vital to the preservation of health are purchased. 

Help-a-family funds totaled $19,206.52.

The help-a-family program provides for the purchase of local food packages for poor families, and frequently for providing tools and/or household equipment which will improve a family's condition. 

During international cooperation year, the Madonna plan and the help-afamily program were expanded to put emphasis on self-help; scholarships in secondary education in Africa are provided for girls in Africa, and training in home economics, secretarial skills and tailoring are given to teenage girls, daughters of refugees in Hong Kong.

The help-a-child program provided support for orphans and children in poor families in Korea to the extent of $105,694.33. These funds are the total of monthly contributions of $10 a month from American sponsors for these children.

A total of $2,158.04 in cash was contributed for the purchase of new garments for infants and children under a project known as the Holy Father's collection

for children. Many of the garments are dispatched to needy children from a storeroom in Vatican City.

Operation Hong Kong provided $976 for women refugees in Hong Kong who make items which we have sold for them-dolls, bags, wool stoles. 

Cash contributions to designated individuals overseas, frequently priests and sisters working with the poor in the developing nations, totaled $406.95. 

The total cash contributions received by NCCW from January 1 to December 

31, 1965, was $162,020.31.

Gifts of new clothing totaled 56,477 pounds. Gifts of used clothing for children totaled 143,663 pounds. 

NCCW has no staff overseas.

The funds and clothing contributed by members of our federation are allocated and distributed by Catholic Relief Services, NCWC. Enclosed for your information is the most recent report of CRS on the places where the funds were allocated and the projects which received them. 

This report is for fiscal 1965. 

We take this opportunity to express our deep gratitude to the committee for permitting NCCW to make known its position on foreign assistance. 

Very sincerely,

Mrs. MARGARET M. ZEMO, Foreign Relief Secretary. 

ANNUAL REPORT: FOREIGN RELIEF COMMITTEE, NATIONAL COUNCIL OF CATHOLIC WOMEN 

(Fiscal year 1964–65) 

1. MADONNA PLAN 

During the past year, the names of three leaders of the National Council of Catholic Women began to be known by women in farflung areas of the world. 

In Meru, Kenya, Gartner Hall was under construction as a Catholic action center. The funds for its construction came from madonna plan contributions of the Winona Council of Catholic Women, in honor of Mrs. Ludwig Gartner, past president of the Winona Council of Catholic Women, and second vice president of the National Council of Catholic Women National Board When Mrs. Gartner died, it was decided to memorialize her contribution to Catholic action by aiding women's Catholic Action in Africa.

In nearby Tanzania, the number of Mary Hannan Mahoney Memorial Clinics came to seven. These clinics were begun with a Madonna plan grant made by the National Council of Catholic Women affiliates after Mrs. Robert Hugh 

Mahoney, national president, died. It was under Mrs. Mahoney's presidency that the Madonna plan was inaugurated in 1958. 

In San Salvador, capital of El Salvador, Central America, the Elizabeth Ann Zepf Madonna shelter is carrying on its services from a new site after the building was hit in the May 1965 earthquake. The Madonna shelter honors a woman who is still living and who is still making a great contribution to Catholic action, after having served as foreign relief chairman and as president of the 

National Council of Catholic Women. 

The funds for the running of the shelter are raised by the Toledo Council of 

Catholic Women. 

These three overseas centers are proof of the personalized charity of the National Council of Catholic Women offiliates exercised through the Madonna plan. The Madonna plan is one of the six specific projects within the overall program of the National Council of Catholic Women's Foreign Relief Committee. These projects are conducted in cooperation with Catholic Relief 

Services-NCWC. A foreign relief secretary, Mrs. Margaret Zemo, in the National Council of Catholic Women headquarters, and a consultant, Miss Eileen Egan in the Catholic Relief Services-NCWC headquarters, work out the details of preparing and promoting the various projects. Cooperating with them are a foreign relief chairman, Mrs. A. G. Patterson of the diocese of Winona, and 

Mrs. Anthony Mazzarelli of the diocese of Grand Rapids. Both are volunteers. 

Among the directly sponsored projects of the Madonna plan during the past year were: The St. Columban Clinic at the 38th parallel in Korea was granted $200 monthly for the fifth consecutive year by the women of the St. Paul Archdiocesan Council of Catholic Women through their vanishing luncheon project (the St. Paul Archdiocesan Council of Catholic Women also helped for the fifth consecutive year our Fatima Clinic in Trivandrum, South India); the supply of

Dryco milk formula for the St. Martha's Clinic in Vatican City (serving mothers in the slums of Rome) was shipped for the seventh consecutive year. 

Other grants made from the general madonna plan collection were made to the following countries through the office of Catholic Relief Services-NCWC: 

Brazil.-Eight grants for a variety of programs including the purchase of sewing machines for homecraft and other courses, the provision of medicaments for mother and child clinics, the provision of salaries for doctors and nurses for slum areas and the supplying of necessary kits for two radio schools helping illiterates.

Colombia.-A grant was made to Radio Sutatenza, otherwise known as Popular 

Cultural Action, to cover the costs of literacy kits, including transistor radios, blackboards, and other supplies, so that 40 more small villages in the Andes Mountains may be included in the radio school system. 

Chile. A grant went to the Nazareth Medical Center for the purchase of a refrigerator for medical supplies, and an electric stove. 

Guatemala.-Four grants went to mother and child clinics in four areas: La 

Caridad, Peten; San Juan Ostuncalco; San Amtonia; and Jocotan.

Haiti.-Two grants to the Salesian Fathers and one grant to the Salesian 

Sisters to provide extra nutrition, and training for mothers and children. 

India.-Seven grants to mother and child medical and training programs conducted in Bombay, Calcutta, Kaipuzha, Kerala, Madras, Kashmir, and Lucknow.

Korea.-Two grants for rehabilitation and vocational courses for Korean girls and women in rehabilitation centers in Taegu and Nazareth Vocational Training 

Schools in Seoul.

Mexico. A grant for medicines and food supplement for mothers and children in Mexico City.

Pakistan.-Four grants for medical aid and training programs under the direction of various religious orders in Muzzafagarh, Bahawalpur, and Sargodha (West Pakistan), and to the St. Mary's Maternity Hospital, Mymensingh (East Pakistan).

Philippines.-A grant to the Heart of Mary Villa in Rizal, Manila, to help renovate quarters for poor mothers. 

St. Vincent, B.W.I.-A grant to St. Benedict's Day Nursery conducted by 

Rev. Roland Roberts.

Taiwan. A grant went to Our Lady of Maryknoll Clinic to help with the antihookworm program among needy Taiwanese. 

Ceylon. On special recommendation of Msgr. Joseph J. Harnett, Far East director of Catholic Relief Services-NCWC, five Madonna plan grants were made to five projects despite the fact that Catholic Relief Services-NCWC has no program in those areas. The grants went to the Blessed Martin's Home in 

Peredaniya; St. Bernard's Home, Fatima Canteen, St. Vincent's Home and 

Prithipura Homes (all in Colombo), to aid in the various welfare and training programs.

Contributions to Madonna plan during the past year, $35,218. world. 

2. HELP A FAMILY 

Help-a-family grants met a great variety of need among families around the During the past year, efforts were made to stress the freedom-fromhunger campaign sponsored by the United Nations. One freedom-from-hunger grant was made to Thailand in order to help the Thabom Rice Bank Cooperative, conducted by Rev. Harry Thiel, Redemptorist missionary, concerned with the social betterment of his area. A rice bank was constructed which will allow the villagers to store rice against the lean preharvest season when rice prices are high and poor families often go into debt. Another grant to Thailand went to a family aid and rehabilitation program under Rev. Anthony Terkowitsch of the 

Holy Redemption Church. 

A grant went to the Young Christian Workers training program in Seoul, Korea. Rev. Francis Park, moderator of the Young Christian Workers has instituted a broad marriage training and family counseling program so that young Koreans (very often recent converts) can learn the deeper meaning of 

Christian marriage.

Help-a-family grants were used by local agencies to meet particular problems of the area. The rehabilitation of families through the Suicide Prevention 

Center of Seoul, Korea, was helped with a continued grant. 

Other grants went to Brazil, Haiti, India, Pakistan, Peru, and Tanzania. 

Contributions to help-a-family during the past year, $22,047.

3. HELP A CHILD 

The help-a-child program by which American families sponsor individual Korean children was featured in the Christmas column of Katherine Burton in 

Sign magazine. As a result of Mrs. Burton's column, entitled "A Christmas

Challenge for the Coming Year" several hundred additional children were sponsored at the rate of $10 monthly.

The new emphasis of the help-a-child program is in help to children in the poorest of poor families. The monthly contribution allows the family to pay its rent and also to send one or two of the children to primary school. 

The cost of the primary school is often $2 monthly. Many hundreds of the most deprived children of Korea are able to learn the tools of the intellect through sponsorships in the help-a-child program. A paragraph from Katherine Burton's column gives a good picture of the program: "For many people, $10 a month for a year is a considerable sum, but if it is divided among a group, it will cost each one no more than the Madonna dollar for which my readers collected so generously for Africa and Brazil. 

The givers of this help-a-child work will receive a report every 3 months from the one distributing the funds and also a picture of the child and letters from him and from them. A religious who operates an orphanage there wrote, "The two boys read your letters again and again and proudly show them everywhere.' A Korean sister writes: 'All the time the mother exerts herself for her children to make a living without hunger and sends deep thanks to you.' And Father

Pak wrote to the members of St. Anne's Society in the States that their adopted girl, Bernadette, had a sad and pessimistic nature (she is 12) but that she 'became happy with frequent smiles since she had adoptive parents in America.'" 

Contributions to help-a-child during the past year, $83,137. 

4. OPERATION HONG KONG 

Rehabilitation efforts on behalf of Hong Kong refugees included a grant to the Casa Ricci on Macau for the rehabilitation of mothers and children who had recently escaped from the China mainland. The stoles made by the Pius Handicraft Center were sold at the National Council of Catholic Women National Convention in Washington and at various conventions throughout the country. 

In response to an urgent plea, Madonna plan grant was made for the Hong Kong Catholic Marriage Counseling Service. This new welfare service has been made very necessary by the fact that there are large numbers of new Catholics in Hong Kong and also by the fact that so many other refugees are living in abnormal circumstances. 

5. CHILDREN IN NEED 

For 20 years the National Council of Catholic Women affiliates throughout the country have sent wearable garments to the Catholic Relief Services-NCWC warehouse in Brooklyn throughout the year, as well as during the Thanksgiving clothing campaign. Women's groups all over the country have been constant in their fidelity to the children-in-need collection.

During the past year, 46,321 pounds of good clothing was sent to the warehouse. 

The value of the overall donation was $69,481.50. 

6. PAPAL COLLECTION FOR CHILDREN 

During the past year, the donation of layettes and new garments in the papal collection for children reached a total of 59,499 pounds. First communion outfits for boys and girls came to 1,978 pounds. Half of these donations were shipped directly to the Holy Father's storerooms in Vatican City. They were then distributed to needy institutions in Italy and in Europe on the basis of requests to the Vatican. The remaining half of the donations was sent around the world in the name of His Holiness. The garments were distributed through welfare centers, hospitals, dispensaries, and especially through institutions aided by the 

National Council of Catholic Women Madonna plan. 

Shipments of new garments were made to the following countries: Brazil, Bolivia, Colombia, Chile, Dominican Republic, El Salvador, Guatemala, Peru, Paraguay, and Venezuela.

In earlier years all first communion outfits were sent to Latin America to be used in connection with the welfare work of parishes in slum areas. During the past year, 500 first communion outfits were sent to Cebu City, Philippines, M

on the basis of a special request from the bishop of Cebu City. The outfits were needed in connection with a great religious celebration commemorating the implanting of catholicism by Spanish missionaries in the Philippine Islands. 

Value of new garments and first communion outfits, $184,431. 

7. ARCHDIOCESAN COUNCIL OF FOREIGN RELIEF OF PHILADELPHIA 

The Committee for Foreign Relief, under the chairmanship of Mrs. Mary T. 

Lowery is an affiliate of the National Council of Catholic Women. 

For many years, the committee has specialized in helping Cloistered Orders of Sisters around the world. The committee has also sponsored a needy diocese in Taiwan. 

All donated items are packed for export by the committee in a center maintained by the committee in Philadelphia. During the past year, 524 cases of relief items weighing 17,350 pounds were shipped through the facilities of Catholic Relief Services-NCWC to destinations in Europe, Asia, and Latin America. In response to specific requests, 1,000 pounds of host flour and 220 Carmelite habits were donated. The sum of $9,497.17 was donated for the purchase of crates and other shipping supplies. 

Estimated value of overall donations, $234,700. 

8. CATHOLIC DAUGHTERS OF AMERICA 

The Catholic Daughters of America, one of the largest national affiliates of the National Council of Catholic Women, sponsors several relief projects in cooperation with Catholic Relief Services-NCWC. The overseas relief effort of the 

Catholic Daughters of America is called Relief for Peace and is directed by Mrs. Manila Caprine, supreme secretary of the organization, and chairman on 

Relief for Peace. Mrs. Caprine works closely with Miss Margaret Buckley, supreme regent of the organization.

During the past year, the special program of the Catholic Daughters of America on behalf of children born to leper parents in South Korea was organized to meet changing needs. The institution maintained for untainted children of leper parents by Archbishop Harold Henry of Kwangju has been taken over by the Korean Government authorities. Some of the children have been placed in other Catholic orphanages or in private families. The various courts of the 

Catholic Daughters of America had been sponsoring individual children in the institution. The board of directors of the Catholic Daughters of America de cided to continue their aid to needy children under the care of Archbishop Harold Henry, whether these children were in orphanages or in private families. 

Other projects of the Catholic Daughters of America include help to individual families in Korea and grants-in-aid to needy priests in Vietnam. Special grants were made at the biennial convention of the Catholic Daughters of America in Florida in July 1964. These grants were made under the Catholic Daughters of 

America mother and child fund to such projects as the Catholic Maternity Institute in Santa Fe; Mrs. Dang Sy, whose husband, a Catholic army man, was sentenced to life imprisonment in Vietnam, and to the Multipurpose Social Center in Akhimim, Upper Egypt.

In addition to their cash grants to overseas aid projects, the Catholic Daughters of America makes generous donations of new and used garments and first communion outfits. 

Total cash donations to all programs, $30,500.

The Junior Catholic Daughters of America contributed 1,592 pounds of sweaters for orphans overseas. The juniors also collected funds to buy Christmas gifts for orphans overseas. Both of these combined donations were sent to Archbishop Harold Henry for the needy children of the Kwangju Archdiocese. 

The value of the junior contribution, $5,177.

SUMMARY 

This report covers the fiscal year of the National Council of Catholic Women from July 1, 1964, to June 30, 1965. The overall 12-month total, including cash gifts and gifts-in-kind, amounted to $664,693. 

Mr. FULTON. As I am of a Presbyterian family, and a grandson of a Presbyterian minister, Dr. John Lockhart Fulton of Pittsburgh, it is a pleasure for me to say if we Presbyterians had bishops I would

certainly nominate and recommend my good friend, His Excellency the Most Reverend John Wright, Bishop of Pittsburgh, the Bishop of Miami, or the Bishop of Wheeling, W. Va. It is good people such as these who back this type of program, who give it the level of policy that every religious person and every charitable person can join; wouldn't you agree?

Mrs. PATTERSON. Yes. 

Mr. FULTON. It unites us. The talk here has been the casualties in war at the present time. We must be the people who have optimism and not look at the pessimistic side of life and that we are all coming to destruction. But the ones of us who feel that the world is a fine place, God made it and we will be able to work out progress and peace all right, agree with you thoroughly. I want to compliment you especially on your phrase "the works of peace"; that is a wonderful phrase.

When we look at the casualties compared in the Vietnamese war, we can see there are 15,000 or 18,000 American casualties. We multiply by 5 or 6 to get the South Vietnamese casualties just recently.

South Korean figures are not available. This would make a total of 100,000 casualties on our side of free peoples and probably two to three times as many casualties on the other side. 

If we add these all up, these casualties would come to approximately 300,000 total casualties in this fight for freedom in Vietnam. 

There are 3 billion people in this world. It is really the only largescale war going on out of 3 billion people. So my proposition with you is in answer to the gentleman from Iowa, that it is a peaceful world because the casualties are only one thirty-thousandths or 1 in 30,000 of the population of the world. 

If you put that in terms of decimal points you have to put a decimal point and put to the right of the decimal point a zero, zero, zero, zero, and then you get a 1, four zeros after the right side of it before we even calculate one war casualty.

I only wish that automobiles would have as few casualties. Each activity is a danger, but for the purpose of freedom, and protection of free peoples we have to stand up for our principles even if we have the war, mustn't we? 

Mrs. PATTERSON. I feel we do.

Mr. FULTON. For the purpose of transportation, we don't even talk of canceling or abandoning or abolishing automobiles, do we? 

Therefore, if we want to succeed and avoid war, we must take the actions and serve the works of peace to work against war and for peace; is that not right?

Mrs. PATTERSON. I believe that sincerely. 

Mr. FULTON. Do you Catholic women in the National Catholic

Council of Women believe that these works have shown results? 

Have not the works and programs of the U.S. Government in the foreign aid programs shown results because they have certainly raised people and lifted them up; isn't that right?

Mrs. PATTERSON. That is right.

Mr. ROYBAL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

tion that the richer should aid the poorer, and the stronger should support the weaker, and that the more advanced hold out a helping hand to the needy.

I think this is a philosophy that the Council of Catholic Women have followed for many years. I remember the work of the Catholic women during the Second World War, particularly with the poor in the district that I represent, the many men who had to go to war and leave their wives and large families, and the Catholic women at that particular time coming to their rescue. 

I remember the work that was done during the Korean conflict. 

I know the work that is being done now. I say to you that you have not only talked about this as a matter of philosophy, you have put it into practice.

I wish to commend you and the organization for the work that you have done throughout the many years.

Our next witness is J. Orrin Shipe, managing director, CUNA 

International, Inc. With Mr. Shipe is John S. Rippey, director of the Washington office.

STATEMENT OF J. ORRIN SHIPE, MANAGING DIRECTOR, CUNA INTERNATIONAL, INC. 

Mr. SHIPE. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, CUNA International, an association representing more than 18,000 credit unions throughout the world, is grateful for this opportunity to report to you on our credit union organizational efforts in underdeveloped lands. 

For well over a decade now, CUNA has been engaged in the challenging missionary work of bringing the thrift and lending services of credit unions to people in foreign nations. Since 1962, we have been materially assisted in this enormous undertaking by the Agency for 

International Development, which has contracted with us to augment our overseas efforts.

For that reason, we believe it is only proper that we should furnish the American public through the vehicle of this committee with a reckoning of how we have made use of the Government's assistance. 

Approximately $1.6 million has been expended under the CUNA- 

AID program during the past 4 years, averaging about $400,000 annually. At the same time, CUÑA has provided, from its own dues income, $400,000 to extend credit unions overseas. In fact, our

organization is presently expending some 12 percent of its dues income for credit union organization work among the poor of this country or in underdeveloped lands.

It was in the decade following the end of World War II that deprived people from all over the world and particularly Latin America began turning to CUNA for know-how and advice in establishing credit unions. Clearly they saw in the credit union a simple democratic tool with which they could rid themselves of the bondage of usury and provide themselves with the capital to build a better life. 

When formally established in 1954, CUÑA's world extension program concentrated its early efforts in Central and South America. It is in this area today where, with the financial support of AID, we are experiencing truly impressive credit union gains.

You may have read in Time magazine only last week of the work undertaken in Peru by the Maryknoll missionary, Father Dan Mc- Clellan. His success in forming credit unions and savings and loan associations among the Indians in the Peruvian Andes, according to Time, is having a startling impact on the economic and social life of that people.

Since the CUNA-AID program was launched in 1962, the number of credit unions in Central and South America has spiraled from 430 to 2,000. Today, nearly half a million Latin Americans have accumulated $31 million in their credit unions and are currently lending to one another some $29 million.

But more important than the dollar statistics is the total impact of the credit union on the lives of the people. It has engendered new hope and confidence and has demonstrated that it is now indeed possible for them to make decisions and to do things for themselves that will materially alter their environment and enhance their standard of living.

In whole villages there is broken centuries-old attitudes bred of despair and grinding poverty. They are supplanted by the cooperative ideals of hard work, democratic participation and integrity in personal dealings.

The impressive credit union development in Latin America was made possible in such a relatively brief period because of the participation of AID in CUNA's world extension efforts. Without AID's backing, we estimate that it would have taken CUNA at least a decade to accomplish what was done in 4 years.

Following this statement, you will find an appendix which contains a country-by-country breakdown of the CUNA-AID program. 

Three East African nations-Kenya, Tanzania, and Uganda-are included in the list. The programs there have only recently been launched, but it is evident that our Latin American experience will be repeated. Our member credit union leagues in Michigan and Saskatchewan in the early 1960's took it upon themselves to finance the sending of a credit union technician to Tanzania. This mission showed clearly that the credit union idea is fully adaptable to East Africa. 

Since then, a CUNA-AID contract has been executed which will broaden and accelerate our African extension work. 

I might add there is included in the appendix a second chart containing data on credit unions throughout the world.

A key aspect of CUNA's credit union overseas program which must be emphasized is its self-liquidating or terminal nature. It is always our goal to impart credit union skills to indigenous people, to set up native institutions necessary to perpetuate the program, and then to depart.

Thus, along with organizing credit unions, we strive to form a credit union league in each country. The league, like the credit union itself, is a voluntary, nonprofit democratic organization. Its job is to organize more credit unions, train leaders, represent the movement with the government, and promote the credit union idea generally. Where a strong and active league is present, it is possible as in Peru, for CUNA to withdraw its assistance, leaving a viable program in the hands of native credit unionists.

In closing, may I suggest that this committee might find informative a staff report compiled by the Subcommittee on International Finance of the House Banking and Currency Committee. This report thoroughly analyzes the joint AID programs of 14 cooperative organizations like CUNA International. It reveals that the total cost to AID for these programs through 1965 was less than $5 million. 

I think you will agree that compared with other governmental expenditures, this is small, indeed. I hope you will also agree that in terms of total impact in underdeveloped lands this expenditure has been a prudent and rewarding investment by our Government. 

This concludes my statement. I appreciate very much the committee's courtesy in granting me this opportunity. Should you have any questions, I will be glad to endeavor to answer them.
