Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Shipe.

Mr. Shipe, I for one am glad to see your organization appear here today. There has been a great deal of interest among members of this committee in the operation of credit unions in underdeveloped countries. I think you have a good statement that will be helpful to the committee. 

I want to congratulate you for your appearance today. Thank you. 

Mr. Frelinghuysen.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I have no questions. I would like to join with the chairman in saying that the testimony has been interesting and I personally feel that your organization has been doing a very valuable work. 

Thank you. 

Mr. SHIPE. Thank you, Mr. Congressman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. I would like to know a little bit more about the organizational structure of your organization. No. 1, I would like to know whether or not it is a federation of credit unions throughout the world and No. 2, I would like to know where you get your funds to aid the underdeveloped countries in starting their own credit unions, and third, I would like to know the amount of interest that you would charge in the event that you do make moneys available to credit unions in other underdeveloped countries of the world. 

Mr. SHIPE. I will try to answer your questions.

First, the organizational structure of the credit union movement is first a credit union which is a voluntary cooperative group who obtained a charter from either the State or Federal Government of the United States or the various countries in which they may be located. 

This organization pools the savings of the group, makes the savings pool available for loans. It is entirely an autonomous organization under their ownership and control. They charge themselves interest for the loans and they pay themselves the dividends or interest on their savings after deducting a small amount of expense for their supplies, et cetera, and set aside a nominal reserve. 

The credit unions have banded themselves together in a voluntary organization called their provincial state or country league, the Dis trict of Columbia Credit League, the Ontario Credit Union League, et cetera.

They represent the credit unions in their particular area and they have in turn formed the voluntary organization CUNA International. 

This organization serves credit unions in more than 70 countries of the world. Our program is to aid in the organization of credit unions without cost or obligation to them. To provide technical guidance in how they can provide educational, public relations, and supply services to them.

The whole program is financed by a 9-cent per member dues if a credit union wishes to become a member of its league and its international organization. The money that comes for the extension of the work into Central and South America and other parts of the world primarily comes from the United States and the Canadian credit unions, because the credit unions in these countries are a long way from being self-supporting or contributing to the overall international effort. 

---

Mr. ROYBAL. In your effort, then, to start credit unions in any particular country, let's say Latin America, do you make available front money to get the credit union started?

Mr. SHIPE. We do not make any capital available to them. What we do provide them with is technical assistance. We have sent skilled, trained, experienced credit union people into South America since 1952 who engage in acquainting the people and the government with what the credit union is and what it can do in assisting the individual.

Then we provide them with assistance in organizing their own credit unions and instructions on how to operate the credit union. It is their own money that they pool together. They make these small loans to themselves. It is the step-by-step progress that makes it possible for them to pool their own money but we provide them with technical instructional educational assistance. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Thank you very much.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Gross. 

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Shipe, do I understand from your statement that you hold contracts in the amount of $1,600,000 to administer this program?

Mr. SHIPE. That has been the total sum since 1962. 

Mr. GROSS. Is the present rate $400,000 annually? 

Mr. SHIPE. Approximately; yes, sir. 

Mr. GROSS. That is for administrative expense, is that correct?

Mr. SHIPE. No. This is for the local technicians in these countries and it is for the community technicians who are providing the training right at the community grassroots levels in these areas, this provides their salaries, travel, their material.

A very small part is for administrative expenses. 

Mr. GROSS. What I am getting at is, you are paid the $400,000 annually, is this correct? CUNA- 

Mr. SHIPE. Providing for the payment, yes. 

Mr. GROSS. Under your contract. Is it one contract, or a series of contracts?

Mr. SHIPE. It is a series of contracts depending on the various countries and the kind of program that AID and CUNA agree can best be done there.

Mr. GROSS. Then you in turn pay country 1epresentatives 

Mr. SHIPE. That is right. What we usually do, Mr. Congressman, and we find this to be very effective, we hire a technician experienced person from this country who is usually bilingual and we station him in Bolivia, say, and he will find and train local Bolivians and teach them how to organize, how to instruct and how to assist people in developing their own credit unions so their chain of reaction will result in more of the local natives acquiring the skills and the knowledge to carry on their own programs.

Mr. GROSS. None of this money goes into the capital structure of credit unions in the various countries?

Mr. SHIPE. That is right. This is used for technical assistance only. 

They pool their own money, it is small money, but it is their own money that they have in the capital of their credit union.

at Mr. GROSS. How many American citizens approximately do you have in foreign countries, or do you 

Mr. SHIPE. About 15 or 20. 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Rosenthal. 

Mr. ROSENTHAL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. In a country such as 

Kenya, could you tell us the number of loans that have been granted?

I am somewhat interested in that country, having visited it last fall. 

I would be interested in knowing what types of loans. 

Mr. SHIPE. They are very basic loans. In those countries where it is an agricultural economy essentially they are loans to help the people to acquire plows, such as we don't use here in our great farms in Iowa any more.

This will replace the stick that they have used to scrape the ground. 

A couple of dollars to get some seed or fertilizer. These are small farms and they are small loans. These would be in the amount of $5, $10, or $15.

Mr. ROSENTHAL. Has there been any report as to the rate of payment on the loans, the success in having them repaid?

Mr. SHIPE. We have had no problem in that. We find that the people have had no source of credit in most of these countries except through the loan shark who charges them as much as 10 percent per week if they are lucky enough to gain his favor to get a loan in the first instance.

The lack of capital and the need of it is so great that the people—we find their integrity has been no more problem than anyplace else. 

Mr. ROSENTHAL. Have the people made a psychological adjustment in going from the loan shark to the credit union?

Mr. SHIPE. Yes. It is not without some resistance on the part of the loan shark, because there have been threats, all kinds of intimidating statements are made. "You are walking into a trap." "This is a fool's paradise, this credit union thing." "When you find yourself out in the cold, don't come back to me, because Mr. Loan Shark will no longer give you money at 10 percent a week." 

Mr. ROSENTHAL. Has there been an increase in the number of applications?

Mr. SHIPE. Yes, we have more demand for credit union asistance than we can find the manpower and funds to finance. If we had the manpower and the funds, we could carry on an accelerated program in many more countries. We have to take a step at a time in order to launch it properly before we can move to another country. 

Mr. ROSENTHAL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Morse. 

Mr. MORSE. I have no questions. I would just like to compliment the witness on his presentation and observe that I have followed the work for several years and believe that it is an inspiring story. I think credit union activity is one of the most important parts of the development programs we are financing. 

Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fulton. 

Mr. FULTON. I would like to compliment you on your statement.

I believe this is the kind of progress that has participation of the 60-929-66-pt. 4- -10

citizens of the particular recipient country, this policy makes cooperation and these local citizens feel they are helping themselves. 

I would like to call your attention to a new idea. You people who testify have an impact on us and we hope we can have some impact on your programs. On this committee I was one of about six or eight with our chairman, Congressman Morgan, who fought the State Department for years and prepared the basic language for the investment guarantees program under title III, which is section 221, and it has now been placed in various subsections, particularly (a), (b), (c), and (d). Have you ever thought of combining your programs with the idea of investment guarantees, because then you would only be guaranteeing against loss. Likewise you could have American investors helping you so that private industry and American capital could fill out your capital needs.

Likewise you could in the local country make contact with financial institutions and possibly with larger investors who would invest to get a good return, but not an exorbitant rate of return. 

If we look at paragraph 2 under (b) we find that it is for loan investment for housing projects, with appropriate participation by the private investor in the loan risk, in accordance with the foreign and financial policies of the United States, of insuring against loss not to exceed 75 percent, and so on.

We have that same idea of insuring against loss in many of our governmental lending agencies, for example FDIC, where we insure the amount of ultimate loss. In that regard, could I ask you how much loss would you say had occurred overall on the loans that have been made by these credit unions abroad? Is there any such figure? 

If there is, would you please put it in the record?

Mr. SHIPE. We would be glad to give you the accurate figure, Mr. 

Congressman, and put it in the record. I can say now it has been so small that it has not been disturbing or out of proportion to the experience we have here in the United States, which is one-twentieth of 1 percent of the amount loaned. 

Mr. FULTON. If you people could come up with language that would place you under title III investment guarantees somehow, I would favor this step because there we have in the act already outstanding at any time, not to exceed $300 million guarantee. You could fit under that guarantee provision very well at a much larger scale than now, with no trouble at all. This would result finally to the United States in very little loss and very little investment. I believe if you would take that as a new source or method that you might surprise yourselves.

I have been one of the counsel for a large banking group in Pittsburgh before I came here, and know they are always looking for new good sound methods of investment. When the U.S. Government already has some guarantee program and these local organizations have a good loan record of small losses, and your history is good, I believe you would surprise yourselves and the U.S. financial community and our country and likewise surprise yourselves under title III. 

That is all. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. SHIPE. Thank you, Mr. Congressman. That is an excellent suggestion and you can rest assured we will pursue it. I can say definitely one of the pressing and overwhelming problems is not only the need for technical assistance because they are so illiterate and inexperienced

as compared to the advances made in our country in this area, but also the capital needs are most depressing. This might be a good assist. 

Mr. FULTON. Would you submit the amendment to the chairman of the committee? He is interested in this and I would join in anything he recommended on this guarantee subject. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Shipe. 

Mr. SHIPE. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Next we have a group of witnesses representing the Citizens Foreign Aid Committee, represented by Brig. Gen. Bon ner Fellers, chairman, and Dr. Elgin Groseclose, economic consultant, and Dr. Melchior Palyi, economist.

General, I want to say it is a pleasure to welcome you back. We have had you with us for a good many years. Personally, I want to say,

STATEMENT OF BRIG. GEN. BONNER FELLERS, CHAIRMAN, CITIZENS FOREIGN AID COMMITTEE 

General FELLERS. My name is Bonner Fellers. I am grateful for this opportunity to appear before this distinguished Foreign Affairs Committee.

I have spent 30 years in the Regular Army. For 2 years, 1940-42, I was the U.S. observer of combat in the Libyan Desert. From 1943 to 

1946 I participated in the war in the Pacific. Among my assignments were chief of MacArthur's joint planning section, military secretary to General MacArthur, and chief of his psychological warfare effort against the Japanese. Over a period of 25 years, I have spent 10 years in the Orient.

Inherent in U.S. foreign aid is our entanglement in the psychological, political, economic, and military affairs of recipient countries. 

From this entanglement arise antipathy, rioting, economic dislocation, and war against the United States.

PSYCHOLOGICAL-POLITICAL 

Lavish and ill-advised aid spending often creates suspicion and resentment among recipients that somehow they are being exploited. 

From 1950 through 1964 foreigners staged 134 insults and riots against the United States. Since 1964 many other riots against us have occurred.

On March 8, 300 Indonesian students attacked the U.S. Embassy in Djakarta with stones and fire bombs. Only last week, student rioters in Ankara, Turkey, greeted Secretary of State Rusk's arrival with banners saying "This is not Johnson's ranch" and "Yankee go home." Turkey has had about $5 billion in U.S. aid. And nearly all of these rioting peoples elsewhere were nationals of countries receiving 

U.S. foreign aid.

ECONOMIC 

In developing countries, our billions in foreign aid frequently causes economic dislocation. Our food for peace, throws foreign farmers out of work and undercuts normal food prices. Premature and unnecessary industrial development tends to preclude attainment of selfsufficiency in food production. But the real reason for famine in Russia, Red China, and India lies in the fact that these Socialist states offer farmers no incentive. Qualified observers hold that without the magic of incentives this food problem cannot be solved. 

MILITARY ASSISTANCE 

Our involvement in Vietnam began with a Marshall plan gift of $293 million to Indochina. To strengthen the French forces, shortly before their surrender at Dienbienphu in May 1954, the Hoover report lists a gift to France of $745 million authorized by President Eisenhower. This military assistance to France's Indochina war was the origin of our present Vietnam entanglement. France surrendered to Communist forces in May 1954. The war cost her 250,000 French and native casualties, and $5 billion; of this $2 billion was U.S. aid. 

We also gave bombers and, toward the end, sent maintenance crews to service the bombers; this, of course, was an act of war. 

At Las Vegas, Nev., last February 16, Secretary of State Rusk gave as one of five reasons for our present armed intervention:

We are committed to assist South Vietnam to resist aggression *** by the aid approved by bipartisan majorities in Congress over a period of 12 years. 

This statement obviously shocked Senator Fulbright who declared:

It never occurred to me that an aid program could be used as a justification for war. 

U.S. TROOPS 

In Vietnam are some 235,000 American troops; by October the plan is to increase this strength to more than 400,000. Neither the National 

Guard nor the Reserves has been called. To expand our Regular forces, our technical specialists have been drawn from NATO, thereby diminishing the effectiveness of our NATO forces by some 20 percent. The press reports that 15,000 more NATO forces are to be withdrawn. Two battalions have been pulled from Alaska for Vietnam duty. There is a general shortage of junior officers. 

NATO defense has always depended on the use of nuclear tactical weapons. These are not now assigned to troops but are stored centrally. In the event of attack, nuclear weapons were to be distributed to troops by helicopters. However, Vietnam needs have stripped 

NATO of helicopter pilots-and probably helicopters.

LOSSES 

Commercial demands for pilots is making a heavy drain on service pilots. The Navy is short pilots who, for various reasons, experience only a 65-percent survivability. We are losing aircraft faster than they are being produced. Tropical Vietnam is mosquito infested.

The pesky mosquito has developed immunity against Atabrine and quinine; our malaria rate is 109 per 1,000.

THE ENEMY 

The Vietcong requires daily 500 tons of supplies from North Vietnam. Some 800 enemy trucks and thousands of porters and sampans deliver these supplies via land and sea. The enemy enjoys Washington-dictated sanctuaries; one is around Hanoi with a 30-mile radius; another is around Haiphong with a 10-mile radius. Recent reports indicate a slight lifting of restrictions in the Hanoi sanctuary which has resulted in destruction of a powerplant. 

In the absence of engineer troops and Seabees, contracts are let to civilian firms on a cost-plus basis. Estimates suggest a cost of $1.5 billion annually. Contractors hire 60,000 oriental laborers who, together with the Chinese and French merchants and property owners, are responsible for a heavy dollar drain into France and Red China.

Chinese merchants in Vietnam alone take in about $750 million a year. All these expenses swell our balance-of-payments deficits.

THE WAY OUT 

By July 1945 in the war against Japan, the 21st Bomber Command had burned out 66 cities and destroyed many of Japan's greatest factories. In 9 months B-29 raids inflicted 806,000 civilian casualties of whom 330,000 were killed. This slightly exceeded the Japanese estimate of 780,000 combat casualties inflicted on their armed forces during the entire war. Preraid evacuations and mass migration after attacks caused a population displacement of 8,500,000 persons. 

In March, the Emperor, watching a Tokyo bombing, had resolved to stop the war, regardless of what might happen to him personally. 

Near the close of the war MacArthur's medium bombers showered leaflets on some of the cities, warning them that before a certain date these cities would be destroyed by the Army Air Force. The leaflets explained their purpose: to urge families to evacuate and survive, to enable the people to appeal for help so as to learn how powerless the Japanese militarists were to defend them. 

After the surrender, evacuees from the cities which had been destroyed were interviewed. Our leaflets had created stark panic. Cities had been immediately and completely abandoned; no lives were lost. 

No people were ever more thoroughly indoctrinated to resist surrender than were the Japanese. Two million firstline troops stood ready to defend their shores against landings. Yet bombing and blockade forced these tough warriors to lay down their arms without a single hostile incident. General MacArthur flew into Japan with 

1,100 men. 

There, then, is the pattern to superimpose on North Vietnam. Warn military target areas mostly supply centers-that total destruction will soon be inevitable. Urge populations in these military target areas to evacuate to designated tropical sanctuaries. Then destroy every military target. Blockade the coastline. Deny munitions and weapons to the enemy; the end will come quickly, and long before 

Red China could intervene.

Red China intervened in Korea only when it was known that Yalu and Manchuria would be sanctuaries. Moreover, Red China is illprepared for a major war. Her population is hungry, poorly housed

and clothed; her industry is not yet capable of supporting a modern 

And Chiang Kai-shek's invasion threat is a deterrent. war.

In knowledgeable Washington circles a feeling seems to prevail that the war in Vietnam will have been settled in time for a "normal" ($50 billion) defense budget for fiscal 1968. Perhaps this optimistic view derives itself from the Vice President's April 19 announcement that the recent Honolulu Conferencehas as much significance for the future of Asia as the Atlantic Charter had for the future of Europe.

If the President intends to export the Great Society to Asia, such a lush largesse might, optimists hope, tempt Communist enemies to lay down their arms. But will it?

Meantime, the Washington-dictated war objective remains "restrained harassment" until the enemy agrees to unconditional discussions. This objective could be achieved, according to the Air Force 

Chief of Staff, General McConnell, "virtually overnight" if present restrictions on air and sea power were lifted. 

Why fight a war of attrition on enemy terms in tropical jungles? 

Why trade American youth for those of Asia? Military assistance, making full utilization of our fleet and air power, is the only prompt, humane way to end the war. Unless the war is terminated quickly, alleged "wars of liberation" will arise in other areas where we have been entangled through foreign aid and military assistance. 

Already our forces are dangerously overextended should trouble arise elsewhere. Our home base must immediately be strengthened. 

Suppose a situation arises in Cuba demanding immediate intervention? 

Suppose the United Arab Republic, with ample Russian arms, strikes Israel?

FOREIGN AID: NO GUARANTEE OF U.S. SECURITY 

From its inception foreign aid advocates have argued: We can no longer live alone. Collective defense is the answer.

Our friends among free world nations make available to us for the use of our forces *** 250 strategic bases, 5 million ground forces * * * 30,000 air craft*** 2,500 combat vessels. (Statement by President Eisenhower, Mar. 

13, 1959.)

Foreign aid is basic to U.S. security. 

Rusk, Apr. 18, 1966.) 

(Statement by Secretary of State 

Today, where is the evidence to support these lavish endorsements of foreign aid? Did our allies support us in the Korean war? How much support are they extending in South Vietnam? 

Secretary Rusk's statement that "foreign aid is basic to U.S. security" is wholly in error. Have not the American people the right to know how foreign aid is basic to our security? Secretary Rusk suports his statement by:

Without it (foreign aid) many countries undoubtedly would have been subverted or overrun in the past two decades. 

Communists have had considerable success in subverting countries which have received foreign aid during the past two decades; for example, Red China, Cuba, the Dominican Republic, and Bolivia.

Whether or not Russia has had aggressive intent during the past two decades is unknown. But if Russia did have such intent, she was deterred not one iota by U.S. foreign aid but rather by the U.S. strategic striking forces-missiles and bombers.

FI te 

THE REAL REASON FOR FOREIGN AID 

Foreign aid is a great leveling process to redistribute the wealth of the world. Foreign aid planners are disappointed that all affluent countries of the West are too sophisticated to lend full support to our effort. General de Gaulle's nationalism is a serious irritant. 

The Johnson plan for Asia, which it appears contemplates extending the Great Society to that continent, would, it is hoped, usher in an oriental era of good will.

Support for this world government movement is found in the writings of Mr. Walt Whitman Rostow in "The United States in the 

World Arena": 

*** it is a legitimate American national objective to see removed from all nations-including the United States-the right to use substantial military force to pursue their own interests. Since this residual right is the root of national sovereignty and the basis for the existence of an international arena of power, it is, therefore, an American interest to see an end to nationhood as it has been historically defined. * * * the urgent imperative to tame military force and the need to deal with peoples everywhere on the basis of an accelerating proximity argue strongly for movement in the direction of federalized world organization under effective international law.

Since Mr. Rostow, until recently, was in charge of State Department planning and is now adviser on foreign affairs to the President, his views are significant. 

Department of State publication "Freedom From War," released 

September 1, 1961, calls for immediate disarmament action, subject to international control with adequate international peacekeeping machinery.

At the present stage of world civilization world government would lead, not to a "Tower of Babel" but rather to a "World of Babel."

Foreign aid and world government can succeed only if everyone loves everyone else. It envisages the millenium now, whereas in reality the millenium is eons beyond a very blurred horizon. 

CONCLUSION 

In view of the Government's unprecedented spending, all foreign aid should be terminated except for our food-for-peace program and aid to South Vietnam. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, General Fellers. 

Dr. Groseclose, you may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF DR. ELGIN GROSECLOSE, ECONOMIC CONSULTANT, CITIZENS FOREIGN AID COMMITTEE Mr. GROSECLOSE. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, thank you for the privilege of again presenting my views on the foreign aid program. 

To conserve your time I respectfully request that a statement of my qualifications as a witness be filed and made an addendum to my oral testimony. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection it is so ordered.

Dr. GROSECLOSE. It is now 10 years since I first appeared before this committee to offer the case against foreign aid, and as I recall, I was the sole witness to register such opposition. Two years later a group

of some 50 leading and distinguished citizens, recognizing the seeds of disaster in foreign aid theory and practice, formed a committee-this Citizens Foreign Aid Committee, under whose sponsorship I now appear, to register a wider opposition to the policy. These committee members are men and women noted for their integrity, their acumen, their judgment, and their devotion to the highest interests of the country.

Together we are still a small voice lifted in the Washington wilderness, but other voices of dissent are being lifted against foreign aid. 

If those voices have not yet penetrated these august chambers, they will one day, and like the trumpets of the hosts of Joshua, bring down the walls of bureaucracy erected by misguided, frightened people who thought to defend their world with bricks of gold and a moat of paper money.

RISING OPPOSITION TO FOREIGN AID 

What are these voices? They are those, first, of travelers and students, missionaries and others who are personally acquainted with the lands where aid money is being spent, who have intimate knowledge of conditions, the people, and their needs. I shall cite some instances later.

Second, they are the voices of thoughtful, concerned, religious, and philanthropic people who originally endorsed foreign aid and gave it enthusiastic support on the grounds of compassion and moral obligation, but who are beginning to recognize the spurious character of foreign aid, and see what it is a doctrinal heresy that draws its roots from atheistic Marxism rather than from Christian-Judaic teaching. 

This heresy is the doctrine, developed by Marx and Engels, that man is purely a material creature and that you can manipulate his feelings, his aspirations, even his politics, by molding his environment, particularly, in foreign aid practice by large investments in capital equipment.

Third, they are the voices of a vast and increasing segment of the electorate, heads of families who are faced with rising prices and rising taxes. These increases were brought on by inordinate overseas spending for projects that did not produce their equivalents in human satisfactions, that in consequence fostered inflation abroad and at home, an inflation that is now being felt throughout the country and by all classes.

I would like to interpolate at this point and comment on previous testimony to the effect that you are promoting peace by educating people. There is no heresy more contrary to Christian doctrine than that. We have the evidence of it in that the greatest war in modern time was started by a people who were perhaps the most educated people in the world today. 

Fourth, they are the voices of responsible leadership of the country, the men in charge of great enterprises, that employ millions of professional, managerial, and crafts personnel of our industries. They realize that the health of the world's economy-including the Communist countries-depends vitally upon the economic health of the United States and that this health is being undermined today by the joint forces of inflation and taxes, both arising from unrestricted overseas spending by the Government in its political and foreign aid

ಸತ hal er ted he the tel country commitments. They know, even more, that the fabric of world trade is supported by the U.S. dollar, and that the value of the dollar is steadily deteriorating through the drain abroad of the gold reserves of this country and the continual increase of overseas short-term monetary liabilities.

Fifth, there are ominously the cries of the hungry and the distressed abroad, the famine sufferers and the impoverished whose plight is directly attributable to foreign aid. If this appears to be a strong charge to this committee, I suggest it reread the testimony I offered 10 years ago. I pointed out then, from my personal observations in the Middle East, that the foreign aid program, with its emphasis upon industrialization, was disrupting traditional economies and creating new strains, dislocations, and discontent, without actually alleviating the old.

FOREIGN AID FAILURES 

I cited the Helmand River Dam project in Afghanistan, one of the first of the "showcase" demonstrations of the purported benefits of foreign aid, and how even such a fanatical proponent of foreign aid as the New York Times had found it to be an abortion. It had actually saddled that poor Middle East country with an inordinate fiscal burden, had raised false hopes, and had created discontent among the tribes by breaking up their age-old livelihood system and coercing them to settle into an agrarian life which was alien to their traditions and their desires. 

As the New York Times conceded:

The Afghan aid program has become a bitter burden that seriously threatens the Afghan economy and presents the United States with a critical problem in a politically strategic area. 

The Times' report went on to say that The United States may have unwittingly and indirectly contributed to driving 

Afghanistan into Russian arms. 

This was March 18, 1956.

That fiasco has been swept under the ample rug of the foreign aid administration where so many other failures also lie, and nothing is heard of it nowadays. My recalling it to your attention is like recalling the ghost of Hamlet's father.

I cited India, and the testimony of the noted anthropologist, Margaret Mead, who had studied the problems of aid for the United Nations, and had reported the pitfalls in a document, "Cultural Patterns and Economic Change." She had cited as an illustration how the sudden introduction of so simple an innovation as iron plows in a rural village set up a widening circle of disruption and dislocation which an imported aid program, dreamed up and fomented by theoreticians of another continent and another culture, was incapable of meeting.

GOVERNMENT INVESTIGATION OF FOREIGN AID 

In 1957, the House Committee on Government Operations issued a scathing report on the waste and failures of the foreign aid program in Iran, but it was ignored and forgotten in the march of the iron men toward bigger and bigger foreign aid, and the dollars in the hundreds of millions continued to pour into Iran. From time to time the Comp-

troller General has issued reports of other fiascos in foreign aid; these, too, are left to gather dust on the shelf.

In my 1956 testimony I had pointed out the dangers inherent in the foreign aid program in Turkey, and my findings were supported in the report a year later of Ambassador Norman Armour, made for the 

Senate Foreign Relations Committee. He stated: 

Basically, Turkey is suffering from too much economic devlopment, too fast. ***The United States must share some of the blame for Turkey's overambitious economic development. 

What were the consequences? Inflation spread its virus throughout the countryside, eating up savings and breeding fevers of discontent; corruption waxed among officials; traders, promoters, and industrialists fattened on foreign aid contracts. The end result was a political explosion from which Turkey has not yet recovered. The President of the Republic was brought to trial and executed; stable government has hardly yet returned; after 17 years Turkey's economy continues to need the prop of international assistance. 

THE INDIAN FAMINE 

Let us return to India, where the distinguished Peter Bauer pointed out in 1959 that India was spending on a single steel mill more than twice its development expenditure on elementary education. (This is found in "U.S. Aid and Indian Economic Development," published by the American Enterprise Association, Washington, 1959, p. 75.)

What is the condition today in India, for which President Truman declared a forced draft program of aid to double the standard of living within a decade?

The matter is front page news around the world. The Indian people are starving, and an international relief is being organized, of which the U.S. share will be 1 bushel of wheat out of very 3 grown by the American farmer.

The causes of this condition are complex, not least of these being the Indian reverence for the cow, so that while the outer world is feeding the starving millions of people, these starving millions are supporting, immune from slaughter and largely useless for any service, some 180 million cattle, or a fourth of the world's bovine population. 

Nevertheless, the foreign aid program which has absorbed in India alone nearly $5 billion must share major accountability for the Indian distress. It is responsible because of its encouragement and assistance to the Indian Government to divert energies and revenues to unwise industrialization rather than the basic needs of the people, who have historically followed an agricultural livelihood. The data to document this may be found in the volumes of budgetary and other briefing material filed with your committee by a succession of foreign aid administrations.

Paradoxically, our very generosity in supplying foodstuffs is also to blame. I use the word "generosity" but without cynicism. I am persuaded that many of the votes for the so-called food for peace were not given out of generosity and philanthropy but they were given to serve the agricultural interest of this country. Nevertheless, the effect of providing cheap wheat and other foodstuffs has been in India exactly what the results would be if India, providentially, were able to flood this country with cheap wheat; it would bankrupt the farmer

Se 11 ot and drive him out of business. We have contributed to the decay of the agricultural economy in India, Pakistan, Iran, Turkey—to mention only the countries with which I am personally acquainted-by our unwise surplus food disposal program, coupled with our overall foreign aid policies.

FOREIGN AID LEADS TO WARS 

Last to be mentioned among the voices heard against foreign aid are the cries of the dying, the wounded, the imprisoned, and the dispossessed in Vietnam. How did we find ourselves involved in this war that no one dares call a war, that no one wishes, and the consequences of which no one has the courage to face?

It all began with a foreign aid program. Senator Fulbright expressed surprise recently when Secretary of State Rusk called his attention to this simple fact, that once you begin to assume responsibility for the well-being of peoples over whom you exercise no sovereignty, you have lost your freedom of action; you are involved, willy-nilly, in every problem, economic, cultural, political, of the people and culture you are assisting. You sign a blank check that may be cashed for millions-yes, billions.

We have the present case of Vietnam. A few years ago it was 

Lebanon, when we had to land marines to preserve the peace. Yesterday it was Santo Domingo. Tomorrow it will be Cyprus, if Greece and

Turkey, both of whom have been lavishly furnished with guns and butter, cannot contain their rivalries in the eastern Mediterranean. 

Only by the fortunate good offices of our major political rival, the Soviet Union, have we been saved from a similar intervention in the latent warfare between India and Pakistan--both starving, yet both furnished with guns and planes and ammunition by our ill-conceived aid program.

Happily, a growing number of Congressmen are beginning to hear and heed these voices of protest. Trustfully, this committee also will hear them, examine their claims, and at long last, relieve the world of this ill-begotten, mishapen incubus called foreign aid, and take bold moves to terminate it once and for all.

ALTERNATIVE TO FOREIGN AID 

I am aware that the dilemma that baffles many on this committee and in Congress, who recognize the truth and logic of what I have presented, is the same dilemma we face in Vietnam: How do we get out of this mess?

Having persuaded the rest of the world that we owe it a living, having pleaded guilty to the sin of being prosperous, having acknowledged the necessity of penance, how do we discharge once and for all this unworthy debt? Or, to those who are driven by fear, how do we woo the uncommitted countries-incidentally, a roster that grows in number in direct ratio to our foreign aid largesse-and coax our socalled friendly powers, France is a case in point, to remain in our debt and not go awhoring after the harlot forces of communism, as even the government of De Gaulle is reported to be doing with Soviet Russia?

I cannot conclude without offering three alternatives.

POLITICAL DISENGAGEMENT 

The first alternative is political disengagement. This counsel is best phrased in the Mother Goose ditty of Little Bo Peep who lost her sheep: "Leave them alone, and they'll come home, wagging their tails behind them."

We may see it working in Egypt, in Indonesia, in Ghana, and no doubt shortly we may see it working in Cuba. These are countries where their leaders have said, like Sukarno, "To hell with American aid" and willy-nilly, we have been compelled to withdraw our foreign aid and watch them go off and to bed with the Communist powers. But they are beginning to sicken of the experience, and without assistance. from the CIA, and without foreign aid bribery, they are beginning to return to a more respectful view of this country. 

Anyone with experience in human nature can understand the process. It is not unlike that of a young man smothered by parental love, who to escape being stifled, breaks away from home. Much of the drift to communism or neutralism in the emerging countries is a reaction to too much U.S. foreign aid, too much U.S. official interest and influence.

RELEASE THE FORCES OF PRIVATE ENTERPRISE 

Second, give private enterprise a better break. Today private investment abroad is being restricted by official pressure to reduce the balance-of-payments deficit. Meantime official spending goes merrily on, and each year Congress, while shouting amen to private enterprise, quietly extends the Government monopoly of foreign finance.

Only recently Congress added another international bank to the list of those which, headquartered though they are in Washington under the very eyes of Congress, are as independent in their motions and as uncontrolled, unregulated, unsupervised in their operations as 

Mariner IV wandering somewhere in interplanetary space.

Along with reducing the foreign aid share of the overseas dollars, we might encourage the backward countries to adopt some of the commercial practices, codes, and moralities that are necessary to attract capital investment. A major cause of economic retardation in these countries is the lack of incentives to capital accumulations and investment, due to deteriorating money, capricious administration, immature moral sanctions, uncertain justice. Foreign aid, instead of dealing with these festering realities by recognizing that the basic need of the world is spiritual regeneration and not economic uplift, and by offering the purgatives of moral and political leadership, has applied the poultices of dollars for glittering showcases of docks and dams, boulevards and electric lights. 

PRIVATE PHILANTHROPIES HAMPERED 

Third, allow the historic processes of personal intercommunication through various religious and philanthropic agencies, and the commercial agencies of communication, the press, journals, and the arts. 

Historically, a principal fruit of the work of these agencies in the 19th century was the reservoir of good will created around the world for the United States-a reserve that, tragically, has been squandered and exploited by various postwar administrations so that it is hard

to find a land today where the American does not find himself suspect and on the defensive.

As a result of Government foreign aid, most of these private philanthropic agencies are today finding increasing difficulty in raising funds for their overseas operations. As head of a Christian agency for the sightless of the Middle East, I can testify to the difficulty-a resistance to giving on the grounds that the Government is doing the job-or is taking so much in taxes that little is left for giving. 

The fact is that Government is not doing the job. The economic cost of blindness, where in the Middle East are an estimated half million sightless, is incalculable; yet for all the billions that our Government has spent in aid to that region, the assistance or encouragement it has offered to combat blindness is close to nil; nor have the governments of that region, under their own or U.S. stimulus, undertaken any program for the blind that can be called by that name. 

In any case, the largest single center for the blind in the Middle East, in terms of persons served, is the privately operated Christian agency which 

I am privileged to head, with a budget not yet in the scores of thousands, compared with the hundreds of thousands, up to hundreds of millions assigned under foreign aid to various economic boondoggling projects. 

Hopefully, therefore, we come before your committee again, Mr.

Chairman, trusting that you will face the realities of the foreign aid issue, and at least recognize that, noble experiment that it was, foreign aid has been a hopeless failure and should be cut off, repudiated, and renounced. 

(The addendum of Dr. Groseclose's qualifications is as follows:) ELGIN GROSECLOSE 

Elgin Groseclose, Ph. D., financial and investment consultant of Washington,

D.C., has been acquainted with the Middle East since 1921 when he taught in a mission school in Persia (now Iran) and did relief work in the Soviet Caucasus. 

His first professional assignment 43 years ago was as a Department of Commerce specialist in Far Eastern finance. His first publications were two monographs, one on "Budgets of Far Eastern Countries," and the other on "Currency Systems of the Orient." Both are official documents of the Department of Commerce. 

In 1935, Dr. Groseclose authored "Money: The Human Conflict," republished in 1962 as "Money and Man." He is also the author of "Introduction to Iran" (Oxford University Press, 1947), a standard reference work on that country, and five other books relating to the Middle East. His most recent book, just published, is entitled "Fifty Years of Managed Money-The Story of the Federal 

Reserve System."

During World War II, Dr. Groseclose served as Treasurer General of Iran, and in the years since he has frequently visited the Middle East on business, on behalf of clients, or as head of Welfare of the Blind, Inc., a Christian agency for the sightless of the Middle East, which he founded in 1956. Since 1944 he has been head of Groseclose, Williams & Associates, financial and investment consultants of Washington, D.C. He is also founder and executive director of the 

Institute for Monetary Research, Inc.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Dr. Groseclose. 

Dr. Palyi, you may proceed, sir.

General FELLERS. Mr. Chairman, Dr. Palyi is not going to give any of his background. Could I just give a little of his background? We are so fortunate to have him here today.

Chairman MORGAN. Certainly. 

General FELLERS. Dr. Palyi, an American citizen, was born in Hungary and educated in Switzerland and Germany, receiving his master's

degree from the Graduate School of Commerce and his doctor's degree from the University of Munich. His academic career includes memberships on the faculties of the Universities of Munich, Goettingen, Kiel, and the Graduate School of Commerce of Berlin, where he became professor of finance in 1929.

Dr. Palyi's broad career in continental countries, Great Britain and the United States, combined business activities with academic work in some of the leading universities both here and abroad. His banking experience was obtained in a major German bank and the Austro- 

Hungarian National Bank.

He was foreign representative of the Hungarian Ministry of Agriculture. Later he was engaged as an expert to the German Govern ment's Industrial Nationalization Committee. 

In 1928, Dr. Palyi became economist for the largest continental banking institution, the Deutsche Bank in Berlin. Also, he was adviser to the Central Bank of Germany from 1931 to 1933, and managing director of its Institute for Currency Research.

In 1933, he was guest of the Midland Bank, Ltd., London, and invited as guest lecturer by the University College of Oxford. 

He made his first visit to America in 1926-27 as visiting professor of the University of Chicago. Since 1933, Dr. Palyi has lived in 

America. His activities as visiting professor and research economist for the Universities of Chicago, Wisconsin, Southern California, and the Northwestern University, included broad literary and platform experience, also lectures at other colleges and universities and broadcasts on the "University of Chicago Round Table of the Air" and the "Northwestern University Reviewing Stand." He was, for a year, news commentator for a Chicago radio station and wrote numerous reviews and articles for the Chicago Tribune.

He is a member of the executive committee of the Economists' National Committee on Monetary Policy, and a member of the board of Lehigh Valley Industries, Inc. 

In Germany, Dr. Palyi-who is the son of a Hungarian newspaper publisher-published a column in as many as 40 major newspapers. 

In 1945, he was sent to England on behalf of the Detroit News and a chain of newspapers to study economic and political conditions. Now, he visits Europe frequently.

His publications include "The Chicago Credit Market," "Compul sory Medical Care and the Welfare State," "The Dollar Dilemma: 

Perpetual Aid to Europe?," "Managed Money at the Crossroads," and 

"An Inflation Primer" (1961). 

In early 1961, the University of Cologne (Germany) conferred on him the honorary doctor title.

At present, Dr. Palyi lives in Chicago, engaged as a financial adviser to banks and other institutions, in addition to his work as author and lecturer.

I think we are fortunate to have Dr. Palyi here today. 

Chairman MORGAN. Dr. Palyi, you may proceed, sir. 

STATEMENT OF DR. MELCHIOR PALYI, ECONOMIST, CITIZENS FOREIGN AID COMMITTEE 

Dr. PALYI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman, for the privilege of presenting my views on foreign aid and international liquidity. I re-

er 1 spectfully request that a statement of my qualifications as a witness be made an addendum to my oral testimony. With your permission, Mr. Chairman, I might like to preface my prepared statement with a few words on international liquidity, since there is a great deal of misunderstanding about that term and about the problem connected with it.

By international liquidity is to be understood the sum total of all means by which deficits in national payments balances can be covered. 

Not to be understood by the problem of international liquidity is its relation to the size or volume of imports of a country or the countries concerned, as it is frequently done by official economists. 

This is for us not only a vital question but perhaps the most important single economic problem of the era. In the 19th century the 

British pound was the international medium next to gold. Today the dollar is the international liquidity, next to gold. Gold is, of course, accepted everywhere instantaneously and in most countries at a fixed price. It has no marketing problem. International liquidity in the form of a currency is accepted if it is, and so far as it is, considered to be convertible into gold.

We are in the very lucky position of being able to produce fabricated international liquidity by printing dollars or writing out checks for dollars. But, of course, the question of convertibility arises and with that the question of the future of the dollar, the very question of our price level and income level, the prospects for international trade and international finance.

However, my proper subject is the relation of international liquidity troubles to the foreign aid burden. 

The late Prof. F. Benham (London School of Economics) figured out in 1961 that total economic aid to the underdeveloped countries would have to amount to about $83 billion annually in order to raise the average per capita income of their some 1,100 million inhabitants to a modest $200 yearly. Five years later, given the lack of progress in the respective countries, given also their population growth and the worldwide rise of prices, it would take an annual $100 billion or more to accomplish the same result.

Of course, the $200 income per capita of these backward nations remains a utopian goal, invoked for sheer rhetorical purposes. In fact, the donor countries in general, the United States in particular, are in the embarrassing situation in which they cannot even spend all the funds earmarked for economic aid. The President of the 

World Bank complained in 1964 about the shortage of "sound, economically viable projects *** from many developing countries," though, he added, good investment opportunities were not lacking. 

It would be futile, indeed, trying to find a correlation between foreign aid and investment opportunities in the recipient countries. 

But there is a very close relationship between foreign aid received and payment deficiencies incurred by the recipients. 

In 1964, according to the latest annual report of the World Bank, the so-called developing countries were on the receiving end of a $9 billion flow of public and private funds coming from the industrial nations of the West. This refers to economic aid, not military. 

Another $1.5 billion or so had been contributed by the Sino-Soviet bloc, raising the grand total to about $10.5 billion-roughly equal to

the deficits in the same countries' 1964 current accounts with the outside world.

The data about the payments balances of the underdeveloped areas are known to be somewhat less than 100 percent reliable. Even so, the near coincidence in the size of the two figures-the current payment deficiencies of those countries and the volume of capital import that covers them-is very remarkable. It discloses the principal function of foreign aid, providing as it does the means by which the underdeveloped countries pay for both: for the excess of their imports over their exports, and for servicing their obligations to foreigners. 

As a matter of fact, external debt service payments on public and publicly guaranteed debts amount to about $3.5 billion a year, and keep rising. This refers to about 70 underdeveloped countries, not including such countries as Mexico and Kuwait and Saudi Arabia which are underdeveloped but have ample international liquidity available.

Such international indebtedness is largely concentrated in 11 countries, India (at the end of 1963) leading the list by $4 billion. 

The total indebtedness of all underdeveloped countries, including the value of the private investment which is to be serviced, amounts to approaching $40 billion and keeps rising year after year. 

The 11 top "leaders" are: 

India with $4 billion; Brazil, $2.3; Argentina, $2.1; Mexico, $1.6; 

United Arab Republic, $1.3; Pakistan, $1.2: Turkey, $1.1; Yugoslavia, $1.1; Israel, $0.9; Chile, $0.9; and Colombia, $0.7, for a total of $17.2 billion.

The current payment deficits we are discussing recur year after year and show no sign of abating. They constitute the source and the hard core of the international liquidity problem. Almost $10 billion was needed in 1964 to take care of these deficits; the more than $10.5 billion actually received (including short-term commercial credits) did the job and permitted the recipients as a group to increase slightly their monetary reserve holdings of gold and foreign exchange. But the consequence is that a gap is torn in the American balance of payments which carries the lion's share of the burden. 

Our gold losses and the vast accumulation of foreign-owned dollar claims are in turn at the root of the global insecurity that expresses itself in the disappearance of gold into private hoards and in recurrent pressure on the dollar.

The world will never get rid of the global liquidity shortage-as long as 70-odd poor nations spend more than they earn and the rich countries accept the obligation to pay the difference. 

May I add one more remark in this context? How far the situation has gone also, I am afraid I am telling this committee things with which it is well acquainted-is shown by the fact that since 1952 we have lost almost $10 billion in gold; our share in the world's official gold reserves has declined from two-thirds to less than one-third. 

Furthermore, we have accumulated a total of short-term indebtedness officially estimated at over $29 billion, in reality probably over $31 billion, so that the ratio of the remaining gold reserve to our foreign short-term obligations amounts to 1 or 2 or less. This is to be corrected by $7 billion of short-term dollar balances that American institutions own abroad; but many of these are not available, cannot be

drawn upon, being in countries which do not pay in dollars or not at all.

If we continue this way there is no other prospect than the bankruptcy of 70 underdeveloped countries. They cannot pay, that is a simple fact of life, unless we refinance their maturing debt and interest charges a technique to camouflage the all-round bankruptcy. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Dr. Palyi.

General FELLERS. Yes. The strategic Air Force was not under his command. The XXI Bomber Command was under Washington. 

Outside of that, yes, sir.

General FELLERS. It wouldn't be necessary.

General FELLERS. Yes, sir, quickly.

You would have to blockade with the fleet and air power. 

Chairman MORGAN. General, I have only one other question, and

I think this question should be answered by both you and Dr. Groseclose. 

At the conclusion of your remarks you say:

In view of the Government's unprecedented spending all foreign aid should be terminated except food for peace and aid to South Vietnam. 

You seem to recommend a continuation of food for peace and yet Dr. Groseclose on page 6 of his statement appears to take the foodfor-peace program apart, especially in India, Pakistan, Iran and Turkey. 

Do I detect some disagreement between you and your colleague, Dr.

Groseclose, on the food-for-peace program? 

General FELLERS. We didn't confer as we wrote these theses. My thought is that there are people starving in the world and therefore until they can raise their own food I think that it is only humane to feed them. I certainly don't recommend it other than a temporary expedient.

Chairman MORGAN. From the testimony we have received in this committee, the agricultural program in Pakistan seems to be improving under the direction of foreign aid. Dr. Groseclose, do you disagree with that?

Mr. GROSECLOSE. There may be some improvement there. It would be strange if there was not some improvement, but it isn't the kind of aid that meets the basic problem.

We have diverted their interests and their energies mainly to industrialization, to the detriment of their agriculture. There may be marginal improvement in their food production. 60-929-66—11

I would agree with the general on food for peace; we should provide disaster relief. We always have. We have been generous, but as a continuing policy it has created the problem which I indicated in my testimony.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you. 

Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Gentlemen, I think I don't need to tell you that we are pleased to have you with us again, and be given the benefit of your thinking upon some of these problems which we have discussed before. 

I think you render a particularly valuable service here in presenting these views when so many other witnesses do take contrary points of view, and to have a balanced presentation, certainly we need the view that you have suggested.

The chairman has touched upon two things which I had noted down here to raise questions about. I would like to pursue the matter of the military situation, General Fellers, just one step further. 

You made some reference in your testimony to the possible action or reaction of Red China. Would you enlarge upon that a little? I suppose other members, like myself, are frequently asked by our constituents and other people as to the likelihood of Red China intervening more openly and more actively than she now is in the conflict in 

South Vietnam.

I would like to have a little amplification of your views upon that point. 

General FELLERS. Thank you. I have been in China many times, but never since it went Communist. They have-may have-made some improvement. But China as a whole is so inadequate that nothing could change her very much in the period that the Communists have been in control; I doubt if it is improved. 

I saw on television last night some very convincing pictures of the youth movement. in China. That is for the pictures. The people are apathetic, not well clothed or housed. The industry of China is not capable of supporting a modern war. Their fighting would have to be done almost entirely by foot soldiers and rifles. The threat of 

Chiang Kai-shek, with a half million good and well trained soldiers, is a deterrent. If Red China got in a war with the United States, we have the means of moving Chiang Kai-shek into Red China. 

Therefore, Chiang Kai-shek's forces are a very great war deterrent. 

Moreover, this thing could be done so fast in Vietnam that Red China, which would have to move slowly and ponderously, would have great difficulty in arriving on the scene before the war was over. Let's put it this way:

When we went into Vietnam it was an inherent risk which we took that Red China might intervene. That is true. I went to hear Madam

Chiang Kai-shek talk at the Press Club about a month ago, and she was asked the question, two questions that were interesting. One was "why wasn't Chiang Kai-shek helping us in Vietnam?" and her answer to that was "You will have to consult your own Government." 

The other question was, "Are the Red Chinese going to intervene? 

Is there danger of it?" 

Her answer is my answer-I subscribe to it. She said, "Red China will intervene or not intervene precisely as it suits her. And nothing you-the United States-can do one way or another will change it."

"If Mao is confident of a victory, he might do it," she said. She thinks he isn't. She said, "Nothing you will do will change the picture. They will do what they want and think best.".

Mr. ADAIR. Your answer remains the same, General, in light of the fact that we are now warned that Red China may shortly explode another nuclear device, possibly a fission type device?

General FELLERS. They have no delivery system. You could have all the hydrogen bombs or nuclear bombs in the world; if you couldn't deliver them, they wouldn't be of much value. Red China has no air force of consequence. 

Mr. ADAIR. My final question, Dr. Groseclose, do I understand you correctly then to say that in your opinion instead of constantly supplying foodstuffs to the underdeveloped countries, making an exception in the case of disasters as you point out, that instead of constantly supplying that we would be doing a vital service to the buildup of their own agricultural potential if we instead gave them instructions, advice, know-how, and tried to help them within the context of their own culture and capabilities?

Mr. GROSECLOSE. That is correct. 

When the foreign aid program started, you may recall, shortly after President Truman made his proposal in his inauguration speech, it was generally contemplated it would be a technical assistance program. They selected a great agricultural expert from my home State in the person of Dr. Bennett, president of the Oklahoma State University, to head the program. At that time it was intended to be mainly an agricultural demonstration project. I was in Iran at the time when suddenly, overnight, it was jacked up from a one-milliondollar-a-year program to a fifty-million-dollar-a-year program. Its nature and facade completely changed. Immediately it began to create troubles in Iran. The Prime Minister warned against the consequences of such a heavy increase in the budget. He warned Mr. 

Warne, as Mr. Warne says in his book, "Keep control of this money, because it is going to produce trouble, graft," and so forth, which it did.

This was analyzed and presented in the document by the Committee on Government Operations.

Mr. PALYI. May I make a remark with respect to the Marshall plan, prefacing it by saying that I am not opposed to every foreign aid. 

But I do think that most of it is misguided and overdone. As to the 

Marshall plan, the late Lord Brand, a sincere believer in laissez faire, and a leading merchant banker, told me in 1948 that the catastrophe of England is the Marshall plan. What the Marshall plan did in the opinion of leading financial authorities in London and Paris at the time was to permit the British and French Governments to indulge in reckless inflationary practices. Foreign aid can operate well too if it is properly applied by the recipients as was the case in Germany, but not in Britain, Italy, and France. Yet, even in Germany it has contributed to the subsequent inflationary course. 

The French Government received cotton from us, sold it to the cotton manufacturers and used the proceeds to fill a hole in the national budget while the American cotton exporters did a bang-up business under the Marshall plan. That is a typical example. Europe's true recovery started after the Marshall plan had ended. When there was no more Marshall plan, they had to start organizing themselves, balance their budgets, or bring them closer to balance, and start on monetary reforms.

Chairman MORGAN. Will you supply it for the record, Doctor? (The following has been supplied for the record:)

STATEMENT BY DR. MELCHIOR PALYI 

In the last two decades, scores of billions have been spent by the United States on economic aid to some 70-odd emerging nations-to foster their economic growth and to raise their living standards. To appreciate the social cost to the donor of this dollar outpour, bear in mind that the financing had to be provided either out of tax revenues, thus raising the total of (disincentive generating) levies; or by recourse on the domestic capital market, preempting it and boosting interest rates; or by deficit financing (inflation). In any case, the domestic respercussions of foreign aid contribute to our difficulties in foreign trade, if only by indirection. And that is not all.

So far as foreign aid generates exports, it increases the strains and stresses under which our inflation-ridden economy labors. Moreover, such subsidized exports bring little return, if any, in payments, but deter American business from efforts to increase their genuine commercial sales in competitive markets abroad, to the detriment of our payments balance. Lastly, and this is the most significant point, probably well over one-half of American economic aid has been provided in cash, thus tearing holes in our international accounts. Indeed, foreign aid is responsible for the deficit in our foreign payments that, in turn, is the hard core of the international liquidity problem. 

Indirectly, the dollar is burdened to some extent also by the foreign aid Britain provides, inasmuch as it contributes to the chronic difficulties of sterling which we are covering time and again. 

What has been accomplished by our generosity, if that is the right word for the export subsidies disguised as foreign aid? Almost nothing, is the answer, so far as the economic and social objectives are concerned. Disregarding a few showcases like Taiwan and the Ivory Coast, most recipient countries show no signs whatsoever of a takeoff in the direction of growing economic and financial strength. They indulge in reckless deficit financing and paper money inflation. 

Consequently, their aggregate savings stay low; actually, savings vanish in conspicuous consumption and capital flight empty land and other hedges are preferred to earning assets. Inflation sparks public waste and corruption which are reflected in unproductive speculating and squandering in the private sectors. 

Individuals have acquired huge fortunes, but the lot of the impoverished masses overwhelmingly landless agricultural proletariats-has not improved. 

Instead of progressing toward self-reliance, these artificially bolstered economies are sinking ever deeper into an apparently hopeless morass of budget deficits, galloping inflations, and growing foreign debts. Their progress is stymied by strangulating foreign exchange restrictions, their production hampered by senseless price controls and other bureaucratic interventions, pretending to combat the inflation. Instead of decreasing, the needy countries' requests for support keep mounting, due to their growing trade deficits and the rising burden of servicing their external debts.

This is a tragic situation, fraught with great dangers to the security of the Western World. It must be remedied, and it is in our power to remedy both: the recipient economies' deterioration as well as the deficit in our own balance of payments.

Experts within and from without the International Monetary Fund have warned time and again that the inflationary propensity of the emerging governments is at the root of their troubles. Yet, we have tolerated and even encouraged such practices. This is the Archimedean point at which a radically new policy has to start. The rule that the respective currencies must be stabilized should be the first axiom of foreign aid disbursements, excepting countries directly involved in war-Vietnam. There is no rhyme nor reason in the

fact that countries like Brazil and Argentina, endowed with tremendous natural wealth and capable populations, should dangle constantly on the verge of na tional bankruptcy, while Thailand, with an annual $120 per capita gross national income, is able to maintain a stable currency, to say nothing of Bolivia and Honduras.

As provided heretofore, foreign economic aid has turned out to be a curse in the disguise of benevolence. It is our responsibility to turn it into a real boom to the benefit of the recipients as well as of the donor. The way to do it is by attaching one vital string to the donation of loans or handouts-by making them contingent on the stabilization of the respective moneys, including the elimina tion of exchange barbed wires, multiple currencies, and similar restrictive practices.

The result would be twofold. The recipient economies could get started on the road toward sound economic growth. Thereby, they would attract investment-seeking private capital-that would gradually enable them to dispense with our aid, the prime cause of the menacing international liquidity shortage. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, gentlemen.

The committee stands adjourned until 10 a.m., Tuesday. (Whereupon, at 12:40 p.m., the committee adjourned, to reconvene at 10 a.m., Tuesday, May 3, 1966.)
